L482: As noted during the original review, the treatment effect appears to be driven by the subpopulation of patients with unrelated donors, and the treatment effect for this subset appears consistent whether they have AML or MDS. For patients with related donors, the HR is greater than one, and the results are inconsistent across disease subgroups. The biological basis for this observation is unclear. Higher HR estimates for the MRD group in the AML subpopulation are likely due to a few influential events observed after two years of follow-up (e.g., see Figure 8). Regarding the MRD group in the MDS subpopulation, since less than 10 events in each treatment group were observed in this subgroup, the results are likely unreliable due to few observed events. There is insufficient information to assess the potential lack of effectiveness. The results should be interpreted with caution.
L483: ## Dose/Dose Response
L484: ## Study MC-FludT.6/L
L485: Excerpted from FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021):
L486: Study MC-FludT.6/L was a multicenter single-arm dose-escalation trial to test the tolerability of treosulfan and fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT from an HLA-matched sibling, 1 antigen-mismatched sibling, or HLA-matched unrelated donor. Eligible patients were \ge 18 years old with a "chemosensitive" hematological malignancy indicated for HSCT but who were considered at high risk for toxicities with standard MAC regimens. Patients were treated in sequential cohorts (n=15 each) at treosulfan dose levels of 10 \text{ gm}/\text{m}^2, 12 \text{ gm}/\text{m}^2, or 14 \text{ gm}/\text{m}^2. The aim of the protocol was to determine a dose and schedule for later trials. The primary efficacy endpoint was hematopoietic recovery. Overall survival was an additional endpoint.

L491: A total of 55 patients were treated on this study, including 19 with AML and 6 with MDS. As discussed in Section 8.1.4 of the original NDA review, hematopoietic recovery and high-grade donor chimerism were achieved at all dose levels, and the 1-year OS was worst in the 14 \text{ g}/\text{m}^2/\text{day} \times 3 cohort. Despite these findings, the Applicant chose the 14 \text{ g}/\text{m}^2/\text{day} \times 3 regimen for the initial pivotal trial, MC-FludT.14/L Trial I.
L492: ## ***Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial I***
L493: Adapted from FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021) and updated based on current review of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II
L494: Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial I was the initial pivotal trial which randomized patients to treosulfan-based conditioning therapy with a dose of 14 \text{ g}/\text{m}^2/\text{day} \times 3 to busulfan-based reduced-intensity conditioning therapy in adults with AML or MDS. As discussed in FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021) Sections 8.1.2 and 8.3.5, this study was terminated early due to delayed hematopoietic recovery and increase early treatment-related mortality. FDA's analysis of the final data cut (1-year follow-up) showed no evidence of a treatment effect for the overall population or when assessed individually for patients with AML
L495: or with MDS. The second pivotal trial, MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, commenced using the lower treosulfan dose of 10 g/m2/day x 3. Table 22 shows the results of selected efficacy outcomes in ITT population of MC-FludT.14/L Trial I and Trial II.
L496: **Table 22. Analyses of OS vs Busulfan by Treosulfan Dose**

L499: The results suggest that the effectiveness for the treosulfan dose of 10 g/m2/day x 3 days in comparison to busulfan x 2 days is better than that for the treosulfan dose of 14 g/m2/day x 3 days for the ITT population and for patients with either AML or MDS.
L500: **Clinical TL Comment:** Clinical evidence from Study MC-FludT.6/L support the benefit of treosulfan for use as part of a preparative regimen. The 1-year OS outcomes for patients with hematologic malignancies who received the treosulfan-based preparative regimen at 10 g/m2/dose were consistent with those in the treosulfan arm of the pivotal trial. Although FDA does not usually use landmark OS in a single-arm trial to support efficacy due to concerns regarding the potential for bias, the findings in MC-FludT.6/L were reproducible in the two lower dose cohorts in the study (see Table 21). Additionally, the results of MC-FludT.6/L were reproducible across studies (see Table 21 and Table 22): 1-year OS in the 10 g/m2 cohort of MCFludT.6/L was consistent with that of the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II and similarly, 1-year OS in the 14 g/m2 cohort was consistent with the results in the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial I. Together, the consistency of these findings reduces concerns of bias and increase the confidence that the observed OS outcomes in MC-FludT.6/L reflect the clinical benefit of 10 g/m2/dose of treosulfan as part of a preparative regimen. Furthermore, the results of these studies also support a dose-dependent effect of treosulfan on OS, with worse OS seen with the treosulfan 14 g/m2/dose compared to lower doses of treosulfan in MC-FludT.6/L and compared to the busulfan regimen in MC-FludT.14/L Trial I.
L501: ## Additional Efficacy Considerations
L502: ## Extrapolation to Pediatric Patients
L503: (b) (4)
L504: As discussed in the original unireview,
L505: (b) (4) MC-FludT.16/NM was a phase 2 study for nonmalignant diseases that randomized patients to a 14 g/m2/dose equivalent of treosulfan or standard busulfan. Because this was not performed in a disease that is being considered for an indication for treosulfan, it was not evaluated for efficacy. The second study, MC-FludT.17/M, was a nonrandomized study for pediatric patients with malignant diseases; all patients received a 14 g/m2/dose equivalent of treosulfan. (b) (4)
L506: ## 8.1.4 Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
L507: The results of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II demonstrate the efficacy of 10 g/m2/dose treosulfan as a component of a preparative regimen prior to allogeneic HSCT. Patients not eligible for myeloablative conditioning were randomized to either treosulfan-based or busulfan-based reduced intensity conditioning. Patients who received treosulfan-based conditioning had longer OS compared to patients who received busulfan-based conditioning (HR 0.67 [95% CI: 0.51, 0.90]). These findings are supported by clinical evidence from Study MC-FludT.6/L where the 1-year OS outcome in patients who received the 10 g/m2/dose treosulfan-based preparative regimen was similar to that in the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II. Although FDA does not usually use landmark OS in a single-arm trial to support efficacy given the potential for bias, the OS findings in MC-FludT.6/L were reproducible in the two lower dose cohorts and were consistent with the OS results for patients treated with treosulfan across two other trials. The consistency of these findings reduces concerns for bias and supports the conclusion that the 10 g/m2/dose treosulfan is effective as part of a preparative regimen.
L508: Extrapolation of efficacy to the pediatric population is reasonable based on the biology of AML and MDS and the mechanism of action of treosulfan as an alkylating agent. (b) (4)
L509: the recommended dose for pediatric patients with AML or MDS is 10 g/m2/dose of treosulfan based on a full extrapolation of efficacy from MC-FludT.14/L Trial II and Population PK approach. (b) (4)
L510: Treosulfan-based preparative regimens prior to allogeneic HSCT in patients with AML or MDS result in a survival advantage compared to busulfan-based preparative regimens; however, it is not clear where treosulfan will be placed in clinical practice. The recommended indication for treosulfan (10 g/m2/dose) is: "for use in combination with fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT in adult and pediatric patients 1 year of age and older with AML (1.1) [or] MDS (1.2)."

L514: ## 8.2.1 Safety Review Approach
L515: No new safety data were included in this resubmission. See FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021) for detailed safety review approach.
L516: Studies MC-FludT.6/L, MC-FludT.7/AML, MC-FludT.8/MDS, MC-FludT.14/L I, MC-FludT.14/L II, MC-FludT.16/NM, and MC-FludT.17/M were included in the integrated summary of safety submitted by the Applicant. We performed the safety analysis on MC-FludT.6L, MC-FludT.7/AML, MC-FludT.8/MDS, MC-FludT.14/L I, and MC-FludT.14/L II for the adult population, and on MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M for the pediatric safety analysis.
L517: Summaries of data and safety analyses by the clinical reviewer were performed using JMP version 15.0 (SAS Institute, Inc., Cary, NC) and MedDRA Adverse Events Diagnostic (MAED, FDA, Silver Spring, MD), versions 2.5 (through 3/21/2021) and 3.0 (after 3/21/20201). Summaries of laboratory data were performed using JMP version 15.0 and Palantir (Palantir Technologies, Inc., Denver, CO, ©2017-2021). Postmarket analysis was conducted using Empirica Signal (Oracle, Santa Clara, CA, ©2014).

L526: ## Categorization of Adverse Events
L527: Adverse events were reported down to the verbatim term. The adverse events were coded using MedDRA version 20.0 for all studies and for the pooled safety population. Where indicated in the tables or text, some adverse events are presented as grouped terms. Regimen-related toxicity (RRT) is defined as directly caused by the conditioning regimen. Bearman's RRT criteria are used unless otherwise specified. Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) included events that started from the start of study drug administration through HSCT day +30, unless otherwise indicated.

L532: ## Routine Clinical Tests
L533: ## 8.1.6 Safety Results
L534: ## Deaths
L535: In the full safety database, there were 14 deaths within 30 days of the last dose of treosulfan and 57 deaths (43 additional deaths) within 100 days of the last dose of treosulfan.
L536: In MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, there were 4 deaths (1.5%) from fatal adverse reactions due to treosulfan by Day +30; there were two additional deaths that were adjudicated as transplant-related mortality. In the busulfan arm, there were two deaths prior to Day +30, both of which were adjudicated as adverse reactions due to busulfan. In the treosulfan arm, there were 17 deaths (6%) by transplant day +100, compared to 19 deaths (7%) by transplant day + 100 in the busulfan arm. However, we note that for six patients, we did not have sufficient information to adjudicate the cause of death.

L543: ## Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
L544: When evaluating TEAEs by Preferred Term (PT), the most common TEAEs were those expected after allo-HSCT. The most common adverse reactions (\ge 10\%) on the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II included musculoskeletal pain, stomatitis, pyrexia, nausea, edema, infection, vomiting, rash, diarrhea, headache, febrile neutropenia, abdominal pain, hypertension, hemorrhage, fatigue, constipation, tachycardia, and hepatotoxicity. The most common grade 3-5 ARs (\ge 10\%) on the treosulfan arm were febrile neutropenia and infection. Rare but potentially serious events that occurred on the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II included ventricular arrhythmias (including tachycardia and extrasystoles; N = 5, 1.852%), erythema multiforme (N = 8, 2.963%), palmar-plantar erythrodysesthesia syndrome (N = 5, 1.852%), and cardiac failure (N = 8, 2.963%).

L549: ## Laboratory Findings
L550: As treosulfan is used as part of a conditioning regimen for allogeneic HSCT, cytopenias occur in nearly 100% of patients. Other laboratory abnormalities are listed below. There were no significant differences in the maximum grades of laboratory abnormalities between the treosulfan and busulfan arms of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II.

L557: ## **Treosulfan Dose (14 g/m2)**
L558: Trial MC-FludT.14/L Trial I used a treosulfan dose of 14 g/m2 for patients randomized to receive treosulfan. This study was stopped early due to increased transplant-related morbidity and mortality observed in the treosulfan arm. Treatment-emergent adverse events through HSCT day +30 are shown in Table 26. There were increased TEAEs of hepatotoxicity, stomatitis, musculoskeletal pain, infection, and febrile neutropenia in the treosulfan cohort, including grades 3-5 TEAEs.

L566: ## 8.2.6 Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing Safety/Tolerability

L568: ## 8.2.7 Safety Analyses by Subgroups
L569: See FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021) for additional subgroup analyses.
L570: ## Drug-Donor Interactions
L571: Treosulfan was used as part of the conditioning regimen for patients undergoing both matched related donor and matched unrelated donor allogeneic HSCT. TEAEs were assessed in the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II in the related and unrelated donor populations. There
L572: were more TEAEs observed in patients with matched unrelated donors, though this may be due to the difference in patient numbers between these two groups. When evaluating Grade 3-5 TEAEs, infection was more common in patients with a matched related donor (N = 11, 17%) than in those receiving a matched unrelated donor (N = 24, 12%).
L573: A comparison between the treosulfan and busulfan groups was performed. All-grade musculoskeletal pain, edema, infection, and hemorrhage were more common in patients with matched related donors receiving treosulfan-based conditioning. Of note, grade 3-5 infection was higher in the treosulfan group than the busulfan group (17.46% vs 4.412%, p-value = 0.022).
L574: ## 8.2.8 Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
L575: ## Dose Dependency for Adverse Events
L576: As MC-FludT.14/L Trial I, in which patients randomized to treosulfan received a dose of 14 g/m2, was stopped early because of increased transplant-related morbidity and mortality, a comparison was performed between all adult patients who received 10 g/m2 and those who received 14 g/m2 of treosulfan. We observed higher rates of hepatotoxicity, GI toxicity, infection (including febrile neutropenia), and musculoskeletal AEs in patients receiving the 14 g/m2 dose of treosulfan, as seen in Table 27.

L581: *Includes Grouped Terms
L582: **Clinical TL Comment:** As noted in the original review, across the safety population, patients who received 14 g/m2/dose of treosulfan experienced substantially more toxicity, including infectious complications, GI toxicity, and hepatotoxicity, compared to those who received the 10 g/m2/dose of treosulfan. In MC-FludT.14/L Trial I, the higher rates of early fatal adverse reactions confirm an increased risk of morbidity and mortality with the 14 g/m2 dose of treosulfan. These findings warrant inclusion of a warning and precaution in the USPI.
L583: ## 8.2.9 Additional Safety Explorations
L584: ## Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L585: An analysis of the SOC “Neoplasms benign, malignant, and unspecified (including cysts and polyps)” in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II revealed one patient in the treosulfan arm and one patient in the busulfan arm with these events.
L586: ## Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L587: Because of the proposed mechanism of action of treosulfan, pregnancy and reproduction are discouraged. There have been no studies or literature reports of subjects who became pregnant while receiving treosulfan, or of children who were born of parents who were receiving treosulfan.
L588: ## Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L589: Two pediatric studies were submitted as part of this application. All pediatric patients received doses of treosulfan that were equivalent to 14 g/m2 dosing. Safety analysis was performed comparing the pediatric patients to the recommended dose of treosulfan (10 g/m2). The
L590: majority of pediatric patients received thiotepe in addition to fludarabine and treosulfan for their conditioning. Analyses comparing pediatric and adult studies (Cohorts 4 and 1) excluded patients who received thiotepe.
L591: An analysis between the treosulfan arm and the busulfan arm in the Phase 2 randomized study MC-FludT.16/NM was conducted. Nearly all of the patients in this analysis received a preparative regimen of either treosulfan or busulfan, fludarabine, and thiotepe. The majority of TEAEs were similar between the two arms. Notable exceptions included higher rates of abdominal pain, rash, pruritis, hemorrhage, and infusion-related reactions in the treosulfan arm of this study.
L592: Pediatric patients from both MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M who received a preparative regimen of treosulfan + fludarabine (Cohort 4) were then compared to adult patients who received the recommended treosulfan-based conditioning regimen (Cohort 3, 10 g/m2). The pediatric patient numbers are small, as the majority of patients received a preparative regimen that included thiotepe and are excluded from this analysis. The analysis revealed higher rates of hepatotoxicity and gastrointestinal toxicities in the pediatric patients who received a dose equivalent to 14 g/m2 compared with adult patients who received the 10 g/m2 dose.
L593: ## Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
L594: See FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021).
L595: ## 8.2.10 Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L596: ## Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience

L598: ## Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting

L600: ## 8.2.11 Integrated Assessment of Safety
L601: The primary data in support of safety for the proposed indication of treosulfan came from study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, in which 270 patients with either AML or MDS were exposed to treosulfan as part of a conditioning regimen prior to allogeneic HSCT. All patients received two doses of treosulfan on days -4 and -3 prior to HSCT, at a dose of 10 g/m2/dose.
L602: The study population was monitored for death, adverse events, serious adverse events, and laboratory abnormalities. By Day +30 following HSCT, there were 4 deaths (1.5%) on the treosulfan arm, all due to fatal pulmonary adverse reactions, as compared to 2 deaths (0.7%) on the busulfan (control) arm.
L603: Adverse events with treosulfan were mainly similar to those observed with busulfan-based conditioning. Common TEAEs (\ge 20\%) included musculoskeletal pain, stomatitis, pyrexia, nausea, edema, infection, and vomiting. Of those, musculoskeletal pain (12% higher), edema (11% higher), and infection (5% higher) occurred at a rate at least 5% higher in the treosulfan arm than the busulfan arm. The most common grade 3-5 AEs (\ge 5\%) in the treosulfan arm were febrile neutropenia, infection, hypertension, stomatitis, and musculoskeletal pain. Of those, infection occurred at a rate of &gt;5\% compared to the busulfan arm (6% higher).
L604: **Table 28. Summary of Major Safety Events from MC-FludT.14/L Trial II through Day +30**
L605: Treosulfan (N = 270)	Busulfan (N = 283)
Any TEAE	250 (92.6%)	272 (96.1%)
Grade 3-5 TEAE	148 (54.8%)	151 (53.4%)
SAE	23 (8.5%)	20 (7.1%)
Fatal Adverse Reactions	4 (1.5%)	2 (0.7%)

L607: Safety data supporting the dose of treosulfan (10 \text{ g}/\text{m}^2/\text{dose}) were from studies MC-FludT.6/L, MC-FludT.7/AML, MC-FludT.8/MDS, and MC-FludT.14/L Trials I and II. In this comparison, 290 patients were dosed at 10 \text{ g}/\text{m}^2/\text{dose}, and 305 patients were dosed at 14 \text{ g}/\text{m}^2/\text{dose}.
L608: Higher rates of hepatotoxicity, GI toxicity, infection (including febrile neutropenia), and musculoskeletal AEs were reported in patients receiving the 14 \text{ g}/\text{m}^2 dose of treosulfan compared to the 10 \text{ g}/\text{m}^2/\text{dose}.
L609: In the pediatric studies, very few patients under the age of 1 year received a regimen without thiopeta. This limits the conclusions regarding the safety of the proposed treosulfan treatment regimen to patients 1 year and older. Higher rates of hepatotoxicity and GI toxicity were reported in pediatric patients who received 14 \text{ g}/\text{m}^2/\text{dose}-equivalent of treosulfan compared to adults who received the 10 \text{ g}/\text{m}^2 dose. (b) (4)
L610: ## 8.3 Statistical Issues
L611: This NDA has received two separate Complete Responses, dated 30 July 2021 and 15 September 2022. These Complete Responses were issued mainly for inadequate data collection and study design. Key issues:
L612: - The Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was stopped early for efficacy at 35% information fraction of EFS. Early stopping for efficacy might result in an overestimation of a treatment effect.
L613: - No alpha-controlled hypothesis testing was prespecified for the overall survival. Not controlling for multiplicity might result in erroneous conclusions.
- The EFS definition per protocol is different from the definition typically used for regulatory consideration. Clinical and regulatory relevance of the EFS endpoint, as defined in the protocol, is questionable.
- Adequate data were not submitted to confirm the EFS results in the previous submissions. Additional data were submitted, but the reliability of data is likely limited beyond Day 100.
- The full study population consists of two heterogeneous subpopulations that usually have separate indications: AML and MDS. As there were no prespecified hypotheses testing and alpha allocation for these subpopulations, residual uncertainty remains regarding the benefit observed in these subpopulations.
L614: Refer to the FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021) for further details. The relevant statistical issues are detailed below.
L615: ## Data Collection
L616: The following issues regarding data collection were noted in the FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021):
L617: - "The primary endpoint of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II was event-free survival (EFS). Because all complete blood cell (CBC) and marrow results were not submitted, we were unable to independently confirm the results of the analysis of the primary endpoint of EFS for Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II.
- Additionally, because of the missing CBC and marrow results, we were not able complete our assessment of whether the results of the study would support the indications for treatment of patients with AML and for treatment of patients with MDS."
L618: The extent of the submitted data is limited. Per Complete Response to the FDA Complete Response Letter 30 July 2021, page 10: "The applicant would like to respectfully submit that the collection of some of the marrow data was considered not mandatory under the study protocol MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, particularly beyond Day +100 and in case of lack of an event. However, in some instances, bone marrow was collected beyond Day +100 and details of these assessments are provided." Given the lack of reliable data collection beyond Day 100 (and "in case of lack of an event"), outcomes relying on such data beyond Day 100 might be challenging to interpret.
L619: ## Event-Free Survival Definition
L620: Per statistical analysis plan, the primary efficacy endpoint is "event-free survival (EFS) within 2 years after transplantation measured from time of end of HSCT (= day 0) to time of event. The associated time span is defined as the interval from day 0 to relapse, graft failure or death (whatever occurs first). The date of the event is either the documented date of relapse/progression or the date of death or the date of primary (date of examination of neutrophilic granulocytes) or secondary graft failure (date of examination of neutrophilic
L621: granulocytes)." This is not a standard definition used for regulatory considerations in AML or MDS populations. Per FDA's *Acute Myeloid Leukemia: Developing Drugs and Biological Products for Treatment Guidance for Industry*, EFS is defined as the "time from randomization to the date of: induction treatment failure, relapse for those who have induction treatment success (e.g., complete remission), or death from any cause, whichever comes first". Interpretation of the results based on the protocol-defined EFS outcome may be challenging.
L622: ## Overall Survival Definition
L623: Per statistical analysis plan, OS is defined as "the time length between day 0 and the date of death due to any cause within the 24-month study period (i.e. excluding post-surveillance evaluation)." Day zero is the time of allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation. This is not a standard OS definition used for regulatory purposes, which typically uses 1) randomization as the start of the period of interest 2) has no restriction on follow-up (i.e., no limit set to 24 months). Since the OS is both safety and efficacy endpoint, the regulatory interest is in the full unrestricted survival period starting from randomization, referred to OS (post-randomization) herein.
L624: ## No Alpha Control and No Prespecified Boundary for Overall Survival Testing
L625: Overall survival was one of the secondary outcomes. The OS results show a relatively high magnitude of a treatment effect with the hazard ratio equal to 0.67 (95% CI: 0.51, 0.90; 193 events) in the AML and MDS population. No alpha was allocated for testing overall survival and no boundary for the interim analysis was prespecified. However, these results were considered to reflect a meaningful improvement on the gold-standard endpoint in oncology.
L626: ## Subgroups
L627: The full study population consists of two distinct populations: patients with AML and patients with MDS. While the OS (post-randomization) results appear to be supportive of the benefit in AML and MDS subgroups, there was no prespecified alpha-controlled hypothesis testing for these subgroups and the upper bounds of the 95% confidence intervals exceed one, suggesting that some residual uncertainty about the treatment effect in each of the subgroups remains.
L628: Additionally, further subgroup of interest included donor type: MUD vs MRD. As noted in the FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021), "Due to differences in minor and other unmeasured major antigens, HLA-matched unrelated donors have a higher degree of histoincompatibility with the recipient than HLA-identical sibling donors, so the minimum intensity of the preparative regimen needed for successful HSCT may differ by donor type." Higher HR estimates for the MRD group in the AML subpopulation are likely due to a few influential events observed after two years of follow-up. Regarding the MDS subgroup, since less than 10 events in each treatment group were observed in this subgroup, the results are likely unreliable due to few observed events. Harm to patients with MRD cannot be ruled out. The subgroup results should be interpreted with caution.
L629: ## Differences in Treatment Regimen
L630: As noted in the FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021),
L631: there were differences in treatment regimens between study groups:
L632: - "Trial 14 - II compared fludarabine in combination with 3 days of treatment with treosulfan to a combination with 2 days of busulfan. Any conclusions regarding superiority would need to take into account the difference between arms in the number of days of treatment with treosulfan vs the number of days of treatment with busulfan.
- Additionally, in Trial 14 - II, patients with unrelated donors also received ATG in the preparative regimen. ATG dosing varied by geographic region. Any conclusions related to the subgroup analysis by donor would need to take into consideration the differences in treatment regimens by donor type"
L633: The differences in the treatment regimen might have an impact on the treatment effect interpretation.
L634: ## Stratification Factors
L635: Per statistical analysis plan for the overall survival, "Cox proportional hazards regression model stratified by centre with donor type (MUD vs. MRD), risk group (I vs. II) and treatment as factors will be fitted." The site factor has 22 levels, the donor type factor has two levels, and the risk group factor has two levels. Including the site in a Cox proportional hazards model would result in small or zero number of events in some strata, making the interpretation of the results challenging (e.g., see Silcocks (2012)). Furthermore, this practice is not typically recommended for trials with few patients per center. Per ICH E9 (page 17), "In some trials, for example, some large mortality trials with very few subjects per center, there may be no reason to expect the centers to have any influence on the primary or secondary variables because they are unlikely to represent influences of clinical importance. In other trials, it may be recognized from the start that the limited numbers of subjects per center will make it impracticable to include the center effects in the statistical model. In these cases, it is not considered appropriate to include a term for center in the model, and it is not necessary to stratify the randomization by center in this situation". For this reason, the results reported herein and in the USPI use a Cox proportional hazards model stratified by donor type and risk group.
L636: ## Proportional Hazards Assumptions
L637: The treatment effects in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II were quantified primarily with the hazard ratios. The hazard ratios are estimated with a Cox proportional hazards model, which relies on proportional hazards assumptions. A plateauing effect or crossing curves can be seen on most Kaplan-Meier plots for the EFS and the OS outcomes. A plateauing effect or crossing curves might indicate that the proportional hazards assumption does not hold. Additional analyses were conducted to quantify the treatment effects with various alternative statistics that do not rely on the proportional hazards assumptions. The results of these analyses do not change the conclusions based on the hazard ratios, suggesting favorable outcomes for the treosulfan compared to the busulfan.
L638: ## 8.4 Conclusions and Recommendations
L639: The review team was able to verify or clarify the efficacy and safety endpoints as provided by the Applicant. The efficacy of 10 g/m2/dose treosulfan as a component of a conditioning regimen prior to allogeneic HSCT was established based on improvement in overall survival with HR 0.67 (95% CI: 0.51, 0.90) demonstrated in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II and on supportive evidence provided by Study MC-FludT.6/L.
L640: The safety of treosulfan 10 g/m2/dose was generally similar to that of busulfan in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II with a nominally higher incidence of musculoskeletal pain and infection in the treosulfan arm. One major safety concern is the high morbidity and mortality with the 14 g/m2/dose treosulfan regimen established in MC-FludT.14/L Trial I and supported by Study MC-FludT.6/L. These findings warrant inclusion of a Warning and Precaution in the USPI since this dose is published in the literature and approved for the pediatric population in some countries.
L641: The efficacy of treosulfan at 10 g/m2/dose was extrapolated to pediatric patients undergoing HSCT for an AML or MDS based on the efficacy demonstrated in the adult pivotal study and safety was established based on data from pediatric studies in patients 1 year and older. (b) (4)

L697: ## 13 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
L698: Recommended Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments:
L699: ## PMR #1
L700: Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to evaluate a serious potential risk of increased drug toxicity and determine an appropriate, safe dose of treosulfan in patients with moderate renal impairment (estimated glomerular filtration rate or creatinine clearance 30-59 mL/min). This trial should be designed and conducted in accordance with the FDA Guidance for Industry titled "Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Renal Function: Study Design, Data Analysis, and Impact on Dosing and Labeling."
L701: ## PMR #2
L702: Conduct a clinical trial to characterize the serious potential risk of QTc prolongation (i.e., exclude large mean increases of &gt;20 msec in the QTc interval) with treosulfan and its active metabolite. Design and conduct the study in accordance with the ICH E14 Guidances for Industry titled, E14 Clinical Evaluation of QT/QTc and E14 Clinical Evaluation of QT/QTc Interval Prolongation and Proarrhythmic Potential for Non-Antiarrhythmic Drugs Questions and Answers (R3) Guidance for Industry.
L703: ## PMR #3
L704: Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to characterize the serious potential risk of increased drug toxicity and further characterize the safety of treosulfan by evaluating the pharmacokinetics of the active monoepoxide intermediate [(2S,3S)-1,2-epoxybutane-3,4-diol-4-methanesulfonate] in adult patients after administration of treosulfan.
L705: ## PMC #1
L706: Conduct a clinical trial to assess the pharmacokinetics of treosulfan among U.S. racial and ethnic groups. This study should characterize the exposure (including pharmacokinetics data) of treosulfan.
L707: ## PMC #2
L708: Conduct a registry-based observational study to assess outcomes by race and ethnicity for patients undergoing allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation using treosulfan-fludarabine as the preparative regimen. Include subgroup analyses by race and ethnicity for overall survival, nonrelapse mortality and relapse-free survival.

L755: **Conduct an *in vitro* study to evaluate treosulfan and epoxide metabolites as substrates of major cytochrome P450 enzymes consistent with FDA guidance.**
L756: The *in vitro* assessment of treosulfan metabolism showed involvement of CYP2D6 (t1/2 with rCYP2D6 = 108 min) and was noted in the previous review.
L757: Based on an updated communication with the Applicant (see below), we have determined that the above PMR is no longer needed.
L758: ## Clinical Pharmacology Information Request
L759: Reference is made to your Complete Response to the CRL and Acknowledgement of Incomplete Response (dated 4-26-24), where you stated that “CYP450 reaction phenotyping with the monoepoxide and the diepoxide metabolites began later and has not yet been completed” and that the reports will be submitted when available (page 33 of 37).
L760: Reference is also made to your response to our PMR-PMC template milestone dates, where you indicated that the final report submission for these metabolites studies occurred in Apr 2024. We note the submission of Reports “CYP1269 R14A” and CYP1269 R14B” to the NDA.
L761: Clarify the discrepancy and whether these aforementioned reports are the formal final study reports.
L762: ## medac GmbH Response
L763: The applicant confirms that the reports CYP1269 R14A and CYP1269 R14B, as filed under CTD Section 5.3.2.2, are the formal final study reports of the CYP450 reaction phenotyping with the treosulfan monoepoxide and the diepoxide metabolites. We apologize for the confusion created.
L764: Based on a review of the submitted reports of the in vitro studies, the monoepoxide (**Table 1**) and diepoxide metabolites (**Table 2**) are not meaningfully metabolized by CYP enzymes. Of note, the t_{1/2} values for positive control compounds for the respective rCYPs were very short. No labeling update is needed based on these results.

L779: The clinical pharmacology of treosulfan was extensively reviewed in the original submission (Unireview link, DARRTS 7-30-21). Briefly, the Applicant is seeking approval of treosulfan as part of conditioning treatment prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT) in adult patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS). The proposed dosing regimen is treosulfan 10 g/m2/day for 3 consecutive days (Days -4, -3, and -2) in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2/day for 5 consecutive days (Days -6 through -2) prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT; Day 0).
L780: Pivotal evidence to support efficacy in the proposed population is based on the results of Study MC-FLudT.14/L Trial II. Key review issues include the adequacy of the proposed treosulfan dosing regimen in adult patients, pediatric extrapolation to identify a treosulfan dosing regimen appropriate for pediatric patients ≥1 year of age, and evaluation of the Applicant's proposals for patients with organ impairment. The Applicant provided pharmacokinetic studies in adults and pediatric patients, a population PK model of treosulfan, and a dose-response evaluation in adults to support the dose selection in the general population and specific populations. The
L781: Applicant did not provide adequate data to resolve other review questions including characterization of treosulfan metabolism and effects of treosulfan on QTc interval.
L782: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology did not recommend approval then because of issues regarding the veracity of the primary study endpoint.
L783: A summary of key review issues and recommendations from the original clinical pharmacology review of NDA 214759 is provided below:
L784: REVIEW ISSUE	RECOMMENDATIONS / COMMENTS
General dosing instructions	The recommended dosage regimen of treosulfan is 10 g/m2/day for 3 consecutive days (Days -4, -3, and -2) in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2/day for 5 consecutive days (Days -6 through -2) prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (Day 0).
Dosing in patient subgroups (intrinsic and extrinsic factors)	
Pediatric Patients \ge1 year of age: The recommended dosage regimen of treosulfan is the same for all patients \ge1 year of age. Efficacy in pediatric patients \ge1 year of age was extrapolated from adults and dose selection was supported by PK and safety data from Studies MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M. Treosulfan exposure after administration of 10 g/m2 in pediatric patients with BSA <0.7 m2 and with BSA 0.7 to <1.1 m2 are 11% and 5% higher, respectively, compared to adults. Exposure-response analyses for safety did not indicate that an 11% increase in exposures would result in significant increase in the probability of common toxicities.
Renal Impairment: No dosage modification is recommended for patients with mild renal impairment. An additional study will be necessary to identify a safe dose of treosulfan for patients with moderate hepatic impairment. A study in patients with severe renal impairment is not feasible and will not be required.
Hepatic Impairment: No dosage modification is recommended for patients with mild hepatic impairment. Studies in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment are not needed, as these patients are typically excluded from transplantation.
L785: The previous clinical pharmacology review identified 5 potential post-marketing requirements / commitments (PMR / PMCs), which are still applicable and should be issued for this NDA (see Table below).
L786: Key Issue(s) to be Addressed	Rationale	Key Considerations for Design Features
Determination of an appropriate safe treosulfan dose for use in patients with moderate renal	There is evidence of renal elimination of treosulfan. Renal impairment may increase treosulfan systemic exposures and require dose adjustment.	(PMR 1) Conduct a clinical trial to evaluate the pharmacokinetics and safety of treosulfan in patients with normal renal function and patients with moderate renal impairment.
L787: Key Issue(s) to be Addressed	Rationale	Key Considerations for Design Features
Evaluation of treosulfan and epoxide metabolites as substrates of metabolizing enzymes.	The Applicant did not evaluate treosulfan or its epoxide metabolites as substrates of major metabolizing enzymes. This evaluation is necessary to describe risk of drug-drug interactions and effects of hepatic impairment on treosulfan safety and PK.	(PMR 2) Conduct an in vitro study to evaluate treosulfan and epoxide metabolites as substrates of major cytochrome P450 enzymes consistent with FDA guidance.
Evaluation of the effects of treosulfan on the QT interval.	The effects of treosulfan on the QT interval have not been evaluated. A QT study should be performed to evaluate the cardiac safety of treosulfan.	(PMR 3) Conduct a clinical trial to evaluate the effects of treosulfan on the QT interval in patients receiving the recommended dosage of treosulfan.
Evaluation of treosulfan active metabolite PK in adult patients.	The PK of treosulfan parent drug was evaluated in 24 adult patients. The PK of active epoxide metabolites was not evaluated in adult patients.	(PMR 4) Evaluate the PK of treosulfan active epoxide metabolites in adult patients.
Determination of the effects of race and ethnicity on the PK of treosulfan.	Race and ethnicity data were not collected in the pivotal study or adult PK sub-study of treosulfan. The effects of race and ethnicity on the PK of treosulfan is therefore unknown.	(PMC 1) Conduct a clinical trial to evaluate the effects of race and ethnicity on the pharmacokinetics of treosulfan.
L788: The only new data submitted in this re-submission were *in vitro* metabolism study results which determined that treosulfan is metabolized by CYP2D6 and its monoepoxide intermediate is metabolized by CYP2C8. The labeling has been updated to reflect this information. *In vitro* metabolism studies for other metabolites are currently ongoing (relates to PMR 2, above).
L789: This NDA is approvable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. The PMRs and PMC listed above should be issued to the applicant.

L800: ## 1 Executive Summary
L801: ## 1.1 Introduction
L802: GRAFAPEX (treosulfan) is an alkylating drug. The Applicant, Medac GmbH, submitted a Class 2 Resubmission of the original NDA 214759. The Applicant is seeking marketing approval for GRAFAPEX (treosulfan) for the use in combination with fludarabine as a preparative regimen for alloHSCT in adult and pediatric patients 1 year of age and older with AML. The current submission is a resubmission of the original NDA that was submitted under section 505(b)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act on August 11, 2020.
L803: ## 1.2 Brief Discussion of Nonclinical Findings
L804: The relevant nonclinical pharmacology, pharmacokinetics, and toxicology studies for treosulfan to support NDA 214759 were previously reviewed in the Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation (Unireview; DARRTS date: July 30, 2021) for the initial submission of NDA 214759. During the review of the NDA, it was determined that the nonclinical data for treosulfan submitted to the NDA in studies or literature for which the Applicant has the right to reference did not provide convincing evidence of DNA alkylation and breaks as the mechanism of action (MOA) of treosulfan or to address genotoxicity potential of the drug. The FDA issued a Complete Response Letter due to Clinical deficiencies. In the letter, FDA recommended the Applicant conduct and submit the results of nonclinical studies to address the comment from Pharmacology/ Toxicology discipline.
L805: To address this issue, the Applicant conducted genetic toxicology studies, and submitted the results to demonstrate the genotoxicity of treosulfan. Genetic toxicology studies included an in vitro bacterial reverse mutation (Ames) assay and an in vitro chromosomal aberration assay.
L806: In the in vitro bacterial reverse mutation assay, treosulfan tested at up to 3160 µg per plate, with or without metabolic activation, caused a concentration-dependent mutagenic effect in the *Salmonella typhimurium* strains. In the in vitro chromosomal aberration assay using human lymphocyte cultures, treosulfan tested at up to 50 µg/mL showed clastogenic properties with respect to chromosomal or chromatid damage with or without metabolic activation. Overall, the study results showed that treosulfan is a genotoxic compound.
L807: ## 1.3 Recommendations
L808: ## 1.3.1 Approbability
L809: There are no Pharmacology/Toxicology approbability issues with the proposed GRAFAPEX (treosulfan) for Injection drug product for the proposed indication.
L810: ## 1.3.2 Additional Non Clinical Recommendations
L811: None.
L812: ## 1.3.3 Labeling
L813: Appropriate labeling changes have been made to the United States Prescribing Information (USPI) to be consistent with the Pregnancy and Lactation Labeling Rule (PLLR) format and current labeling practices. A sentence regarding the genetic toxicology studies has been added under Section 13.1 Carcinogenesis, Mutagenesis, Impairment of Fertility.
L814: ## 2.2 Previous Reviews Referenced
L815: Refer to the Pharmacology/Toxicology review in the original NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation for NDA 214759 submitted to DARRTS on July 30, 2021.
L816: ## 3 Genetic Toxicology
L817: ## 3.1 *In Vitro* Reverse Mutation Assay in Bacterial Cells (Ames)
L818: Mutagenicity study of treosulfan in the *Salmonella typhimurium* and *Escherichia coli* reverse mutation assay (in vitro)
L819: Study no.:	38665
Study report location:	4.2.3.3.1
Date of study initiation:	September 17, 2021
GLP compliance:	Yes
QA statement:	Yes
Drug, lot #, and % purity:	Treosulfan Lyophilisate 1000 mg,
F190859A, 98.9%
L820: ## Key Study Findings
L821: - Treosulfan was positive for mutagenicity in the reverse mutation assay.
- Treosulfan induced a concentration-dependent mutagenic effect and at least 2-fold increase in the number of revertant colonies for test strains TA100 and TA1535 with or without metabolic activation. No mutagenic effect was observed for the test strains TA98, TA1537 and WP2 *uvrA*.
- Cytotoxicity was observed in all tester strains at 3160 µg/plate, resulting in a reduction in the number of spontaneous revertant colonies. No test item precipitation was observed.
L822: ## Methods
L823: Strains:	4 strains of Salmonella typhimurium (TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537) and 1 strain of Escherichia coli (WP2 uvrA)
Concentrations in definitive study:	10, 31.6, 100, 316, 1000 and 3160 µg/plate
Basis of concentration selection:	cytotoxicity at ≥3160 µg/plate in tester strain TA100 with and without S9 mix
Negative control:	sterile deionized water
Positive control:	2-aminoanthracene, 2-nitrofluorene, sodium azide, 4-nitroquinoline-N-oxide, benzo[a]pyrene
Formulation/Vehicle:	sterile deionized water
Incubation & sampling time:	50 hours at 37°C
L824: ## Study Validity
L825: The mean number of revertant colonies observed for the negative/solvent control was comparable to historical control data obtained from the previous studies. All positive controls used induced the expected increase in the mean revertant colonies relative to the negative/solvent control, thereby confirming the responsiveness of the strains and the sensitivity of the test system.

L831: ## 3.2 *In Vitro* Assays in Mammalian Cells
L832: In vitro assessment of the clastogenic activity of treosulfan in cultured human peripheral lymphocytes
L833: Study no.:	38666
Study report location:	4.2.3.3.1
Date of study initiation:	September 27, 2021
GLP compliance:	Yes
QA statement:	Yes
Drug, lot #, and % purity:	Treosulfan Lyophilisate 1000 mg, F190859A, 98.9%
L834: ## Key Study Findings
L835: - Treosulfan was positive for clastogenicity in the in vitro chromosome aberrations assay in human peripheral lymphocytes.
- Treosulfan induced 1.0 - 9.0% of chromosomal aberrations (excluding gaps) of the cells at concentrations of 3.125 - 50 µg/mL medium (4-hour exposure) and 1.3 - 8.4 % of chromosomal aberrations at concentrations of 3.125 to 25 µg/mL medium (24-hour exposure) in the absence of metabolic activation.
- Treosulfan induced chromosomal aberrations (excluding gaps) of the cells at concentrations of 3.125 - 50 µg/mL medium (4-hour exposure) in the presence of metabolic activation.
L836: ## Methods
L837: Cell line:	human peripheral lymphocytes
Concentrations in definitive study:	3.125, 6.25, 12.5, 25, and 50 µg/mL +S9 activation (4 hours) and -S9 activation (4 or 24 hours)
Basis of concentration selection:	cytotoxicity at ≥31.6 µg/mL with or without S9 activation
Negative control:	sterile deionized water
Positive control:	cyclophosphamide monohydrate (+S9), mitomycin C (-S9)
Formulation/Vehicle:	sterile deionized water
Incubation & sampling time:	4 and 24 hours in the absence of activation system and 4 hours in the presence of activation system.
L838: ## Study Validity
L839: The positive controls induced a significant increase in the percentage of cells with structural aberrations. The percentage of cells with structural aberrations in the negative and positive controls was comparable to the historical control data.

L862: ## CLINICAL TEAM LEADER REVIEW
## DIVISION OF HEMATOLOGIC MALIGNANCIES 1
L863: Application Number	NDA 214759.65
Submission Type	Resubmission
Submission Date	8/10/2022
Applicant	medac
Proposed Trade Name	(b) (4)
Nonproprietary Name	Treosulfan
Dosage Form and Strength	Injection, lyophilized (1000 mg, 5000 mg)
Route of Administration	Intravenous
Proposed Dosing Regimen	10 g/m2 body surface area (BSA) per day as a two-hour intravenous infusion, given on three consecutive days (day -4, -3, -2) before hematopoietic stem cell infusion (day 0)
Proposed Indication	As part of conditioning treatment prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT) in adult patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS).
Team Leader	Donna Przepiorka, MD, PhD
L864: **Regulatory Background:** NDA 214759 was submitted 8/11/2020, and a CRL was issued on 7/30/2021. A resubmission with response to the CRL was received 4/21/2022, and an Acknowledge Incomplete Response Letter was issued on 5/16/2022 due to deficiencies in the datasets. SDN 65 includes a response to the CRL and responses to the issues in the 5/16/2022 letter.
L865: **Background:** During review of the datasets in preparation for endpoint adjudication, a number of implausible laboratory results were identified. An Information Request was sent to the Applicant on 9/1/2022. The Applicant clarified several typographical errors that needed to be corrected. Two issues remained unresolved:
L866: - In lb.xpt, there were 86 instances where the marrow blast percentage (BLAST) was &lt; 1 % and in some cases displayed as low as 0.01 %, numbers too low to be plausible for a blast count by morphology. In their 9/6/2022 response, the Applicant indicated that "Documented values &lt;1%, however, are obviously not related to classical microscopic morphological assessments of bone marrow smears or histological BM specimens. *It is reasonable to assume* that the study sites performed, e.g., multiparameter flow cytometric (MFC) detection of minimal residual disease (MRD) of bone marrow aspirates to detect leukemic blasts and documented these results to the best of their abilities."
L867: **Review:** *The Applicant's response supports the uncertainty regarding whether the data for the parameter BLAST is a true marrow blast count. As such, this data file is unreviewable for the purposes of endpoint adjudication.*
L868: - In adchim.xpt, there are 245 cases in which there is a result for a marrow chimerism study, but there are no marrow results in adlb.xpt based on the data element for parameter BLAST.
L869: In their 9/6/2022 response, the Applicant indicated that "The questioned marrow results (leukemic blasts in bone marrow) are not available." They further asserted that marrow results were not required if there were no signs of relapse or progression and that additional marrow results were in adcefa.xpt.
L870: Marrows on Day +28 and Day +100 were required by protocol independent of whether there were signs of relapse. Assessment of the discordant cases indicated that 179 of the missing were from Day +28 or Day +100, and that the data available in adcefa.xpt accounted for only 24 of the missing Day +28 or Day +100 cases.
L871: **Review:** *From the presence of marrow chimerism data without concordant marrow blast counts, one can conclude that there are still data that exist but are not submitted in accordance with the protocol.*
L872: 3. In the table on page 65 of the Complete Response to the CRL received in SDN 65, the Applicant indicated that presence or absence of dysplasia was not a data element collected, and that the Cheson 2006 criteria were used.
L873: **Review:** *The Cheson criteria require analysis of outcomes with and without incorporating the presence of dysplasia. Consequently, it may not be possible to assess the overall response rate, the endpoint used for regulatory decision-making, for the subgroup of patients with MDS.*
L874: **Summary Statement:** *Comment #1 of the CRL advised that submission of the CBC and marrow results through the assessment period for EFS would be needed to confirm that the results of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II. The current version of the dataset is incomplete, and the parameter for the marrow blast count contains misleading information.*
L875: **Recommendation:** *Acknowledge Incomplete Response Letter*
L876: ## Letter-Ready Comments
L877: - You have not provided substantial evidence of effectiveness of treosulfan as a part of a preparative regimen for allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation for adult and pediatric patients with AML or MDS for the following reasons:A major element of the response criteria for both AML and MDS is the percentage of blasts on morphological examination of the marrow. In response to our 9/1/2022 query regarding implausible results in lb.LBSTRESN for LBTESTCD==BLAST, you indicated that this variable may include results of MRD assays rather than the percentage of marrow blasts. As such, there is insufficient information to confirm the primary endpoint of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II.
- In response to our 9/1/2022 query identifying cases where there were results for marrow chimerism studies in adchim.xpt but no results for blasts in lb.xpt on the same date, you indicated that marrow results were not required in the absence of signs of relapse, and that additional blast percentages were submitted in adcefa.xpt. According to the Flow
L878: Chart / Clinical and Laboratory Tests in the protocol for Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II, disease status by marrow examination was required for at least Day +28 and Day +100. Assessment of the discordant cases indicated that 179 of the missing cases were from Day +28 or Day +100, which would be required by protocol independent of whether there were signs of relapse. Additionally, the data available in adcefa.xpt accounted for only 24 of the missing Day +28 or Day +100 cases. Therefore, it appears that there is still substantial missing data.
L879: To address this issue, submit a pdf of the source documentation for all marrow results (i.e., copies of the pathology reports) through the complete assessment period for EFS for MC-FLudT.14/L Trial II, submit a revision of lb.xpt (and all dependent data files) to separate out results of the MRD assays from the percentage of marrow blasts, and submit a revision of adnewrel.xpt to include the results for percentage of marrow blasts from both lb.xpt and adcefa.xpt. Whether the results will be adequate to support approval of both indications will be a review issue.

L928: ## 1 Executive Summary
L929: ## 1.1 Product Introduction
L930: Proposed Trade Name:	Grafapex
Established Name:	Treosulfan
Also Known As:	Treosulphan, dihydroxybusulfan, CAS # 299-75-2, Ovastat®
Chemical Name:	1,2,3,4-butanetcol, 1,4-dimethanesulfonate; threitol 1,4-dimethanesulfonate; threitol 1,4-bismethanesulfonate; L-threitol 1,4-bis(methanesulfonate); (2S,3S)-2,3-dihydroxybutane-1,4-diyl dimethanesulfaonte; (2S,3S)-(-)-1,4,-Di(mesyloxy)-2,3-butanediol
Molecular Formula:	C_6H_{14}O_8S_2	Chemical Structure:	
Molecular Weight:	278.3 g/mol
Dosage Forms:	Injection, lyophilized (1 gm, 5 gm)
Therapeutic Class:	Antineoplastic
Chemical Class:	Small molecule
Pharmacologic Class:	Alkylating agent
Mechanism of Action:	Treosulfan is an alkylating agent. DNA alkylation is thought to be responsible for the cytotoxic activities of treosulfan. Treosulfan depleted hematopoietic stem cells and showed immunosuppressive and antitumor activity in mouse models of leukemia.
L931: NDA 214759 for Grafapex was submitted under the 505(b)(1) pathway for the indication "as part of conditioning treatment prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT) in adult patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS)" using a treosulfan dose of 10 g/m2 per day combination with fludarabine based on the results of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II. The Applicant also proposed to include in the USPI Section 14 a description of
L932: ## 1.2 Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L933: A complete response is recommended under 21 CFR 314.125(b)(5) due to lack of substantial evidence of effectiveness.
L934: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L935: NDA 214759
L936: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L937: MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II (NCT00822393) was a multicenter, randomized, active-controlled trial comparing treosulfan to busulfan in combination with fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT. Eligible patients included adults 18 to 70 years old with AML or MDS, Karnofsky performance status &gt; 60%, and age \ge 50 years or hematopoietic cell transplantation comorbidity index [HCT CI] score &gt; 2. The patients were randomized to receive treosulfan 10 g/m2 daily on days -4, -3 and -2 or to busulfan 0.8 mg/kg every 6 hours on days -4 and -3 in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2 daily on days -6, -5, -4, -3 and -2, and hematopoietic stem cell transplantation on day 0. Antithymocyte globulin was administered as well to patients with unrelated donors.
L938: The study was designed as a noninferiority trial with a primary endpoint of event-free survival (EFS) with 2 years of follow-up after transplantation. Events were defined as disease relapse, graft failure, or death. The noninferiority margin on the hazard ratio scale was 1.3. If noninferiority was demonstrated, then the primary endpoint would be tested for superiority. Overall survival (OS) was a secondary endpoint, but there was no alpha allocated to testing OS.
L939: The trial was stopped at the second interim analysis when EFS noninferiority was achieved. Since the trial continued enrolling until the results of the interim analysis were known, an additional "last analysis" was conducted using all enrolled patients. On the basis of the last analysis, the Applicant concluded that the results supported noninferiority for EFS, and in sequential testing, superiority of treosulfan for EFS (48.6% information level). Superiority of treosulfan for OS was not demonstrated at the second interim analysis but was at the last analysis.
L940: The review team was not able to confirm the results of the primary endpoint analysis, since the raw data needed to verify the components the EFS endpoint were incomplete. Additionally, in the absence of a prespecified plan for testing OS for superiority with control of Type 1 error, the results of the OS analysis were not considered substantial evidence of effectiveness.
L941: ## 1.3 Benefit-Risk Assessment
L942: In the absence of evidence to conclude effectiveness, a benefit-risk assessment cannot be formulated.
L943: ## 1.4 Patient Experience Data

L946: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L947: NDA 214759
L948: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L949: ## 1.5 Comments to Applicant
L950: - You have not provided substantial evidence of effectiveness of treosulfan as a part of a preparative regimen for allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation for adult and pediatric patients with AML or MDS for the following reasons:

The primary endpoint of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II was event-free survival (EFS). Because all complete blood cell (CBC) and marrow results were not submitted, we were unable to independently confirm the results of the analysis of the primary endpoint of EFS for Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II.

- Additionally, because of the missing CBC and marrow results, we were not able complete our assessment of whether the results of the study would support the indications for treatment of patients with AML and for treatment of patients with MDS.
L951: To address this issue, submit the CBC and marrow results through the complete assessment period for EFS for MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II. Whether the results would be adequate to support approval of both indications will be a review issue.
L952: - [REDACTED] (b) (4) OS cannot be used as the sole basis for approval for the following reasons:

The statistical plan for Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II did not include control of Type 1 error for overall survival (OS).

- There was no prespecified boundary for OS interim analyses and no alpha allocation for multiple OS analyses. Therefore, p-values are nominal, and analysis of OS is considered to be exploratory.
L953: To address this issue, submit data from an adequate and well-controlled trial of treosulfan vs busulfan with a prespecified analysis of OS for each indication with control of Type 1 error.
L954: ## Additional Comments
L955: ## Clinical/Statistical
L956: - Treatment of AML and treatment of MDS are considered different indications. In order to obtain approval for both indications, you would need to demonstrate substantial evidence of effectiveness for each individually.
L957: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L958: NDA 214759
L959: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L960: - Your subgroup analyses of EFS and OS suggest a substantial difference in treatment effect by donor type. Clarify how the results support your claim of noninferiority of treosulfan for HSCT from both related and unrelated donors (with the latter using ATG).
- You conducted your efficacy analyses in the Full Analysis Set (FAS). For a randomized trial with EFS and/or OS endpoints, the analyses should be conducted on the intent-to-treat (ITT) population.
- For Study MC-FludT.14/L II, the variable RACE in ADSL has no data element for any of the study subjects. Consequently, the submission did not provide adequate information to ensure that the study results are applicable to the US population. Consider conducting a study to assess outcomes by race and ethnicity for patients undergoing allogeneic HSCT using treosulfan-fludarabine as the preparative regimen. Include subgroup analyses by race and ethnicity for overall survival, nonrelapse mortality and relapse-free survival with at least 3 years of follow-up.
- (b) (4)
L961: ## Clinical Pharmacology
L962: - Address the following in the NDA resubmission:Provide data to determine an appropriate safe dose of treosulfan for patients with moderate renal impairment (CLcr 30-59 mL/min). This should be conducted in accordance with the FDA Guidance for Industry titled "Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Renal Function: Study Design, Data Analysis, and Impact on Dosing and Labeling."
- Provide data to support the evaluation of the effects of treosulfan on the QTc interval. This should be done in accordance with ICH E14.
- Provide data evaluating treosulfan and its epoxide metabolites as substrates of metabolizing enzymes *in vitro*.
- Provide data evaluating the pharmacokinetics (PK) of treosulfan active metabolites in adult patients.
- Provide data evaluating the effects of race and ethnicity on treosulfan PK.

L967: - We recommend that prior to the resubmission of the NDA, you conduct an in vitro study demonstrating DNA alkylation and breakage with treosulfan to adequately characterize the alkylating and genotoxicity properties and determine the potential serious risk of reproductive and developmental toxicity and carcinogenicity with treosulfan. This study should be submitted to the NDA with the resubmission.
L968: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L969: ## 2.1 Analysis of Condition
L970: Acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and high-risk myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS) have high rates of morbidity and mortality.
L971: ## 2.1.1 Acute Myeloid Leukemia
L972: AML is the most common type of acute leukemia in adult patients, with an incidence of 21,450 patients in the US in 2019. Fifty percent of patients with AML die of their disease. The incidence of AML increases with age. As the population of the United States ages, the incidence and median age of AML continues to increase. The median age of diagnosis is approximately 68 years [1]. Older patients often do not tolerate intensive chemotherapy, underscoring the need for new therapies.
L973: AML is a life-threatening condition. Allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) is required for curative therapy of high risk AML and IPSS-R intermediate, high, and very high risk MDS [2, 3]. A meta-analysis of 24 trials evaluating allogeneic HSCT, autologous HSCT, and chemotherapy alone for AML identified a significant reduction in hazard of death if allogeneic HSCT occurs in CR1 as compared to autologous HSCT or chemotherapy alone (hazard ratio 0.9, 95% CI 0.82-0.97) [4]. Further analysis revealed that the benefit of allogeneic HSCT was significant only in intermediate-risk (HR 0.76) and poor-risk (HR 0.69) AML, but not in favorable-risk (HR 1.06) AML. Additionally, the combined HR for relapse-free survival was 0.8 (0.74-0.86) across all risk groups and 0.78 (0.73-0.84) for intermediate and poor-risk patients.
L974: ## 2.1.2 Myelodysplastic Syndrome
L975: MDS encompasses a group of hematopoietic stem cell disorders, which have dysplasia in myeloid, erythroid, or megakaryocytic lineages and are associated with cytopenias [5]. The median age of diagnosis is 70 years [5]. The most recent SEER 5-year age-adjusted incidence
L976: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L977: NDA 214759
L978: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L979: rate (2014-2018) is 4.2 per 100,000, with a higher incidence in men (5.8 per 100,000 compared to 3.1 per 100,000 in women) [6].
L980: Risk classification of MDS is performed using the Revised International Prognostic Scoring System (IPSS-R). Patients are classified as having very low, low, intermediate, high, or very high risk MDS based on bone marrow cytogenetics, bone marrow blast percentage, and cytopenias [7]. Patients with intermediate, high-risk, or very high-risk MDS based on IPSS-R require allogeneic stem cell transplantation for curative therapy [8].
L981: ## 2.2 Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L982: Currently, no drugs are approved for use as a preparative regimen prior to allogeneic HSCT for AML or MDS.
L983: Drugs used in the standard of care for preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT in AML and MDS include, but are not limited to, cyclophosphamide, busulfan, fludarabine, melphalan, ATG, and radiation therapy. Table 1 provides comparisons of various HSCT preparative regimens from 1996-2020.

L990: ## 2.2.1 Myeloablative conditioning
L991: Preparative regimens for allogeneic HSCT are not standardized and range greatly from institution to institution and country to country. Myeloablative conditioning (MAC) results in complete eradication of host hematopoiesis and severe immunosuppression. Severe myelosuppression may directly kill leukemia stem cells, and AML is further attacked by donor cells, causing a graft versus leukemia (GVL) effect. The most common myeloablative preparative regimens are cyclophosphamide (120 mg/m2) and total body irradiation and busulfan (14-16 mg/kg) and cyclophosphamide (120-200 mg/m2). However, MAC is associated with high rates of transplant-related morbidity and mortality, particularly in older patients with pre-existing comorbidities [9].
L992: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L993: NDA 214759
L994: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L995: ## 2.2.2 Reduced intensity conditioning
L996: Reduced intensity conditioning (RIC) regimens have lower rates of transplant-related morbidity and mortality, but they range in their myelosuppressive and immunosuppressive characteristics. In general, the CIBMTR defines RIC regimens as TBI \le 5 Gy (single dose) or \le 8 Gy (fractionated), busulfan doses \le 9 mg/kg, or melphalan doses \le 150 mg/m^2. The most frequently used RIC regimens in a 2015 publication included fludarabine with either low-dose TBI or an alkylating agent [10]. A T-cell depleting agent such as ATG or Campath is used often, especially for matched unrelated donors.
L997: ## 2.2.3 MAC versus RIC
L998: Table 1 shows a comparison of HSCT conditioning regimens over the last 25 years. For patients with AML, MAC regimens are generally not significantly different from each other [11-13].
L999: Comparison of MAC vs RIC for patients with AML has not led to consensus. A 2017 publication of a study conducted by the Blood and Marrow Transplant Clinical Trials Network (BMT CTN) showed decreased transplant-related mortality with RIC HSCT. The study population included patients with MDS or AML aged 18-65 with HCT-CI \le 4. While overall survival (OS) was similar between the groups, MAC HSCT resulted in a significantly decreased risk of relapse-free survival (RFS) compared to RIC HSCT [14]. Interestingly, patients with MDS did not have a difference in RFS. However, multiple regimens were available for both RIC and MAC HSCT, with no one standard of care. Sixty-four percent of MAC HSCTs were Flu/Bu4, 30% were Bu/Cy, and the remainder were Cy/TBI. RIC HSCT regimens included Flu/Bu2 (81%) and Flu/Mel (19%) [14]. Other studies have not shown differences in OS or event-free survival, however [15-19]. For AML patients with detectable minimal residual disease, MAC may result in improved survival [20].
L1000: Within RIC regimens, two of the most commonly used conditioning regimens are fludarabine + busulfan and fludarabine + melphalan. The CIBMTR published an analysis of their transplant registry, comparing 622 AML patients who received a fludarabine + melphalan RIC HSCT and 791 AML patients who received a fludarabine + busulfan RIC HSCT. Fludarabine + melphalan was associated with higher transplant-related mortality but lower relapse risk; overall survival and leukemia-free survival were similar between the two regimens [1].
L1001: ## 2.2.4 Patient Selection for RIC
L1002: While RIC HSCTs are safe and have efficacy, particularly in an older, less fit population, MAC regimens continue to confer a benefit in survival and relapse. However, as the age of AML diagnosis increases, more AML patients are undergoing RIC HSCT. While there are no standard guidelines for which patients should receive RIC vs MAC, one trial made the following recommendations for RIC [14]:
L1003: - Patients &gt;65 years of age OR
- Patients with HCT comorbidity index (HCT-CI) scores \ge 3.
L1004: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1005: NDA 214759
L1006: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1007: HCT-CI scoring includes categories for age and a number of potential comorbidities in different organ-system classes. A multi-institutional retrospective analysis of approximately 3000 patients validated the use of the HCT-CI score for TRM. Age &gt;20 was associated with increasing risk of TRM, with statistical significance for all patients 40 years of age and older. An HCT-CI of 0, 1, or 2 resulted in no statistical significance in the hazard ratio for survival; survival rates were &gt;60% at two years for both RIC and MAC. An HCT-CI score of 3 or 4 resulted in two-year survival of 50% for RIC and 45% for MAC; an HCT-CI \ge 5 had two-year survival rates of 35 and 29%, for RIC and MAC, respectively [9].
L1008: Based on these data, for patients with AML and MDS who are either \ge 65 years of age, or who have an HCT-CI score \ge 3 should be considered for RIC allogeneic HSCT.
L1009: ## 3 Regulatory Background
L1010: ## 3.1 U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L1011: Treosulfan is not currently marketed in the United States and has no history of being marketed in the United States.
L1012: ## 3.2 Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
L1013: Interactions between the Applicant and the Agency were conducted under IND 054552. The clinical trials supporting this application were conducted outside the US and not under the IND. The key presubmission regulatory activities for this agent are as follows:
L1014: - IND 054552 was received in October 1997. It was inactivated in March 2003 and terminated in July 2010 due to lack of activity for more than 5 years.
- IND 054552 was reinstated and reactivated in 2013 to provide a source for cross references.
- Type B EOP2 Meeting 9/4/2014 - Key advice included that US Busulfex would be expected as the comparator, data was needed to show that EFS is a valid surrogate of OS in the population of patients transplanted for AML in CR and with MDS, data would be needed to serve as the basis for the noninferiority margin, the design does not provide any assurance that there would be sufficient power to test long-term outcomes within disparate groups by disease, donor type and MHC compatibility, (b) (4)
L1015: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1016: NDA 214759
L1017: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1018: - Orphan drug designation was granted 4/8/2015 for treosulfan as a “conditioning treatment prior to HSCT in malignant and nonmalignant diseases in adults and pediatric patients.”
- Type C Meeting responses 12/3/2015 - Key advice included the need to use US Busulfex as the comparator in the US, the need for justification for use of EFS rather than OS as the basis for determining noninferiority, the need to demonstrate that the constancy assumption was met in the determination of the noninferiority margin, the need to provide data showing the magnitude of the treatment effect of Bu-Flu over a comparator (such as chemotherapy alone) for the intended population, the need to justify why a loss of nearly 10 percentage points of OS is not clinically meaningful relative to the treatment effect of Bu-Flu over a comparator, the need to justify the design not addressing the heterogeneity in the eligible population with regard to disease, disease status, donor type and MHC compatibility, [REDACTED](b) (4), that the ITT population (all randomized) should be used for the primary efficacy analysis, and that OS and EFS should be conducted based on event-driven analyses.
- Type C Meeting 9/17/2019 - Key advice included the need to provide data showing the magnitude of the treatment effect of Bu-Flu over a comparator for the intended population, the need to justify why a loss of nearly 10 percentage points of EFS is not clinically meaningful relative to the treatment effect of Bu-Flu over that comparator, and that the analyses should be event-driven and that the confidence intervals and significance levels be adjusted accordingly. Additionally, since EFS at 2nd interim analysis used only a 28% information level, the data did not appear sufficiently mature as a basis for a marketing application.
- Type C Written Response Only 2/24/2020 - FDA advised the Applicant to evaluate the potential for treosulfan to prolong the QT/QTc prolongation interval, and to identify the pathways by which treosulfan and its active metabolites, if any, are eliminated to determine the need to assess the effect of organ impairment on their systemic exposures.
- Type B Pre-NDA Meeting 4/2/2020 - FDA noted that in the proposed “Justification of NI Margin”, the assertion that BuFlu has an equivalent treatment effect vs. chemotherapy to other conditioning regimens was not supported by data, that the noninferiority margin was based on a point estimate rather than incorporating the variability of the historical estimate, and that the proposed post-hoc justification of the noninferiority margin should document all studies considered for such developing such a margin, and the reasons for including or excluding studies in the subsequent analyses.
- Type C Written Responses Only 7/6/2020 - FDA provided advice on the design of Study MC-FludT.18/QTc to assess for effects on QTc, indicated that the NDA should include a complete
L1019: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1020: NDA 214759
L1021: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1022: assessment of the safety of the drug, including effects on QTc, and requested that any delay in conduct of the QT study should be justified in the NDA submission.
L1023: - NDA 214759 was received on 8/11/2020 and filed on 10/7/2020 with a standard review classification. The following filing and review issues were noted:For MC-FludT.14/L II, the post-HSCT bone marrow studies and the CBC results post Day +100 were not submitted from the clinical sites as part of the CRF, we are not able to independently verify the primary endpoint of EFS at 24 months. Without these data, our ability to perform a substantive review will be limited.
- For Study MC-FludT.14/L II, the variable RACE in ADSL has no data element for any of the study subjects. The submitted package lacks adequate information to ensure that the study results are applicable to the US population.
- Midcycle Communication was held 1/10/2021. The FDA reiterated the above review issues.
- Late Cycle Communication was held 5/5/2021. In addition to the above, the FDA identified the following issue:Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II included patients with matched related donors (MRD) and matched unrelated donors (MUD). Survival (OS) was significantly different between the treosulfan and busulfan arms for the ITT population with a hazard ratio (HR) of 0.65 (95% CI: 0.48-0.88), but on subgroup analysis, there was no apparent treatment effect in the patients with MRDs (HR 1.002 (0.55, 1.84)).

L1027: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L1028: ## 4.1 Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L1029: Two clinical sites (Site 01 Essen and Site 27 Mainz) were inspected for Study Mc-FludT.14/L. Site 01 Essen has the largest number of patients enrolled (~20% of the randomized population) and a disproportionate number of protocol violations. Site 27 Mainz has a disproportionate hazard ratio for the primary endpoint (0.21 vs 0.69 for the entire study population) and a disproportionate number of protocol violations. Based on the inspections, OSI concluded that the conduct at the study sites was adequate and study data derived from clinical sites appeared reliable.

L1031: Not applicable
