L1036: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1037: NDA 214759
L1038: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1039: ## 5.1 Executive Summary
L1040: Treosulfan (L-Threitol 1,4-bismethanesulfonate) is an alkylating drug and is closely related to busulfan with the addition of 2 hydroxyl groups to facilitate solubility in water. Since treosulfan was synthesized 60 years ago and has been widely used as a cytotoxic drug to treat ovarian cancer for many decades, it is well-characterized in peer-reviewed scientific literature. The Applicant (Medac GmbH) provided references to published studies in support of the marketing application. Treosulfan is indicated in combination with fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation in patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) or myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS). Except as specifically identified, all nonclinical data and information discussed below and necessary for approval of this NDA (#214759) are owned by Medac GmbH or are data for which Medac GmbH has a right of reference. Any information or data that Medac GmbH does not own or have a written right to reference is for descriptive purposes only and is not relied upon for approval of this NDA.
L1041: In cellular assays employing primary pediatric leukemic cells and leukemic cell lines, 72-hour treosulfan exposure had a cytotoxic effect with inhibitory concentration yielding 50% response (IC_{50}) values ranging from 0.7 to 490 \muM. Cells from patients with common acute lymphoblastic leukemia (cALL) were most sensitive, while cells from a patient with mature B-ALL were most resistant to treosulfan. In most cases, under the conditions tested, cells were more sensitive to treosulfan than to busulfan. Immune cells were also sensitive to treosulfan cytotoxicity, as T cells, NK cells, and stem cells isolated from healthy donors yielded IC_{50s} from &lt;0.4 to 49.7 \muM, with stem cells exhibiting the most sensitive effect.
L1042: Treosulfan showed in vivo myeloablative and immunosuppressive activity in a murine allogeneic bone marrow transplant model. BALB/c mice received 1.5 g/kg/day of treosulfan intraperitoneally (i.p.) x 3 days. Stem cells (assessed via colony forming units, CFUs) were rapidly depleted by post-treatment Day 1 and were negligible for the remaining 12 days of the experiment. Fluorescence activated cell sorting (FACS) analysis also showed decreases in T and B cells. The number of immune cells producing tumor necrosis factor (TNF)-\alpha or interferon (IFN)-\gamma remained constant or decreased slightly, while the percentage of interleukin 2 (IL-2)-producing T cells decreased to 50% of control values on Days 6-12. A mixed lymphocyte reaction test demonstrated that treosulfan-induced T-cell depletion suppressed alloreactivity in host splenic cells (BALB/c) mixed with C57/black stimulator lymphocytes.
L1043: The Applicant also included several references describing treosulfan's antileukemic and antineoplastic activity in vivo. Antitumor activity was demonstrated in mice bearing sarcoma or carcinoma tumors.
L1044: In cardiovascular in vitro safety pharmacology studies, treosulfan did not exhibit the potential to induce QT prolongation. There were also no changes in cardiovascular parameters when
L1045: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1046: NDA 214759
L1047: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1048: fasting dogs received 5-200 mg/kg of treosulfan intravenously (i.v.) and were monitored via ECG for 4 hours. Central nervous system (CNS) functions were not altered in mice that received 100 mg/kg of treosulfan i.p. or in a fasted dog that received 200 mg/kg of treosulfan via oral administration (p.o.).
L1049: To assess the safety of treosulfan, the Applicant conducted a GLP-compliant toxicology study up to 4 weeks in adult Sprague-Dawley rats. Rats received treosulfan at doses of 10, 50, or 150 mg/kg via i.v. once per day x 28 days, followed by a 2-week recovery period. The Applicant assessed 2 different formulations of treosulfan, a powdered form and a lyophilisate form. Toxicokinetic (TK) analysis showed that exposures were dose-proportional and were comparable between males and females. Exposures and toxicities were also generally comparable between formulations. There was no apparent accumulation over time. Drug-related adverse findings included bone marrow suppression, lymphoid atrophy in spleen, thymic hemorrhage and atrophy, GI tract hyperplasia, muscle necrosis, multi-organ inflammation, and injection site reactions. Inflammation was observed in the kidney, colon, urinary bladder, and prostate gland. The male and female reproductive organs (epididymis, testes, prostate, and ovary) were also targets of toxicity. Other findings included optic nerve hemorrhage, and Peyer's patch hyperplasia. There were 2 unscheduled deaths, both animals were in the high dose group and received the lyophilizate formulation. One female died in the 3rd week of dosing and was found to have edematous lungs and dilated intestines filled with fluid. The second animal was a male in the recovery cohort that had to be euthanized 1 day prior to the scheduled sacrifice (Day 42). The male had swelling in several areas including the nose/snout, paw, and testes that was discovered, upon histology analysis, to be a malignant leukemia. Males treated with either formulation also had enlarged epididymis and testes, which was not reversed during the recovery phase. Females treated with the powdered formulation had dose-dependent shrunken ovaries. Animals receiving either formulation had a dose-dependent increase in brain weight.
L1050: Treosulfan is genotoxic and induced a concentration-dependent increase in percent of anaphases with aberrations in onion root tips. The percentage of cells with aberrant anaphases increased from 3% to 21% when cells were treated with 50 mM of treosulfan or 0.05 mM of diepoxybutane (DEB), respectively. Dedicated carcinogenicity or fertility studies were not conducted with treosulfan, as they are not needed to support the use of a drug intended to treat patients with advanced cancer. Cytotoxic drugs that demonstrate genotoxicity are generally recognized as being carcinogenic, and in the general toxicology study, one animal treated with treosulfan presented with leukemia. A 7-month chronic-dose study conducted in Wistar rats revealed dose-dependent fertility impairment in males and females (i.e. arrested spermatogenesis and absent corpora lutea and follicles). To assess the potential for developmental toxicity, the Applicant conducted a juvenile toxicity study in which juvenile rats received treosulfan dosed at 10, 50, or 100 mg/kg/day i.v. in main and recovery groups during postnatal development days (PND) 10 to 35. Since the hematopoietic organs are targets of treosulfan, the findings in juvenile animals were typically comparable to the findings observed
L1051: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1052: NDA 214759
L1053: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1054: in adult animals. Juvenile rats had delayed development, observed as reduced body weight and a dose-dependent reduction in the size of male reproductive organs (testes and prostate). Moreover, females at 100 mg/kg experienced a significant delay in sexual maturation (vaginal opening delayed by 1.8 days). A pharmacokinetic assessment of the blood-brain barrier penetration of treosulfan showed that following single intravenous administration of 500 mg/kg to juvenile or young adult rats, plasma exposures (C_{max}) were comparable between groups but juvenile rats had exposures 2-fold higher than those observed in young adult rats. A local tolerance test conducted in New Zealand White rabbits was negative for drug-related findings.
L1055: There is no information on the presence of treosulfan in milk. As many drugs are secreted in breastmilk and because treosulfan has the potential to cause serious toxicities, the label includes a warning not to breastfeed during treatment with treosulfan and for 1 week after the final dose. The 1-week recommendation is based on the short half-life of the drug (5 \times T_{1/2} &lt; 1 week).
L1056: No embryofetal developmental toxicity was conducted. Drugs that are genotoxic and target rapidly dividing cells are expected to cause embryofetal lethality or malformations (see ICH S9). The product label will include this information and will provide a duration of contraception consistent with the FDA guidance *Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations* for genotoxic drugs.
L1057: From a pharmacology/ toxicology perspective, there are no outstanding issues that would prevent the approval of treosulfan in patients undergoing allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation(b) (4) with AML or MDS; however, there is one nonclinical post-marketing requirement (PMR). During the review of the NDA, it was determined that the nonclinical data for treosulfan submitted to the NDA in studies or literature for which the Applicant has the right to reference did not provide convincing evidence of DNA alkylation and breaks as the mechanism of action (MOA) of treosulfan. The PMR is to conduct an in vitro study to show treosulfan binds to DNA and causes DNA breakage.
L1058: As the recommendation has been a complete response (see Section 1.2), the pharmacology/toxicology discipline recommends the Applicant conduct and submit the results of the study at the time of resubmission.

L1061: ## 5.3 Pharmacology
L1062: ## Primary Pharmacology
L1063: ## In vitro pharmacology
L1064: Treosulfan exhibited antileukemic activity in pediatric tumor cells and cell lines. Munkelt et al. (2008) employed flow cytometry to quantify the cytotoxic effect of 72-hour exposure to treosulfan on primary pediatric leukemic cell specimens (bone marrow aspirates, pleural effusions, and/or peripheral blood) obtained from 20 children at the time of diagnosis, during therapy, or at relapse. Under the conditions tested, treosulfan showed greater activity than busulfan in all specimens except for 1 cALL relapse specimen (Table 2). Treosulfan demonstrated the greatest activity toward stem cells (CD34+) isolated from a healthy donor (IC_{50} &lt; 0.4 \mu M).

L1070: ## In vivo pharmacology
L1071: Treosulfan had myeloablative properties in vivo. The Applicant assessed the ability of treosulfan to condition mice for allogeneic bone marrow transplant (Expt. (b) (4) 008). Investigators treated C57BL/6J adult male mice (5/group) with a 4-day conditioning regimen of either 4 daily doses of 25 mg/kg Busulfex or 3 daily doses of 2000 mg/kg treosulfan administered i.p., followed by transplantation with 10 \times 10^6 bone marrow cells from B10.A donors. All Busulfex-treated mice survived the 16-week post-transplant period with little demonstration of toxicity. In contrast, treosulfan-treated mice experienced weight loss and diarrhea, and 1 mouse died. Donor chimerism (anti-H-2Dd staining) on myeloid and lymphoid cells showed no donor cells in Busulfex-treated mice. Two treosulfan-treated mice exhibited 85-100% donor chimerism; mice had grossly inflamed small intestines upon necropsy and may have developed GVHD (Figure 1).
L1072: Figure 1. Donor Chimerism in Peripheral Mouse Blood Following Busulfex or Treosulfan Conditioning and Allogeneic BMT
L1073: Bx = Busulfex; TR = treosulfan; BMT = bone marrow transplant (Excerpted from the Applicant's submission)
L1074: Treosulfan had myeloablative and immunosuppressive properties in studies conducted in mice. Sjöö et al. (2006) exposed BALB/c mice to treosulfan at 1.5 g/kg/day via i.p. injection for 3 consecutive days, then conducted stem cell and immune cell assays to characterize the effects. Stem cell quantity was rapidly depleted by post-treatment Day 1 and remained negligible through the remainder of the experiment (post-treatment Day 12), as assessed via colony forming units-granulocyte macrophage (CFU-GM; Error! Reference source not found., top row, left panel). FACS analysis revealed decreases in splenic B and T cells by post-treatment Day 1, reaching nadirs of 12.5% and 25% of controls, respectively, that remained suppressed through post-treatment Day 12 (Error! Reference source not found., top row, middle and right panels). Immunophenotyping showed comparable decreases in CD4+ T helper and CD8+ T killer cells, rather than elimination of specific subpopulations (not shown). Treosulfan treatment resulted in an increase in the percentage of IL-2-producing cells on post-treatment Days 1-3 following 6-hour in vitro activation with PMA/Ionomycin. This IL-2-producing CD3+ T-cell population decreased to 50% of controls from post-treatment Days 6-12 (Figure 2, bottom row, left panel). The number of splenic cells producing TNF-\alpha remained constant (Figure 2, bottom row, middle panel). The percentage of T cells producing IFN-\gamma only slightly decreased over the 12 post-treatment days (Figure 2, bottom row, right panel). Finally, a mixed lymphocyte reaction experiment demonstrated that treosulfan-induced T-cell depletion suppressed alloreactivity in host splenic cells (BALB/c) mixed with C57/black stimulator lymphocytes (data not shown).
L1075: Figure 2. Treosulfan Effects on Bone Marrow Stem Cells and Immune Cells
L1076: Control = green squares/ dotted line; treosulfan = blue diamonds/ solid line; busulfan = purple circles/ dotted line; cyclophosphamide = red triangles/ solid line
L1077: (Adapted from publication)
L1078: Treosulfan also had antineoplastic and antileukemic activity in several xenotransplantation models. In one published study the Applicant referenced, NOD/SCID mice bearing subcutaneously implanted human pediatric pre-B acute lymphoblastic leukemic (ALL) cells grew palpable tumors (about 5 mm) in 18 days, and then received a single intraperitoneal (i.p.) bolus injection of treosulfan at 1000, 2000, or 3000 mg/kg (Fichtner, 2003). Mice in the 1000 mg/kg group had delayed tumor growth (~7 days) compared to the saline-treated controls. Mice that received 2000 mg/kg had no evidence of tumors by Day 25, but tumors recurred in all mice by Day 55. Mice that received 3000 mg/kg also did not show any presence of tumor, but 1/8 had recurrent tumor by Day 55 (data not shown). In another Applicant-referenced study, NOD/SCID mice bearing Ewing's sarcoma (ES) cell line-derived tumors (CHP-100 cells) received an i.p. bolus injection of 3000 mg/kg of treosulfan. While control mice had a mean tumor volume of ~3500 \text{mm}^3 on Day 13, treosulfan-treated mice had a mean tumor volume ~100 \text{mm}^3, demonstrating activity against ES tumors in vivo (not shown).

L1084: Safety Pharmacology
L1085: ## *Cardiovascular function*
L1086: ## **Study # CYP1269 R11-R12:** Test of Treosulfan on hERG, hNav1.5, hCav1.2, hKir2.1 and hKCNQ1/minK Cardiac Ion Channels Using Automated Patch Approach
L1087: Investigators incubated HEK293 cells stably expressing human hERG potassium channel with treosulfan lyophilisate (200, 1000, or 5000 µM) or 0.3 µM of the reference article, E-4031, and measured potassium current using the patch-clamp technique in a non-GLP study. For comparison, the C_{max} in adults administered a clinical dose of 10 g/m2 i.v. treosulfan is approximately 300 µg/mL (~1000 µM). Treosulfan had no effect on hERG tail current (IC_{50} &gt; 5000 µM). The positive and negative controls behaved as expected. Investigators further evaluated the effects of 200, 1000, and 5000 µM treosulfan against additional cardiac ion channels using the patch clamp assay: hNav1.5 (peak and late), hCav1.2, hKir2.1, and hKCNQ1/minK. The % inhibition of each cardiac ion channel that was recorded when cells were exposed to 5000 µM is shown in Table 3.
L1088: **Table 3. Summary of % Current Inhibitions in Cardiac Channel Assays**
L1089: currents:	hERG tail	hNav1.5 Na+ (peak1)	hNav1.5 Na+ (peak2)	hNav1.5 Na+ (late)	hCav1.2 L-type Ca	hKir2.1 rapid delay K+	hKCNQ1/minK slow delay K+
% inhibition at 5000 µM	1.5%	16.8%	34.5%	18.3%	18.3%	3.7%	6.1%
L1090: 1 = tonic block 2=Use-dependent block
L1091: Treosulfan (10 µg/mL) had no effect on contractions of isolated guinea pig ileum produced by acetylcholine or histamine (Leo, 1978b). It also had no effect on isoprenaline-induced dilation of isolated cat tracheal muscle following exposure to concentrations ranging from 3 to 20 µg/mL.
L1092: In vivo evaluation of cardiovascular effects of treosulfan in female fasting adult mongrel dogs showed that i.v. dosing of 5-200 mg/kg of treosulfan had no effect on arterial or venous blood pressure, or the carotid occlusion reflex (Leo, 1978b). Moreover, 4-hour ECG evaluations in these treosulfan-exposed dogs were unremarkable. Female adult rats exposed to i.v. doses of treosulfan at 20 mg/kg, then 40 mg/kg 30 minutes later exhibited no changes in blood pressure.
L1093: ## *Respiratory and CNS Function*
L1094: Mice that received 100 mg/kg of treosulfan i.p. were not protected from convulsions induced by i.v. injection of pentetrazole or from the convulsant effects induced by electroshock (Leo, 1978b). Treosulfan (50 mg/kg p.o.) offered no analgesic effect to mice following i.p. injection of 50 mg/kg of acetic acid. It also offered no conduction anesthesia to electric stimulation in tail nerves following i.v. injection of 2% treosulfan. Ham/ICR mice administered 150 or 300 mg/kg of treosulfan i.p. showed no signs of abnormal behavior. A fasted female dog administered 200 mg/kg of treosulfan p.o. exhibited no change in respiration, heart rate, or pupil size for 4 hours post-dose.
L1095: ## 5.4 ADME/PK
L1096: The conversion of treosulfan to the epoxide compound is pH-dependent, and negligible \text{pH} &lt; 6. As such, transformation from treosulfan to the mono- or diepoxide species, and ratios of all 3 species, will differ based on the pH in the various in vivo compartments.
L1097: ## Treosulfan transformation into mono- and diepoxide species
L1098: Chemical reaction scheme showing the transformation of Treosulfan (I) into monoepoxide (II) and diepoxide (III) species.

Reaction 1: Treosulfan (I) \xrightarrow{k_1} Monoepoxide (II) + MesOH

Reaction 2: Monoepoxide (II) \xrightarrow{k_2} Diepoxide (III) + MesOH
L1099: I = Treosulfan; II = (2S,3S)-1,2-exoxy-3,4-butanediol + 4-methanesulfonate (MSA);
L1100: III = (2S,3S)-diepoxybutane (DEB) + MSA

L1107: ## 5.5 Toxicology
L1108: ## 5.5.1 General Toxicology
L1109: **Study title/ number:** 4-Week Subchronic Toxicity Study of Treosulfan by Repeated Intravenous Bolus Injection to CD® Rats/ Study # (b)(4) 37259
L1110: ## **Key Study Findings**
L1111: - There were 2 unscheduled deaths in the 150 mg/kg (high dose) group that received the lyophilizate formulation; one of these was a male in the 150 mg/kg treosulfan lyophilizate recovery group sacrificed on Day 42 due to malignant leukemia in the bone marrow, head, paws, and tail
- The target organs included the bone marrow (myelosuppression, decreased bone marrow cells), spleen (lymphoid atrophy), thymus (hemorrhage, atrophy), GI tract (hyperplasia), muscle (necrosis), injection site, and reproductive organs (infiltrates/inflammation in prostate, changes in epididymis and testicular weights).
- Additional toxicities included hemorrhage of the optic nerve and multi-organ inflammation
L1112: **GLP compliance:** Yes

L1117: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1118: NDA 214759
L1119: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1120: adrenals, edematous lungs, small spleen and thymus, and dilated intestines filled with liquid. Moreover, there was congestion and atrophy in bone marrow, congestion in kidneys, liver, lungs, spleen, and thymus, a granuloma of foreign body in the lungs, lymphoid atrophy in spleen, erosion of glandular mucosa in stomach, and atrophy and acute hemorrhage in thymus.
L1121: 1 male in the 150 mg/kg treosulfan lyophilisate recovery group was sacrificed on Day 42 (1 day before scheduled recovery sacrifice) due to swelling in several areas: tail, nose/snout, paw, and testes. Necropsy revealed enlarged kidneys and a hardened, brown-discolored liver. Histopathology showed the animal suffered from malignant leukemia in the bone marrow, head, paws, and tail, which caused the swelling in these areas. There were lymphocytic infiltrations and granulomatous inflammation in the epididymis; epidermal hyperplasia, exudative inflammation, and/or epidermal ulceration in the head, paws, and tail; bacterial infection in the paws and tail; an eosinophilic infiltration in the pelvis of the right kidney; hematopoiesis and interstitial fibrosis in the liver; increased hematopoiesis in the spleen; granuloma of foreign body in the tongue; and ulceration, chronic inflammation, and hypertrophy with atypical cells in the urinary bladder.

L1188: ## Juvenile Development
L1189: ## **Study title/ number: Subchronic Toxicity Study of Treosulfan by Intravenous Administration to Juvenile CD® Rats with a 5-Week Recovery Period/ Study # 26054**
L1190: ## Key Study Findings
L1191: - The original high dose was set at 250 mg/kg/day; however, several animals died prematurely at that dose and the dose was reduced to 100 mg/kg/day
- Adverse findings in males and females included reduced neutrophils
- Males in the 100 mg/kg group gained less weight over time than males in other groups and had minimal interstitial lymphocytic infiltrates
- Females in the 100 mg/kg group had a 1.8-day delay in the time point of vaginal opening and minimal reduction in splenic cellularity with concomitant reduction in spleen weight upon necropsy
L1192: GLP compliance: Yes
L1193: ## Methods
L1194: Dose and frequency of dosing: 0, 10, 50, or 100 mg/kg in main study and recovery groups on postnatal day (PND) 10 to 35
0, 10, 50, or 250 mg/kg/day in satellite groups on PND 10 to 31

Route of administration: IV bolus injection

Formulation/Vehicle: 0.9% NaCl solution

Species/Strain: Rat/ CD®/Sprague-Dawley

Number/Sex/Group: Main study: 10/sex/group
Recovery: 4/sex/group
30/sex/group

Satellite groups:

Unique study design:

Deviation from study protocol affecting interpretation of results: No
L1195: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	10 satellite animals at 250 mg/kg died prematurely between PND 19 and PND 30, resulting in a dose reduction to 100 mg/kg in the main study and recovery animals.
Clinical Signs	There were no drug-related changes at the injection site or appearance of clinical signs in treated animals.
Body Weights and Feed Consumption	Although males in the 100 mg/kg group gained significantly less weight than other groups, they consumed a comparable quantity of food. In contrast, females in the 100 mg/kg group gained a similar amount of weight as other groups but consumed significantly more food (+16%) from PND 52 to PND 66

L1209: ## **5.5.5 Other Toxicology Studies**
L1210: ## Local Tolerance
L1211: The right ear of each New Zealand White adult rabbit (n = 5) received 50 mg of treosulfan injected intra-arterially and 10 mg of treosulfan injected peri-venously parallel to the marginal vein of the same ear (Leo 1985). Left ears served as controls. There were no drug-related effects throughout a 5-day observation period.
L1212: ## Impurity Qualification
L1213: Chemistry and Manufacturing Controls (CMC) consulted with Pharmacology/Toxicology and Clinical Pharmacology regarding the potential safety risk of [REDACTED](b) (4). The Applicant reported a level of no more than (NMT) [REDACTED](b) (4)% of [REDACTED](b) (4) in any treosulfan batch. Clinical Pharmacology stated that from their perspective, there was no safety concern with the [REDACTED](b) (4)% limit for [REDACTED](b) (4). Pharmacology/Toxicology agrees that from a Pharmacology/Toxicology perspective there are no safety concerns since [REDACTED](b) (4) and as treosulfan has been in clinical use in Europe for many years, any adverse events caused by elevated [REDACTED](b) (4) levels would have been reported.
L1214: OPMA consulted with Pharmacology/Toxicology regarding the potential risk of [REDACTED](b) (4)-derived extractables per vial of treosulfan drug product, as patients exposed to the maximum daily dose of 16.2 g/day of treosulfan would be exposed to a total of
L1215: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1216: NDA 214759
L1217: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1218: (b)(4) mcg/day of extractables. The individual extractable substances are made up of heterogeneous versus homogeneous unknowns; each below (b)(4) mcg/day. Following a discussion with OPMA and CMC reviewers, Pharmacology/Toxicology concluded that this level is acceptable from a Pharmacology/Toxicology perspective because 1) treosulfan is indicated in combination with fludarabine for patients with advanced cancer, 2) patients will only receive treosulfan for 3 consecutive days, 3) treosulfan is already known to be genotoxic and carcinogenic, 4) fludarabine is also genotoxic and 5) (b)(4) used in Europe (where treosulfan has been used in patients with ovarian cancer for nearly 50 years).
L1219: ## 6 Clinical Pharmacology
L1220: ## 6.1 Executive Summary
L1221: The Applicant is seeking approval of treosulfan as part of conditioning treatment prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT) in adult patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS). The proposed dosing regimen is treosulfan 10 g/m2/day for 3 consecutive days (Days -4, -3, and -2) in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2/day for 5 consecutive days (Days -6 through -2) prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT; Day 0).
L1222: Pivotal evidence to support efficacy in the proposed population is based on the results of Study MC-FLudT.14/L Trial II. Key review issues include the adequacy of the proposed treosulfan dosing regimen in adult patients, pediatric extrapolation to identify a treosulfan dosing regimen appropriate for pediatric patients \ge 1 year of age, and evaluation of the Applicant's proposals for patients with organ impairment. The Applicant provided pharmacokinetic studies in adults and pediatric patients, a population PK model of treosulfan, and a dose-response evaluation in adults to support the dose selection in the general population and specific populations. The Applicant did not provide adequate data to resolve other review questions including characterization of treosulfan metabolism and effects of treosulfan on QTc interval.
L1223: ## Recommendations
L1224: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed the information contained in NDA 214759. This NDA is not approvable from a Clinical Pharmacology perspective given that the Agency was not able to independently verify the primary endpoint supporting evidence of effectiveness (Refer to **Section 8**). The key review issues with specific recommendations and comments are summarized below.
L1225: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1226: NDA 214759
L1227: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1228: REVIEW ISSUE	RECOMMENDATIONS/COMMENTS
Pivotal or supportive evidence of effectiveness	In Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, treatment with the proposed dosing regimen of treosulfan + fludarabine improved overall survival (OS) with a hazard ratio (HR) and 95% confidence interval (CI) of 0.65 (0.48, 0.88) compared to treatment with busulfan + fludarabine. However, the Agency was not able to independently verify the primary endpoint of event-free survival (EFS) for Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (Refer to Section 8).
General dosing instructions	The recommended dosage regimen of treosulfan is 10 g/m2/day for 3 consecutive days (Days -4, -3, and -2) in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2/day for 5 consecutive days (Days -6 through -2) prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (Day 0).
Dosing in patient subgroups (intrinsic and extrinsic factors)	
Pediatric Patients \ge1 year of age: The recommended dosage regimen of treosulfan is the same for all patients \ge1 year of age. Efficacy in pediatric patients \ge1 year of age was extrapolated from adults and dose selection was supported by PK and safety data from Studies MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M. Treosulfan exposure after administration of 10 g/m2 in pediatric patients with BSA <0.7 m2 and with BSA 0.7 to <1.1 m2 are 11% and 5% higher, respectively, compared to adults.
Renal Impairment: No dosage modification is recommended for patients with mild renal impairment. An additional study will be necessary to identify a safe dose of treosulfan for patients with moderate hepatic impairment. A study in patients with severe renal impairment is not feasible and will not be required.
Hepatic Impairment: No dosage modification is recommended for patients with mild hepatic impairment.
L1229: ## Clinical Pharmacology Outstanding Issues
L1230: Key Issue(s) to be Addressed	Rationale	Key Considerations for Design Features
Determination of an appropriate safe treosulfan dose for use in patients with moderate renal impairment.	There is evidence of renal elimination of treosulfan. Renal impairment may increase treosulfan systemic exposures and require dose adjustment.	Conduct a clinical trial to evaluate the pharmacokinetics and safety of treosulfan in patients with normal renal function and patients with moderate renal impairment.
Evaluation of treosulfan and epoxide metabolites as substrates of metabolizing enzymes.	The Applicant did not evaluate treosulfan or its epoxide metabolites as substrates of major metabolizing enzymes. This evaluation is necessary to describe risk of drug-drug interactions and effects of hepatic impairment on treosulfan safety and PK.	Conduct an in vitro study to evaluate treosulfan and epoxide metabolites as substrates of major cytochrome P450 enzymes consistent with FDA guidance.
L1231: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1232: NDA 214759
L1233: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1234: Key Issue(s) to be Addressed	Rationale	Key Considerations for Design Features
Evaluation of the effects of treosulfan on the QT interval.	The effects of treosulfan on the QT interval have not been evaluated. A QT study should be performed to evaluate the cardiac safety of treosulfan.	Conduct a clinical trial to evaluate the effects of treosulfan on the QT interval in patients receiving the recommended dosage of treosulfan.
Evaluation of treosulfan active metabolite PK in adult patients.	The PK of treosulfan parent drug was evaluated in 24 adult patients. The PK of active epoxide metabolites was not evaluated in adult patients.	Evaluate the PK of treosulfan active epoxide metabolites in adult patients.
Determination of the effects of race and ethnicity on the PK of treosulfan.	Race and ethnicity data were not collected in the pivotal study or adult PK sub-study of treosulfan. The effects of race and ethnicity on the PK of treosulfan is therefore unknown.	Conduct a clinical trial to evaluate the effects of race and ethnicity on the pharmacokinetics of treosulfan.
L1235: ## 6.2 Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L1236: ## 6.2.1 Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L1237: Treosulfan is an alkylating agent with cytotoxic activity. The PK of treosulfan was described using a population PK model. The final population PK (PPK) model for treosulfan is a two-compartment model with linear elimination and intercompartmental clearance. BSA is included as a significant covariate on CL, V1, V2 and Q in the final model, supporting a BSA-based dosing regimen.
L1238: The area under the curve (AUC; mean \pm SD) in adult patients was 1,157 \pm 193 hr\cdotmcg/mL after administration of 10 g/m^2. There was no dose accumulation.
L1239: **Distribution:** The volume of distribution in adult patients is about 41.1 liters (CV%: 19.2%). In animal studies, treosulfan penetration through the blood brain-barrier is limited [see Section 5.4]. Treosulfan does not bind to plasma albumin.
L1240: **Elimination:** The median terminal half-life of treosulfan is 2.2 hours.
L1241: **Metabolism:** Treosulfan is converted into an active monoepoxide intermediate [(2S,3S) 1,2 epoxybutane 3,4 diol 4 methanesulfonate] and finally to L diepoxybutane [(2S,3S) 1,2:3,4 diepoxybutane].
L1242: **Excretion:** A median of 42% of the treosulfan dose is excreted unchanged with the urine within 24 hours, and 89% of this unchanged fraction is excreted within the first 8 hours after administration.
L1243: ## 6.2.2 General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
L1244: ## General Dosing
L1245: The recommended regimen of treosulfan in adults and pediatric patients \ge 1 year of age is treosulfan 10 g/m2/day for 3 consecutive days (Days -4, -3, and -2) in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2/day for 5 consecutive days (Days -6 through -2) prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (Day 0).
L1246: ## Therapeutic Individualization
L1247: No dosage adjustment is recommended for patients with mild renal impairment (CLcr 60-89 mL/min) or mild hepatic impairment [total bilirubin less than or equal to upper limit of normal (ULN) with AST greater than ULN or total bilirubin greater than 1 to 1.5 times ULN with any AST]. No information is provided or requested for patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment as they are typically excluded from transplantation. Additional data will be necessary to inform the dosing of treosulfan in patients with moderate renal impairment (CLcr 30 – 59 mL/min).
L1248: ## Outstanding Issues
L1249: The following issues were not adequately addressed by the available data:
L1250: - Determination of an appropriate safe dose of treosulfan for patients with moderate renal impairment (CLcr 30-59 mL/min)
- Evaluation of the effects of treosulfan on the QTc interval
- Evaluation of treosulfan and its epoxide metabolites as substrates of metabolizing enzymes *in vitro*
- Evaluation of the PK of treosulfan active metabolites in adult patients
- Determination of the effects of race and ethnicity on treosulfan PK
L1251: ## 6.3 Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
L1252: ## 6.3.1 General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
L1253: General Information	
Description	
Chemical structure:
Treosulfan has the molecular formula C6H14O8S2 and a molecular weight of 278.3 g/mole.
L1254: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1255: NDA 214759
L1256: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1257: Bioanalysis	
In Study MCFludT.14/L Trial I PK Sub-study, treosulfan in human plasma and urine was measured using an HPLC assay. Accuracy (%RE) and precision (%CV) of the quality controls (QCs) for the runs used in measuring treosulfan plasma and urine concentrations were \le 15\%.
In Studies MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M, treosulfan and monoepoxide in human plasma were measured using an LC-MS/MS assay. Accuracy (%RE) and precision (%CV) of the QCs for the runs used in measuring treosulfan and monoepoxide plasma concentrations were \le 15\%.

Drug Exposure at Steady State	
The area under the curve (AUC; mean \pm SD) in adult patients was 1,157 \pm 193 hr\cdotmcg/mL. Treosulfan exposure after administration of 10 g/m^2 in pediatric patients with BSA <0.7 m^2 and with BSA 0.7 to <1.1 m^2 is 11% and 5% higher, respectively, compared to adults.

Range of Effective Dose or Exposure	
Patients receiving a higher dose of treosulfan (14 g/m^2/day for 3 consecutive days; 1.4 times the recommended dose) in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial I had a higher incidence of early fatal and/or serious adverse reactions including infections, febrile neutropenia, musculoskeletal pain, and hepatotoxicity.

Accumulation	
There was no dose accumulation with once daily administration of treosulfan over 3 consecutive days.

Active Moieties	
Treosulfan is converted into an active monoepoxide intermediate [(2S,3S)-1,2-epoxybutane-3,4-diol-4-methanesulfonate] and finally to L-diepoxybutane [(2S,3S)-1,2:3,4-diepoxybutane].

QT Prolongation	
The effects of treosulfan on the QT interval in humans have not been evaluated. In vitro, treosulfan does not significantly inhibit hERG at a concentration of 1,000 \muM.

Distribution	
Volume of Distribution	
The volume of distribution in adult patients is 41.1 liters (CV%: 19.2%). The treosulfan volume of distribution increases with BSA. In animal studies, treosulfan penetration through the blood brain-barrier is limited [see Section 5.4].

Plasma Protein Binding	
Treosulfan does not bind to plasma albumin.

Elimination	
Terminal Elimination Half-Life (SD)	
The median terminal half-life of treosulfan is 2.2 hours in adults and 1.5 hours in pediatric patients.
In pediatric patients, the median terminal half-life of the active monoepoxide intermediate is 1.6 hrs. No data are available on the PK of the monoepoxide intermediate in adults.

Metabolism	
Primary Metabolic Pathway(s)	
Treosulfan converts to mono- and di-epoxide metabolites in a temperature- and pH-dependent manner in (b)(4) human plasma. No studies have been conducted to evaluate treosulfan or its active metabolites as substrates of major metabolizing enzymes.
In pediatric patients (age range 4 months to 17 years), there was no relationship between age and the monoepoxide:parent AUC_{inf} ratio, suggesting no significant age-related differences in metabolism of treosulfan among pediatric patients. No monoepoxide PK data were available from adult patients; additional data will be needed to evaluate treosulfan and metabolite PK in adults.
L1258: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1259: NDA 214759
L1260: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1261: Excretion	
Primary Excretion Pathways, % dose (SD)	A median of 42% of the treosulfan dose is excreted unchanged in the urine within 24 hours, and 89% of this unchanged fraction is excreted within the first 8 hours after administration.
Drug Interactions	
Inhibition/Induction of Metabolism	In vitro, a combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites inhibits CYP2C19 and CYP3A at clinically relevant concentrations. A combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites does not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, or CYP2D6 at clinically relevant concentrations.
Evaluation of treosulfan as an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A was inconclusive due to cytotoxicity caused by incubation of treosulfan.
Inhibition of Transporter Systems	In vitro, a combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites inhibits P-gp. A combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites does not inhibit BCRP, BSEP, MATE1, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, or OCT2 at clinically relevant concentrations.
L1262: ## 6.3.2 Clinical Pharmacology Questions
L1263: ## Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of effectiveness?
L1264: No. The Agency was not able to independently verify the primary endpoint of EFS for Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (Refer to Section 8). Therefore, the efficacy evaluation for the current submission focused on overall survival (OS) results in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II. In Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, 570 adult patients planned to undergo allogeneic HSCT for AML or MDS were randomized 1:1 to receive treosulfan 10 g/m2/day x 3 days + fludarabine 30 mg/m2/day x 5 days or busulfan 0.8 mg/kg Q6H x 8 doses + fludarabine 30 mg/m2/day x 5 days. Treatment with the proposed dosing regimen of treosulfan + fludarabine improved OS with a HR (95% CI) of 0.65 (0.48, 0.88) compared to treatment with busulfan + fludarabine in the overall population. In adult patients with AML, treosulfan + fludarabine improved OS with a HR (95% CI) of 0.61 (0.41, 0.92) compared to treatment with busulfan + fludarabine while in adult patients with MDS treosulfan + fludarabine improved OS with a HR (95% CI) of 0.62 (0.34, 1.10) compared to treatment with busulfan + fludarabine. Refer to Section 8.1.1 for a detailed assessment of treosulfan efficacy in Trial II.
L1265: ## Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population for which the indication is being sought?
L1266: Yes. Treatment with the proposed dosing regimen resulted in improved overall survival compared to treatment with busulfan + fludarabine in adult patients with AML or MDS (Refer to Section 8.1.1).
L1267: Use of a higher dose of treosulfan (14 g/m2/day x 3 days) was evaluated in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial I. Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial I was identical in design to Trial II with the exception of the
L1268: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1269: NDA 214759
L1270: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1271: treosulfan dose (14 \text{ g/m}^2) and treosulfan schedule (Days -6, -5, and -4). A planned interim analysis of Trial I showed that the treosulfan 14 \text{ g/m}^2 + fludarabine arm had poorer survival and higher infectious complications than the busulfan + fludarabine arm and the trial was stopped early (Refer to **Section 8.1.2**). The differing outcomes in Trial I vs Trial II are attributed to the difference in treosulfan dose which lead to higher incidence of early fatal and/or serious adverse reactions.
L1272: The relationship between exposure and time to neutrophil engraftment [defined as time from Day 0 to the first of 3 consecutive days with absolute neutrophil count (ANC) &gt;0.5 \times 10^9/\text{L}] was assessed in adult patients. No significant E-R relationship between treosulfan AUC versus time to engraftment was identified; however, the adult E-R analysis had significant limitations including a small number of patients (24), a single dose level (14 \text{ g/m}^2) with narrow exposure range, and non-daily blood counts to assess ANC.
L1273: Separate exposure-response analyses for safety were conducted using data from 82 pediatric patients treated with treosulfan (10, 12, or 14 \text{ g/m}^2) + fludarabine in Studies MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M. There was no significant relationship identified between treosulfan exposure (AUC or C_{\text{max}}) and common adverse events (infection and gastrointestinal disorders). In addition, no significant relationship was identified between treosulfan exposure and duration of neutropenia (days with ANC &lt;0.5 \times 10^9/\text{L}) in pediatric patients. Limitations of the pediatric E-R analysis include administration of thiotepea in some (but not all) patients and non-daily blood counts to assess ANC.
L1274: In summary, treosulfan doses higher than 10 \text{ g/m}^2 are not appropriate for this population due to the risk of increased early morbidity and mortality with doses higher than recommended; do not exceed the recommended dosage of treosulfan.
L1275: ## Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors?
L1276: Limited pharmacokinetic data were available from adult patients treated with treosulfan. Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial I included a PK Sub-study which collected PK samples from 24 adult patients after administration of treosulfan 14 \text{ g/m}^2. PK samples were not collected in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II. A population PK (PPK) model was developed using data from the 24 adult patients in the Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial I PK Sub-study and 83 pediatric patients treated with treosulfan 10, 12, or 14 \text{ g/m}^2 in two pediatric studies (MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M). The final PPK model was described by a two-compartment model with linear elimination and intercompartmental clearance. BSA is included as a significant covariate on CL, V1, V2 and Q in the final model, supporting a BSA-based dosing regimen. Given the variability of treosulfan PK parameters (CV% 16.7% for \text{AUC}_{24\text{h}} and 19.2% for Vd), the proposed treosulfan dosing regimen of 10 \text{ g/m}^2 is acceptable. Refer to the Pharmacometrics review in **Section 16.4.1** for detailed review of the final PPK model.
L1277: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1278: NDA 214759
L1279: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1280: There were no clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of treosulfan based on sex, mild renal impairment (CLcr 60-89 mL/min), or mild hepatic impairment (total bilirubin less than or equal to ULN with AST greater than ULN or total bilirubin greater than 1 to 1.5 times ULN with any AST).
L1281: **Pediatric Patients:** Given the need for alternatives in pediatric patients, information regarding an appropriate dose of treosulfan for pediatric patients was evaluated. The Applicant provided safety and PK data from 115 pediatric patients administered treosulfan prior to alloHSCT in the following two clinical studies:
L1282: - MC-FludT.16/NM: open-label, randomized (1:1) study of treosulfan + fludarabine (n=46) vs busulfan + fludarabine (n=45) prior to alloHSCT in 91 pediatric patients (age range 4 months to 17 years) with non-malignant diseases. 82.5% (75/88) of treated patients received an intensified regimen with the addition of thiotepe.
- MC-FludT.17/M: open-label, single-arm study of treosulfan + fludarabine prior to alloHSCT in 70 pediatric patients (age range 4 months to 17 years) with hematological malignancies. 92.8% (65/70) of patients received an intensified regimen with the addition of thiotepe.
L1283: In both pediatric studies, the Applicant selected the dose of treosulfan by matching the treosulfan AUC24h in adults following the 14 g/m2 dosing regimen.

L1302: Exposure-response analyses for safety based on the two pediatric studies did not indicate that an 11.2% increase in exposure would result significant increase in the probability of common toxicities (infections and gastrointestinal disorders) given the variability of the drug exposure. Therefore, for the convenience of dosing and to avoid potential dosing errors with the Applicant's proposed dosing regimens, FDA recommends a treosulfan dose of 10 g/m2 for all pediatric patients \ge1 year.
L1303: **Geriatric Patients:** Pharmacokinetic differences between patients &lt;65 years of age compared to patients \ge65 years of age were evaluated using data from the Trial I PK Sub-study. The geometric mean AUC24h (1,126 \mug*h/mL for &lt;65 years vs 1,225 \mug*h/mL for \ge65 years) and Cmax (554 \mug/mL for &lt;65 years vs 556 \mug/mL for \ge65 years) were similar between age groups. Given the small number of patients \ge65 years of age with pharmacokinetic data (n=4), the effects of geriatric age on treosulfan PK were inconclusive. In addition, in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II there were no significant differences in treosulfan safety or effectiveness observed between patients 65 years of age or older (n=73) and younger adult patients (n=197) on the treosulfan + fludarabine arm. Refer to **Section 8.3.6** for additional information.
L1304: **Renal Impairment:** Treosulfan is excreted in the urine (median 42% recovery unchanged). The effects of renal impairment on treosulfan PK were evaluated using PK data from the Trial I PK Sub-study. A trend towards lower treosulfan clearance and higher treosulfan AUC was observed

L1312: The effects of renal impairment on treosulfan safety were evaluated using data from Trial II. L1325: **Race and Ethnicity:** The Applicant did not collect information on race or ethnicity from Study MC-FludT14.L Trial I, including the PK sub-study. Race was recorded for pediatric patients in Studies MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M; however, of the 83 pediatric patients with PK data, 80 were White, 2 were Asian, and 1 was Black. Therefore, the effects of race and ethnicity on treosulfan PK are unknown given that there were too few patients with differing races to assess the effects of race on treosulfan PK. Additional information will be necessary to evaluate these effects.
L1326: ## Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is the appropriate management strategy?
L1327: Treosulfan was evaluated *in vitro* as an inhibitor or inducer of major CYP enzymes and as an inhibitor of major transporters. Separate *in vitro* stability studies were conducted to evaluate the relative concentration of treosulfan and its active metabolites under conditions used in the *in vitro* CYP and transporter studies. Based on the stability studies, both treosulfan and its active metabolites are present *in vitro* and the relative concentrations change over time (reduction in parent treosulfan with corresponding increase in metabolites). Therefore, it is not possible to determine the contribution of each moiety to the observed results.
L1328: ## CYP Enzymes:
L1329: *In vitro*, a combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites inhibits CYP2C19 and CYP3A at clinically relevant concentrations. For CYP2C19, the treosulfan reversible IC50 was 972 µM and for CYP3A, the treosulfan reversible IC50 was 1,870 µM (calculated based on initial treosulfan
L1330: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1331: NDA 214759
L1332: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1333: concentration). A combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites does not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, or CYP2D6 at clinically relevant concentrations (IC_{50} &gt; 10,000 \mu M based on initial treosulfan concentration).
L1334: Physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modelling was used to predict the effects of treosulfan administration on CYP3A, CYP2C19, and P-gp substrates *in vivo*. The PBPK model and mechanistic static model were used to predict the magnitude of enzyme mediated DDI caused by treosulfan and its metabolite, EBDM, respectively. Results of the analysis showed that the potential DDI of treosulfan as a CYP3A4 inhibitor and a CYP2C19 inhibitor cannot be excluded. Refer to **Section 16.4.2** for detailed review of the Applicant's PBPK model.
L1335: Given the short half-life of treosulfan (2.2 hrs) and context of use (3-day course), no *in vivo* DDI study is required. For certain CYP3A or CYP2C19 substrates where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious or life-threatening toxicities providers should monitor for adverse reactions and reduce the dosage, as needed, if recommended in the prescribing information of these substrates.
L1336: Evaluation of treosulfan as an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A in human hepatocytes was inconclusive due to cytotoxicity caused by incubation of treosulfan.
L1337: ## *Transporter Systems:*
L1338: *In vitro*, a combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites inhibits P-gp. At an initial treosulfan concentration of 3,000 \mu M, treosulfan incubation inhibited P-gp mediated transport of loperamide by 51.1% (IC_{50} \sim 3,000 \mu M). Based on PBPK analysis, the potential DDI of treosulfan as a P-gp inhibitor cannot be excluded; however, the risk of clinically significant DDIs is low.
L1339: *In vitro*, a combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites does not inhibit BCRP, BSEP, MATE1, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, or OCT2 at clinically relevant concentrations (no concentration-dependent inhibition up to at least 3,000 \mu M initial treosulfan concentration).
L1340: ## **Has the cardiac safety of treosulfan, including effects on the QT interval, been adequately characterized?**
L1341: *In vitro*, treosulfan does not significantly inhibit hERG at a concentration of 1,000 \mu M. The Applicant has not systematically evaluated the effects of treosulfan on the QT interval in patients after administration of treosulfan. A total of 613 patients have received treosulfan in trials of treosulfan as part of a preparative regimen prior to transplantation. Post-baseline ECGs were available from only one study (MC-FludT.6/L). Two of 35 evaluable patients had a change to abnormal ECG post-baseline (one asymptomatic T-wave negativity and one sick sinus/atrial fibrillation).
L1342: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1343: NDA 214759
L1344: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1345: Given the mechanism of action (alkylating agent) and context of use (preparative regimen prior to alloHSCT) for treosulfan, a thorough QT study in healthy volunteers is not feasible. A QT study designed to exclude large mean increases (&gt;20 msec) in the QT/QTc interval should be conducted to evaluate the effects of treosulfan on the QT interval in patients receiving the recommended dosage of treosulfan.

L1367: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1368: NDA 214759
L1369: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1370: The applicant submitted information from 7 clinical trials of treosulfan, in combination with fludarabine, as preparative conditioning regimen for allogeneic HSCT. L1371: Studies MC-FludT.6.L, MC-FludT.7/AML, MC-FludT.8/MDS, MC-FludT.14/L I, MC-FludT.14/L II, MC-FludT.16/NM, and MC-FludT.17/M were included in the integrated summary of safety submitted by the Applicant. We performed the safety analysis on MC-FludT.6L, MC-FludT.7/AML, MC-FludT.8/MDS, MC-FludT.14/L I, and MC-FludT.14/L II for the adult population, and on MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M for the pediatric safety analysis.
L1372: Summaries of data and safety analyses by the clinical reviewer were performed using JMP version 15.0 (SAS Institute, Inc., Cary, NC) and MedDRA Adverse Events Diagnostic (MAED, FDA, Silver Spring, MD), versions 2.5 (through 3/21/2021) and 3.0 (after 3/21/20201). Postmarket analysis was conducted using Empirica Signal (Oracle, Santa Clara, CA, ©2014).
L1373: ## 8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
L1374: ## 8.1 Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L1375: ## 8.1.1 Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II
L1376: *"Clinical Phase III Trial to Compare Treosulfan-Based Conditioning Therapy With Busulfan-Based Reduced-Intensity Conditioning (RIC) Prior to Allogeneic Haematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation in Patients with AML Or MDS Considered Ineligible To Standard Conditioning Regimens - Trial II"*
L1377: ## INVESTIGATIONAL PLAN
L1378: ## Trial Design
L1379: ## Design and Objectives
L1380: The study was designed as a non-inferiority Phase III trial to compare treosulfan-based conditioning therapy to busulfan-based reduced-intensity conditioning therapy in adult patients with high-risk hematologic malignancies. Patients were randomized between the two arms of the study.
L1381: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1382: NDA 214759
L1383: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1384: The primary endpoint of the study was event-free survival at 2 years after transplantation. Events were defined as disease relapse, graft failure, or death.
L1385: Secondary endpoints included overall survival (OS), incidence of relapse (RI), non-relapse mortality (NRM), and transplant-related mortality (TRM) at 2 years after transplantation. Additional secondary objectives included CTCAE grade III+ mucositis between days -6 and +28, engraftment at day +28, donor chimerism at days +28 and +100, acute and chronic GvHD within 2 years of transplantation, and other CTCAE grade III/IV events between days -6 and +28.
L1386: ## Key Eligibility Criteria
L1387: The study population consisted of adult patients with AML or high risk MDS who were not eligible for myeloablative conditioning regimens. The study defined this group of patients as patients who were at least 50 years of age at the time of HSCT or with an HCT-CI score &gt; 2. Karnofsky performance score was at least 60%.
L1388: ## Treatment Plan Overview
L1389: Patients were randomized to receive either RIC with the control (reference) arm of busulfan + fludarabine, or the test arm of treosulfan + fludarabine (Table 8).
L1390: **Table 8. Treatment Overview of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II**
L1391: Treatment	Day -6	-5	-4	-3	-2	-1	0	+1	+3	+6
Treosulfan (IV, 10 g/m2 within 120 minutes) – test arm	X	X	X	
Busulfan (IV, 0.8 mg/kg within 120 minutes) - control	X	X	
Fludarabine (IV, 30 mg/m2 within 30 minutes)	X	X	X	X	X	
ATG-S-Fresenius / Grafolon (IV, 10 mg/kg in MUD) – Germany, Hungary, Italy, Poland	X	X

Cyclosporine-A (IV, PO, dose level adjusted)	X	X	X...	X...	X...
Methotrexate (IV, mg/m2/day)	15	10	10
Source Data: Clinical Protocol, adapted from Appendix 16-16.1.1
L1392: All patients received fludarabine (30 mg/m2/d) on days -6 to -2. Patients on the control arm received busulfan (0.8 mg/kg) on days -4 and -3. Patients receiving busulfan received anti-seizure prophylaxis. Patients on the treatment arm received treosulfan (10 mg/m2/d) on days -4 to -2. Additionally, patients who received a MUD received ATG. The type of ATG and dosing varied by country.
L1393: GVHD prophylaxis was identical in both arms. All patients received level-adapted cyclosporine from Day -1 through Day +100 and methotrexate on days +1, +3, and +6, with folic acid rescue.
L1394: ## Monitoring Schedule
L1395: Study subjects underwent screening within three weeks prior to the start of conditioning

L1398: Grafapex (treosulfan)

Of note, for patients who did not have signs of relapse, bone marrow studies following day +100 were not required.

L1402: ## Statistical Analysis Plan
L1403: The submitted SAP was version 7.0, dated Dec. 03, 2019.
L1404: ## Analysis Set
L1405: Full analysis set (FAS): all randomized patients who were treated with at least one dose of study medication and had at least one efficacy parameter documented after baseline. The patients within the FAS were to be analyzed with respect to the groups into which they were randomized (intention to treat principle). Sponsor's analyses were based on FAS.
L1406: ## Start point for Time-to-event Endpoints
L1407: Allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation.
L1408: **Stat Reviewer's Comment:** This FAS population used by Sponsor is not an ITT population. FDA re-did all analyses based on the ITT population and started from time of randomization.
L1409: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1410: NDA 214759
L1411: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1412: ## Sample Size Determination
L1413: The noninferiority margin is HR=1.3. No hazard rate of the control arm and accrual rate was specified in the SAP. According to Page 61 of the DMC Charter, at 1-sided \alpha=2.5\%, at most 481 events or 930 patients would be enrolled. The trial would have a power of 80%. Assuming that roughly 3% of patients have to be excluded from FAS, at most 960 patients would be enrolled.
L1414: **Clinical TL Comment:** In the document "Internal Report. Estimating the treatment effect of busulfan-based conditioning to justify the non-inferiority margin of the phase III study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II" (February 27, 2020) in Module 5.3.5.3, the Applicant provide several cases of published comparative analyses or randomized studies of HSCT vs chemotherapy for EFS, RFS, and OS (using various definitions of each) for multiple different populations of patients with AML or MDS. No particular publication was identified as having a population exchangeable with that in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II. This document does not provide justification for the NI margin prespecified in this trial.
L1415: **Stat Reviewer's Comment:** Under IND 054552, in the Meeting Minutes of Type B Pre-NDA Meeting held on Apr 02 2020, FDA requested that the report justifying the noninferiority margin of MC-FludT.14/L (Trial II) should be placed in the Module 5.3.5.4. The Sponsor clarified that the noninferiority margin was prespecified and cannot be changed. The Agency acknowledged the Sponsor's clarifications on the noninferiority margin and reiterated that the noninferiority margin use and interpretation in efficacy assessment would be a review issue.
L1416: In the NDA submission, no report justifying the noninferiority margin was submitted. However, because FDA could not independently verify the primary endpoint of EFS for Study MC-FludT.14/L II, review was focused on the OS analysis. Therefore, no IR was issued for EFS noninferiority related issues.
L1417: ## Analysis of Efficacy Endpoints
L1418: ## Primary:
L1419: Kaplan-Meier methods was used to calculate the 12-and 24-month EFS rate and the associated 95% CIs. Noninferiority was tested using a Cox proportional hazards regression model stratified by center with donor type (MUD vs. MRD), risk group (I vs. II) and treatment as factors. The three factors in the Cox model were stratification factors used in the randomization procedure.
L1420: **Stat Reviewer's Comment:** FDA recommends stratified log-rank test for testing superiority and Cox model stratified by stratification factors at randomization to estimate HR and 95% CI.
L1421: ## Secondary:
L1422: The Kaplan-Meier method was used to estimate survival probabilities and 95% CIs. The unstratified log-rank test was used to test for a difference between the two treatment arms.
L1423: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1424: NDA 214759
L1425: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1426: Cox models similar to the model used for primary endpoint analysis would be used to estimate the hazard ratio (HR) and 95% CIs.
L1427: ## Interim Analyses for Efficacy
L1428: Three interim analyses were pre-specified in the study protocol, which allowed to stop the trial as soon as non-inferiority has been demonstrated. The interim analyses were scheduled based on a combination of number of patients and number of events. O'Brien-Fleming boundary was used.
L1429: If significant non-inferiority could be shown at interim or final analysis, the study would stop enrollment and superiority with respect to the primary study endpoint would be sequentially tested.

L1456: ## Financial Disclosure
L1457: The financial disclosure form for MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was submitted with the application. No investigators with disclosable financial interests were identified.
L1458: ## Data Quality and Integrity
L1459: The quality and integrity of the submitted data were sufficient to review the application for the secondary endpoint of overall survival. Insufficient data were provided to independently verify the primary endpoint of event free survival at 2 years.
L1460: ## Patient Disposition
L1461: Five hundred seventy patients were randomized to busulfan or treosulfan arms (Figure 5). Following randomization, subjects in each arm did not receive study drug, primarily due to screening failures. In addition, two patients in the treatment arm were not evaluable for efficacy endpoints as they did not receive HSCT. The full analysis set is comprised of 283 patients in the busulfan arm and 268 patients in the treosulfan arm.
L1462: **Figure 5. Distribution of Patients in MC-FludT.L/14 Trial II**

L1464: ## Protocol Violations/Deviations
L1465: The clinical trial report contained reports of 490 of 570 patients with reported deviations (86% of total patients). Of these, 201 of 570 patients (35.3%) had a major protocol deviation. Slightly greater than 17% of patients had a procedural deviation, with missing evaluations at different timepoints. There were 32 patients (5.6%) with treatment deviations. Table 13 further characterizes the categories of major protocol deviations.

L1487: ## Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
L1488: Conditioning preparative regimens were administered at the research sites by qualified study personnel. Patients did not receive any experimental treatments within 4 weeks prior to the study. Concomitant medications for GvHD prophylaxis were prescribed in the trial; all other prophylactic and concomitant medications were recorded in the CRF.
L1489: ## Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L1490: The study was designed as an NI study with the option to test superiority at the final analysis. Enrollment was stopped at the second interim analysis. The final analysis was based on 48.6% information. Superiority of Treosulfan was not achieved at the second interim analysis but was at the last analysis, which included 570 patients and 234 events. While the trial showed treosulfan was superior to the control with respect to the primary endpoint at the final analysis, EFS [HR was 0.64, (0.49, 0.84)], there are reporting issues that cause FDA to question the usefulness of the result. The lack of 'Day +100 bone marrow' reports calls the EFS evaluation into question. FDA was not able to independently verify the primary endpoint of EFS. Therefore, efficacy review and analyses were focused on the overall survival, which was found to be supportive.
L1491: ## EFS
L1492: The first interim analysis happened at 40 EFS events, and DMC recommended to continue enrollment. One hundred sixty-four events happened at the second interim analysis. Non-inferiority was achieved with 476 patients, and enrollment was stopped. The final analysis included 168 events. Superiority of Treosulfan was not achieved at the second interim analysis but was at the last analysis, which included 570 patients and 234 events.
L1493: Table 16. Actual Stopping boundaries for efficacy and futility monitoring shows the actual stopping boundaries for efficacy and futility monitoring. Because noninferiority margin was achieved, the trial stopped at 48.6% information. As well studied in literature, results of early interim analyses tend to overestimate treatment efficacy. This early stop may result in insufficient follow-up.

L1505: ## OS
L1506: FDA uses a Cox model stratified by the stratification factors of randomization to calculate the hazard ratio (HR) and 95% confidence interval (CI). All analyses are conducted on the ITT population, which includes all randomized patients according to their assigned treatment at randomization. The starting point of OS is time of randomization, not time of transplant.
L1507: The median (range) of follow-up time was 29.5 months (0-54.8) months as determined by reverse Kaplan-Meier method. The HR is 0.65 (0.48, 0.88). Figure 6 shows the Kaplan-Meier Curve for OS in ITT population.

L1543: **Clinical TL Review Comment:** The endpoints based on hematopoietic recovery, chimerism and nonrelapse mortality are safety issues rather than efficacy issues. GRFS and CRFS represent a mixture of safety and efficacy data. The relapse/progression endpoint might be considered as supportive of efficacy, but the results of analyses using cumulative incidence with competing risks are difficult to interpret when the incidence of competing risks is so high. As such, none of the prespecified secondary endpoint provide information to address a determination of efficacy. Since the patients with MDS were transplanted when not in remission, achievement of remission might reflect effectiveness, but this was not a prespecified endpoint; however, results of marrow examinations which would allow for assessment of response were not collected systematically, so response cannot be assessed.
L1544: There were no efficacy-related patient-reported outcomes submitted for review.
L1545: ## Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
L1546: All FDA analyses were based on the ITT population unless specifically annotated.
L1547: ## Boundary Analysis for OS
L1548: There was no alpha-allocation plan for OS or any other secondary endpoints. Therefore, the
L1549: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1550: NDA 214759
L1551: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1552: significance of OS is hard to interpret. All p-values are nominal and cannot be included in labeling since there was no pre-specified plan to control multiplicity.
L1553: To facilitate decision making, we calculated O'Brien-Fleming (OBF) boundaries and Pocock boundaries using the information fraction of EFS. OBF assigns more weight toward later analysis, while Pocock boundary assigns alpha to each analysis equally. Therefore, OBF is more conservative compared to Pocock and recommended in analyses for primary and key secondary endpoints.
L1554: For comparison, in a hypothetical well-controlled trial with EFS as primary endpoint and OS as key secondary endpoint, the fixed sequence testing procedure at each prespecified interim analysis would be to test EFS first, if EFS achieved statistical significance according to the OBF boundary, then test OS at the same significance level. Because this trial was designed as a noninferiority test and used the margin of HR=1.3, which was more liberal than what was usually recommended by FDA, it stopped early at 48.6% information of EFS. Although not prespecified, information fraction of OS at the stopping point would be even smaller. We then applied the less strict Pocock boundary to compare with significance level from OBF.
L1555: From this boundary analysis, we wanted to see how significant the OS results would be assuming the same information fraction as EFS at the time points of interim analyses. Please note these boundaries are exploratory only and based on a hypothetical exercise.
L1556: We conducted a stratified log-rank test for OS at the last analysis. The p-value was 0.0049. At this point, the OBF boundary is 0.0025 and the Pocock boundary is 0.014. The OS results crossed the Pocock boundary, but not the OBF boundary. Therefore, there would have been different conclusions regarding statistical significance based on the different methods. As previously specified, the OBF boundary is more conservative and assigns more weight toward later analysis, while the Pocock boundary assigns weights equally to early and late analyses. These mixed results suggest that it is not clear if OS would have been considered to be significant; however, no definitive conclusions can be made based on the OS results because the multiplicity approach was not prespecified.
L1557: **Table 21. O'Brien Fleming and Pocock Boundaries for OS using Information Fraction of EFS**
L1558: Interim Look	Sample Size	# of Events	IF(%)	Pocock	OBF
Upper	Lower	Upper	Lower
1	221	40	8.3	0.003	0.003	<.0001	<.0001
2	460	164	34.1	0.009	0.009	0.0001	0.0001
Confirmatory	460	168	34.9	0.006	0.006	0.0001	0.0001
Last	551	234	48.6	0.007	0.007	0.0013	0.0013

L1563: ## Subgroup Analysis for OS
L1564: We conducted an exploratory subgroup OS analysis to investigate if there is any detrimental trend in the subgroups that may raise safety concerns. Table 22 summarizes the HR and 95% CI of OS in these interested subgroups by treatment arms. Figure 9 is a forest plot that demonstrating OS results in both the subgroups and the overall population. The same Cox model for OS analysis in the overall population was applied here. The overall HR is 0.65 (0.48, 0.88). In the subgroups, most HRs are less than 1 except for the low or very low risk MDS and the MRD donor type group. However, the sample size of these two groups are relatively small and the 95% CI of HRs cover 1. There was no CI that could indicate a clearly detrimental effect in any subgroup because all confidence intervals were fairly wide. This demonstrates the uncertainty in the hazard ratio estimates presented.

L1583: ## Analysis of Trial Conduct
L1584: To further understand the quality of OS data, we performed the following analyses:
L1585: ## - **Informative Censoring for OS**
L1586: In this analysis we reversed the censoring indicator for death, which means censoring is now counted as event and death as censoring. We then used Kaplan-Meier method to make a reversed censoring survival curve for the two treatment arms. If there is any informative censoring in one of the treatment arms, the survival curves will separate from each other. Figure 10 shows the reversed K-M curve for OS in the ITT population, which does not indicate that informative censoring is present.

L1607: ## 8.1.2 Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial I
L1608: *"Clinical Phase III Trial To Compare Treosulfan-Based Conditioning Therapy With Busulfan-Based Reduced-Intensity Conditioning (RIC) Prior To Allogeneic Haematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation In Patients With AML Or MDS Considered Ineligible To Standard Conditioning Regimens - Trial I"*
L1609: ## INVESTIGATIONAL PLAN
L1610: ## Trial Design
L1611: ## Design and Objectives
L1612: The study was designed as a non-inferiority Phase III trial to compare treosulfan-based conditioning therapy to busulfan-based reduced-intensity conditioning therapy in adult patients with high-risk hematologic malignancies. Patients were randomized between the two arms of the study.
L1613: The primary endpoint of the study was event-free survival at 2 years after transplantation. Events were defined as disease relapse, graft failure, or death.
L1614: Secondary endpoints included overall survival (OS), incidence of relapse (RI), non-relapse mortality (NRM), and transplant-related mortality (TRM) at 2 years after transplantation. Additional secondary objectives included CTCAE grade III+ mucositis between days -6 and +28, engraftment at day +28, donor chimerism at days +28 and +100, acute and chronic GvHD within 2 years of transplantation, and other CTCAE grade III/IV events between days -6 and +28.
L1615: ## Key Eligibility Criteria
L1616: The study population consisted of adult patients with AML or high risk MDS who were not eligible for myeloablative conditioning regimens. The study defined this group of patients as patients who were at least 50 years of age at the time of HSCT or with an HCT-CI score &gt; 2. Karnofsky performance score was at least 60%.
L1617: ## Treatment Plan Overview
L1618: Patients were randomized to receive either RIC with the control (reference) arm of busulfan + fludarabine, or the test arm of treosulfan + fludarabine (Table 26). All patients received fludarabine (30 mg/m2/d) on days -6 to -2. Patients on the control arm received busulfan (0.8 mg/kg) on days -4 and -3. Patients receiving busulfan received anti-seizure prophylaxis. Patients on the treatment arm received treosulfan (10 mg/m2/d) on days -6 to -4. Additionally, patients who received a MUD received ATG. The type of ATG and dosing varied by country.
L1619: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1620: NDA 214759
L1621: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1622: **Table 26. Treatment Overview of MC-FludT.14/L Trial I**
L1623: Treatment	Day -6	-5	-4	-3	-2	-1	0	+1	+3	+6

L1652: ## Patient Disposition
L1653: Three hundred thirty patients were randomized to busulfan or treosulfan arms (Figure 13). Following randomization, subjects in each arm did not receive study drug. Following treatment administration 1 patient in the control arm and two patients in the treatment arm were not evaluable for efficacy endpoints. The evaluable patient set is comprised of 112 patients in the busulfan arm and 112 patients in the treosulfan arm.

L1659: ## Protocol Violations/Deviations
L1660: The clinical study report contained reports of 236 of 330 patients with reported protocol deviations (72% of total patients) (Table 30). Of these, 120 (75.5%) were treated with busulfan and 116 (67.8%) were treated with treosulfan. Of note, there were more deviations regarding administration of the investigational drug product in patients receiving busulfan (12.6%) than in patients receiving treosulfan (3.5%). Six patients did not receive any busulfan, and one patient did not receive any treosulfan.

L1678: ## Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
L1679: Conditioning preparative regimens were administered at the research sites by qualified study personnel. Patients did not receive any experimental treatments within 4 weeks prior to the study. Concomitant medications for GvHD prophylaxis were prescribed in the trial; all other prophylactic and concomitant medications were recorded in the CRF.
L1680: ## Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L1681: The trial discontinued after planned interim analysis. It was determined that dose should be lower in this elderly population because neutropenic phase was much longer than control. A higher mortality was also found in the Treosulfan group. As early as 28 days after transplantation, 7 patients (4.2%) of the Treosulfan treatment group, and none of the patients of the Busulfan treatment group had died.
L1682: As stated in the clinical report: "Consistently with the longer duration of neutropenia in patients receiving Treosulfan, a higher frequency of patients with CTCAE Grade III/IV infections was observed in the Treosulfan treatment group (54.8% vs. 36.2%). This observation is also reflected in the higher frequency of SAEs reported for the Treosulfan treatment group (11.3% vs. 5.9%), most notably infections (6.5% vs. 2.0%). Likewise, the cumulative incidence of infection related TRM on Day +100 was higher in the Treosulfan treatment group (7.9%) compared to the Busulfan treatment group (4.0%, one-sided p=0.0630, adjusted for donor type, risk group and centre using Cox regression model).
L1683: The Treosulfan group had a higher cumulative incidence of NRM and a significantly higher cumulative incidence of TRM on Day +100 for patients receiving Treosulfan (10.2%) compared to patients receiving Busulfan (5.3%, p=0.077)."
L1684: ## Efficacy Results – Secondary and Other Relevant Endpoints
L1685: Not applicable.

L1689: ## Subpopulations
