L1703: ## 8.1.3 Registry Studies
L1704: ## 8.1.3.1 Retrospective EBMT-PD/IE-WP analysis:
L1705: *Re-analysis of EBMT-registry data on Fludarabine/Melphalan and Busulfan/Cyclophosphamide based conditioning treatment compared to Fludarabine/Treosulfan based conditioning of adult AML and MDS patients treated in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II phase III by matched pairs.*
L1706: Key study objectives were comparison of OS, relapse (RI), and NRM, all at 2 years after allo-HSCT. Comparison patients (N = 1493) received conditioning with either Flu-Mel or Bu/Cy without TBI. The MC-FludT.14/L Trial II cohort consisted of 252 patients, with patients &lt; 50 years of age excluded from the analysis. The EBMT patient cohort had a lower HCT-CI score, higher Karnofsky score, lower recipient age, and higher donor age than the MC-FludT.14/L Trial II cohort. Four matched comparisons were performed as follows:
L1707: - MDS: Flu/Mel vs Flu/Treo
- MDS: Bu/Cy vs Flu/Treo
- AML: Flu/Mel vs Flu/Treo
- AML: Bu/Cy vs Flu/Treo
L1708: In MDS, Flu/Treo was statistically superior to Bu/Cy in OS. In MDS, no other comparisons were statistically significant. In AML, Flu/Treo was statistically superior to Flu/Mel and Bu/Cy in OS and NRM.
L1709: Multivariable sensitivity analyses were also performed. In MDS, Flu/Treo again was statistically superior to Bu/Cy, with no other analyses reaching significance. In AML, Flu/Treo was statistically superior to Flu/Mel and Bu/Cy in NRM and OS. Relapse incidence was comparable among groups.
L1710: *Stat Reviewer's Comment: Patient level data were not submitted. Therefore, FDA could not independently verify the results.*
L1711: ## 8.1.3.2 CIBMTR SC17-04b
L1712: *Comparison of allogeneic transplantation outcomes for acute myeloid leukemia and myelodysplastic syndrome with full or less intensity busulfan-fludarabine regimens and busulfan-cyclophosphamide regimen in the United States to a treosulfan-containing regimen in a medac-sponsored European clinical trial MC-FludT.14/L (Trial II).*
L1713: The treosulfan “trial” population is identical to the population above in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (N = 268). The comparison population included patients from 96 transplant centers. Conditioning regimens for this analysis included the following:
L1714: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1715: NDA 214759
L1716: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1717: ## RIC:
L1718: - Busulfan + fludarabine (Flu/Bu2) (N = 318)
- Flu/Bu + ATG (N = 199)
L1719: ## MAC
L1720: - Busulfan + fludarabine (Flu/Bu4) (N = 327)
- Flu/B4 + ATG (N = 169)
- Busulfan + cyclophosphamide (Cy/Bu4) (N = 317)
L1721: The primary endpoint for the analysis was 2-year OS. Secondary endpoints included 2-year DFS, 2-year EFS, relapse, TRM/NRM, and incidence of aGVHD and cGVHD. There were no differences in HCT-Cl scores across the groups. Cy/Bu4 was more likely to be given to younger patients than the other regimens. OS, DFS, and EFS were higher for patients in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II than those in the CIBMTR database. This is likely due to higher relapse rates with the RIC and Flu/B4 + ATG regimens and higher TRM with the other MAC regimens. aGVHD did not differ between the groups. cGVHD was higher in the trial regimen when compared to the RIC and ATG-containing regimens but was comparable to the rate observed in the Cy/Bu4 population.
L1722: *Stat Reviewer's Comment: Patient level data were not submitted. Therefore, FDA could not independently verify the results.*
L1723: ## 8.1.4 Additional Studies in AML or MDS
L1724: ## MC-FludT.6/L
L1725: ## *Allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation in patients with advanced hematological malignancies after treosulfan-based conditioning therapy - A clinical phase II study*
L1726: Study MC-FludT.6/L was a multicenter single-arm dose-escalation trial to test the tolerability of treosulfan and fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT from an HLA-matched sibling, 1 antigen-mismatched sibling, or HLA-matched unrelated donor. Eligible patients were &gt;= 18 years old with a "chemosensitive" hematological malignancy indicated for HSCT but who were considered at high risk for toxicities with standard MAC regimens. Patients were treated in sequential cohorts (n=15 each) at treosulfan dose levels of 10 gm/m2, 12 gm/m2, or 14 gm/m2 (see Table 12 for details of the regimen). The aim of the protocol was to determine a dose and schedule for later trials. The primary efficacy endpoint was hematopoietic recovery. Overall survival was an additional endpoint.
L1727: There were 55 patients accrued, including 19 with AML and 6 with MDS among the various dose cohorts. The median age was 50 years (range, 18 - 66 years); 60% were male and 40% were female. Table 34 shows the selected efficacy outcomes by dose as reported by the Applicant.
L1728: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1729: NDA 214759
L1730: Grafapex (treosulfan)

L1735: The Applicant concluded that efficacy and safety of the combination of treosulfan and fludarabine were comparable at all 3 treosulfan dose levels.
L1736: ## *MC-FludT.7/AML*
L1737: ## *Clinical phase II trial to evaluate the safety and efficacy of treosulfan based conditioning prior to allogeneic haematopoietic stem cell transplantation in patients with acute myeloid leukaemia*
L1738: Study MC-FludT.7/AML was a multicenter single-arm trial to test treosulfan and fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT from an HLA-matched sibling or HLA-matched unrelated donor. Eligible patients were 18-60 years old with AML indicated for HSCT. The preparative regimen consisted of treosulfan at a dose level of 14 gm/m2 in combination with fludarabine (see Table 7 for details of the regimen). The primary objective was "evaluation of engraftment". Overall survival was an additional endpoint. The target accrual was 75 patients. The sample size was calculated to allow for at least 65 patients eligible to assess the safety outcomes in comparison to historical data using busulfan. Seventy-five patients were enrolled. The median age was 45 years (range, 19 - 59 years); 51% were male and 49% were female. At study entry, 80% were in first CR and the remainder in later CR. The Applicant reported that neutrophil recovery at Day 28 was achieved by 93%, complete chimerism at Day 100 was achieved by 92%, Day-100 nonrelapse mortality was 3%, and overall survival at 1 year was 77% (Study MC-FludT.7/AML Clinical Study Report Tables 11.4.1.1A, 11.4.1.2A, 14.2.4A, and 14.2.6A). The Applicant concluded that the regimen "has myeloablative potential as demonstrated by the fast and durable eradication of the physiologic recipient haematopoiesis. Long-term outcome data are in good accordance with data from the literature on AML transplantation after standard or myeloablative conditioning." (Study MC-FludT.7/AML Clinical Study Report Section 13).
L1739: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1740: NDA 214759
L1741: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1742: ## *MC-FludT.8/MDS*
L1743: ## *Clinical phase II trial to evaluate the safety and efficacy of treosulfan based conditioning prior to allogeneic haematopoietic stem cell transplantation in patients with myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS)*
L1744: Study MC-FludT.8/MDS was a multicenter single-arm trial to test treosulfan and fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT from an HLA-matched sibling or HLA-matched unrelated donor. Eligible patients were 18-60 years old with MDS indicated for HSCT. The preparative regimen consisted of treosulfan at a dose level of 14 gm/m2 in combination with fludarabine (see Table 7 for details of the regimen). The primary objective was "evaluation of engraftment". Overall survival was an additional endpoint. The target accrual was 45 patients. The sample size was calculated to allow for assessment of safety outcomes in comparison to historical data using busulfan. Forty-six patients were enrolled; one patient was subsequently diagnosed as AML and excluded from the outcome analyses. The median age was 50 years (range, 22 - 63 years); 47% were male and 53% were female. At study entry, 47% had excess blasts. The Applicant reported that neutrophil recovery at Day 28 was achieved by 96%, complete chimerism at Day 100 was achieved by 93%, Day-100 nonrelapse mortality was 9%, and overall survival at 1 year was 82% (Study MC-FludT.8/MDS Clinical Study Report Tables 11.4.1.1A, 11.4.1.2A, 14.2.4A, and 14.2.6A). A response assessment using the standard response criteria was not provided. The Applicant concluded that the regimen "has myeloablative potential as demonstrated by the fast and durable eradication of the physiologic recipient haematopoiesis. Long-term outcome data seemed to be in favour of this new regimen when compared with data from the literature." (Study MC-FludT.8/MDS Clinical Study Report Section 13).
L1745: ## *MC-FludT.17/M*
L1746: ## *Clinical Phase 2 trial to describe the safety and efficacy of Treosulfan-based conditioning therapy prior to allogeneic haematopoietic stem cell transplantation in paediatric patients with haematological malignancies*
L1747: Study MC-FludT.17/M was a multicenter single-arm trial to test treosulfan and fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT from an HLA-matched or 1-allele mismatched related or unrelated donors. Eligible patients were 28 days to &lt; 18 years old with ALL, AML, MDS or JMML indicated for HSCT. The preparative regimen consisted of BSA-adapted treosulfan dosing equivalent to exposure from 14 gm/m2 in adults in combination with fludarabine and thiotepra (see Table 7 for details of the regimen). The primary objective was to estimate freedom from transplant-related mortality at Day 100. Overall survival was an additional endpoint. The target accrual was 70 patients. The sample size was calculated to allow a descriptive analysis of hematopoietic recovery, toxicity and PK parameters as part of an agreed PIP. Seventy patients were included in the analyses. The median age was 9.5 years (range, &lt; 1 - 17 years); 63% were
L1748: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1749: NDA 214759
L1750: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1751: (b) (4)
L1752: male and 37% were female. There were 29 patients with AML and 10 with MDS.
L1753: (Study MC-FludT.17/M Clinical Study Report Tables 14.2.1A and 14.2.3A). The Applicant concluded that this regimen "can successfully be used as conditioning treatment before allo-HSCT in the selected paediatric population with malignant haematological diseases." (Study MC-FludT.17/M Clinical Study Report Section 13).
L1754: ## 8.2 Integrated Review of Effectiveness
L1755: ## 8.2.1 Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
L1756: ## Methods
L1757: The Applicant proposed an indication for treosulfan "as part of conditioning treatment prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT) in adult patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS)." The proposed dose of treosulfan was of 10 g/m2/dose for use in the combination described.
L1758: (b) (4)
L1759: The clinical development program included two randomized control trials, three single-arm trials and two registry studies (Table 35). All clinical trials were conducted outside the US.
L1760: **Table 35. Clinical Studies Submitted to Demonstrate the Effectiveness of Treosulfan in Combination with Fludarabine as a Preparative Regimen for Patients with AML or MDS**
L1761: Trial	Design	Population	Treosulfan Dose	Contribution
MC-FludT-14/L Trial II	Randomized Phase 3	Adults*- AML, MDS	10 g/m2/dose	Pivotal Trial
MC-FludT-14/L Trial I	Randomized Phase 3	Adults*- AML, MDS	14 g/m2/dose	Pivotal Trial
MC-FludT.7/AML	Single-arm Phase 2	Adults- AML	14 g/m2/dose	Dose selection
MC-FludT.8/MDS	Single-arm Phase 2	Adults- MDS	14 g/m2/dose	Dose selection
MC-FludT.17/M	Single-arm Phase 2	Children - Heme cancers	14 g/m2/dose	Dose selection

L1764: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1765: NDA 214759
L1766: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1767: There were several issues identified with the elements of the clinical development program:
L1768: - Patient level data were not submitted for the registry studies. Therefore, FDA cannot independently verify the results. As such, the registry studies do not contribute to the determination of the effectiveness of treosulfan.
- EFS and OS are the acceptable endpoints for approval of a drug for use in a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT for patients with AML or MDS. Since Studies MC-FludT.7/AML and MC-FludT.8/MDS are single-arm trials, they were not designed adequately to assess the time-to-event endpoints of EFS and OS. As such, Studies MC-FludT.7/AML and MC-FludT.8/MDS do not contribute to the determination of the effectiveness of treosulfan.
- Similarly, the single-arm trial Study MC-FludT.17/M is not designed adequately to assess EFS or OS and would not support an indication in the pediatric patients with AML or MDS.
- Studies MC-FludT.14/L Trial I (Trial 14 - I; treosulfan 14 g/m2/dose) was failed trial. The study was stopped early at the first interim analysis which reported an EFS HR=1.23 (95% CI 0.84, 1.80) and an OS HR=1.30 (0.85, 2.00) in the Full Analysis Set. The trial was stopped early because of a safety concern.
L1769: The Applicant proposed to use Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (Trial 14 - II; treosulfan 10 g/m2/dose) to support the proposed indication. Trial 14 - II was a multicenter, randomized, active-controlled trial comparing treosulfan to busulfan in combination with fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT. Eligible patients included adults 18 to 70 years old with AML or MDS, Karnofsky performance status &gt; 60%, and age ≥ 50 years or hematopoietic cell transplantation comorbidity index [HCT CI] score &gt; 2. The patients were randomized to receive treosulfan 10 g/m2 daily on days -4, -3 and -2 or to busulfan 0.8 mg/kg every 6 hours on days -4 and -3 in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2 daily on days -6, -5, -4, -3 and -2, and hematopoietic stem cell transplantation on day 0. Antithymocyte globulin was administered as well to patients with unrelated donors.
L1770: In general, a randomized controlled trial with a survival-based endpoint is appropriate to support an indication for a drug in a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT. FDA frequently requires two trials to determine efficacy, but one trial might be sufficient if the trial is adequately-designed and well-conducted, and the results are internally consistent, clinically meaningful, and statistically very persuasive. Potential issues with the design of Trial 14 - II in particular included:
L1771: - Trial 14 - II was designed as a noninferiority trial with a primary endpoint of EFS within 2 years of follow-up. FDA was not involved in the review of this protocol prior to conduct of the study, and there was no pre-existing agreement on an acceptable noninferiority margin in this setting.
L1772: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1773: NDA 214759
L1774: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1775: - For Trial 14 - II, EFS was defined as the interval from Transplant Day 0 to relapse, graft failure or death (whatever occurs first); patients alive without an event were censored at 2 years from HSCT.For patients with AML transplanted in remission, relapse-free survival (RFS) would be the usual endpoint to assess efficacy.
- For patients with MDS transplanted not in remission, EFS might be an acceptable endpoint if defined to reflect the need to achieve remission.
- OS was a secondary endpoint of Trial 14 - II. The analysis of OS was planned as exploratory; the timing of the OS analysis was not event-driven, and there was no prespecified alpha-spending plan to correct for multiplicity.
- In Trial 14 - II, the efficacy analyses were planned to be conducted in the subgroup of patients who were randomized, received at least one dose of study drug, and had at least one efficacy assessment postbaseline (Full Analysis Set; FAS) and in a subgroup of the FAS without selected protocol deviations (Per Protocol Set; PPS). However, for a randomized trial, the ITT population is the preferred analysis set.
- Trial 14 - II compared fludarabine in combination with 3 days of treatment with treosulfan to a combination with 2 days of busulfan. Any conclusions regarding superiority would need to take into account the difference between arms in the number of days of treatment with treosulfan vs the number of days of treatment with busulfan.
- AML and MDS are different diseases, so any trial used to support both indications would need to test for efficacy in each individually. Subgroup analyses of efficacy by diagnosis were prespecified in Trial 14 - II.
- Due to differences in minor and other unmeasured major antigens, HLA-matched unrelated donors have a higher degree of histoincompatibility with the recipient than HLA-identical sibling donors, so the minimum intensity of the preparative regimen needed for successful HSCT may differ by donor type. Any trial used to support an indication using both related and unrelated donors would need to test for efficacy by each individually. Subgroup analyses of efficacy by donor type were prespecified in Trial 14 - II.
- Additionally, in Trial 14 - II, patients with unrelated donors also received ATG in the preparative regimen. ATG dosing varied by geographic region. Any conclusions related to the subgroup analysis by donor would need to take into consideration the differences in treatment regimens by donor type.
L1776: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1777: NDA 214759
L1778: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1779: ## Primary Endpoint
L1780: ## Event-Free Survival
L1781: For Trial 14 - II, the Applicant's last analysis of EFS for submission was the last analysis at 234 events (total 48.6% information level). At the prespecified second interim analysis (164 events, 34.1% information level), the Data Monitoring Committee (DMC) recommended to stop recruitment because non-inferiority was achieved. Recruitment continued until interim analysis results became available for the DMC. Therefore, the last analysis had more events than the second interim analysis. The Applicant provided results for the full follow-up period with median follow-up of 29.7 months in the treosulfan arm and 29.4 months in the busulfan arm. The HR for EFS was 0.64 (0.48, 0.87) in the PPS and 0.64 (0.48, 0.87) in the FAS. The Applicant concluded that the results supported noninferiority, and in sequential testing, superiority of treosulfan.
L1782: However, the Applicant did not submit all CBC and bone marrow results, so FDA was unable to verify the primary endpoint independently and could not confirm that this was a positive trial. Although, in general, FDA does not accept clinical trials for which the primary endpoint cannot be confirmed, for this submission the Division Director indicated that the OS endpoint would be an acceptable alternative for the assessment of efficacy.1
L1783: ## Secondary and Other Endpoints
L1784: ## Overall Survival
L1785: For Trial 14 - II, the analysis of OS was considered exploratory. FDA assessed OS in the ITT population with using the full follow-up period. Table 36 shows the results of FDA's analyses of OS with a median follow-up of 29.5 months (range, 0 - 54.8 months). No p-value is shown, as this analysis was exploratory.

L1796: The OS HR and 95% CI for the patients with MDS failed to demonstrate superiority for treosulfan, and the lack of superiority is supported by the Applicant's analysis in the FAS (HR 0.63; 0.37; 1.10).
L1797: ## Additional Efficacy Endpoints
L1798: The Applicant prespecified several secondary endpoints for Trial 14 - II, described in 8.1.1, but most of these were safety endpoints and none was suitable to use in support of the OS demonstrating effectiveness for treatment of AML or MDS.
L1799: For treatment of **AML in remission**, RFS would be a suitable endpoint to support a finding of superiority in OS. Because CBC and marrow examination data were not collected systematically in Trial 14 - II, RFS cannot be assessed, and there are no secondary endpoints to corroborate the OS outcome for the patients with AML.
L1800: For treatment of **MDS not in remission**, EFS (defined suitably) or CR that is durable might be suitable to assess activity. Because CBC and marrow examination data were not collected systematically in this protocol, EFS and CR cannot be assessed, and there are no secondary endpoints to corroborate an efficacy outcome for the patients with MDS.
L1801: ## Subpopulations
L1802: Exploratory subpopulations prespecified for analysis of OS in Trial 14 - II included donor type, risk group, combination of donor type and risk group, disease (AML and MDS), age group (&lt; 50 years vs. \ge 50 years), HCT-CI Score (\le 2 vs. &gt; 2), remission status in AML (CR1 vs. &gt; CR1), disease status at study entry in MDS (untreated vs. treated), risk group within AML patients, and risk group within MDS patients. Race and ethnicity were not included in the subpopulation analyses as data on race and ethnicity were not collected. This deficiency leaves some uncertainty about the generalizability of the efficacy outcomes with respect to the US population.
L1803: Table 22 in Section 8.1.1 shows the results of the FDA subpopulation analysis of OS for Trial 14 - II. In general, where the subgroups were large enough for credible analyses, the results demonstrated consistency in the subpopulations with the result in the ITT population overall. The exception was the subpopulation by donor type. Table 37 shows the results of FDA's analysis of OS by donor for the ITT population and in patients with AML or with MDS separately.

L1805: NDA 214759
L1806: Grafapex (treosulfan)

L1811: The results in Table 37 show that the treatment effect appears to be driven by the subpopulation of patients with unrelated donors (HR 0.56), and the treatment effect appears to be consistent with patients with unrelated donors whether they have AML (HR 0.51) or MDS (HR 0.65). For the patients with related donors, the treatment effect is much smaller or negative than for those with related donors, and the HR is inconsistent across the disease subgroups, although the number of patients with MDS and related donors is small. Of note, as shown in Appendix Figure 16.5.1, within a disease population, the busulfan arms appear to have similar OS (e.g., 16.5.1 A vs B red lines for patients with AML), but for either disease, the OS appears to be worse with treosulfan for patients with related donors in comparison to those with unrelated donors. The biological basis for this observed difference is unclear; it may be related to the use of ATG that was limited to those with unrelated donors, or to some unmeasured genetic element in the older related donors. Additional information is needed to assess this potential lack of effectiveness.
L1812: ## Dose/Dose Response
L1813: The Applicant reported that a) for autologous HSCT, the single-dose MTD of treosulfan was 47 gm/m2, and b) in an investigator-initiated pilot study of treosulfan 10 g/m2/day x 3 in combination with fludarabine was tolerable as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT and produced a high rate of complete donor chimerism (Study MC-FludT.6/L Clinical Study Report Section 9.4.4). For Study MC-FludT.6/L, the dose-escalation trial for development of the proposed preparative regimen, the Applicant studied treosulfan doses 10, 12 and 14 g/m2/day x 3 in combination with fludarabine. As discussed in Section 8.1.4, hematopoietic recovery and high-grade donor chimerism were achieved at all dose levels, and the 1-year OS was worst in the 14 g/m2/day x 3 cohort. Nonetheless, the Applicant chose the 14 g/m2/day x 3 regimen for the initial pivotal trial, MC-FludT.14/L Trial I (Trial 14 - I).
L1814: As discussed in Sections 8.1.2 and 8.3.5, Trial 14 - I was terminated early due to delayed hematopoietic recovery and increase early treatment-related mortality. FDA's analysis of the final data cut (1-year follow-up) showed no evidence of a treatment effect for the overall population or when assessed individually for patients with AML or with MDS. The second pivotal trial, Trial 14 - II, commenced using the lower treosulfan dose of 10 g/m2/day x 3.

L1823: ## Additional Efficacy Considerations
L1824: ## Extrapolation to Pediatric Patients
L1825: MC-FludT.16/NM was a phase 2 study for nonmalignant diseases that randomized patients to a 14 g/m2/dose equivalent of treosulfan or standard busulfan. Because this was not performed in a disease that is being considered for an indication for treosulfan, it was not evaluated for efficacy. The second study, MC-FludT.17/M, was a nonrandomized study for pediatric patients with malignant diseases; all patients received a 14 g/m2/dose equivalent of treosulfan.
L1826: ## 8.2.2 Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
L1827: In the absence of verification of the primary endpoint, and without prespecification of Type-1 error for multiple comparisons, Study 14-II does not provide considerable evidence of effectiveness.
L1828: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1829: NDA 214759
L1830: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1831: ## 8.3 Review of Safety
L1832: ## 8.3.1 Safety Review Approach
L1833: The FDA reviewed the safety data for 1,206 patients in clinical trials (Table 39). These included 728 patients treated with treosulfan and 478 treated with busulfan (active control). FDA utilized the following cohorts for this safety review:
L1834: - Cohort 1: The 270 patients treated with treosulfan in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II were used to assess the safety profile of treosulfan in the intended population, as compared to the standard RIC of busulfan + fludarabine.
- Cohort 2: The 168 patients treated with treosulfan in MC-FludT.14/L Trial I were used to assess potential safety signals at the treosulfan dose of 14 g/m2, as compared to the standard RIC of busulfan + fludarabine.
- Cohort 3: The 290 patients treated with treosulfan at 10 g/m2 (MC-FludT.6/L and MC-FludT.14/L Trial II) and the 305 patients treated with treosulfan at 14 g/m2 (MC-FludT.6/L, MC-FludT.7/AML, MC-FludT.8/MDS, and MC-FludT.14/L Trial I) were compared to assess differences in dose-related toxicity.
- Cohort 4: The 158 pediatric patients (treosulfan, N = 115; busulfan, N = 43) enrolled in MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M were assessed for safety signals within the pediatric population. The 12 pediatric patients who received treosulfan + fludarabine (without thiopeta) were compared to the 10 g/m2 population from Cohort 3.
L1835: All analyses were performed using the ISS datasets (12/11/2020, SDN 0017).

L1838: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1839: NDA 214759
L1840: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1841: Treosulfan is an alkylating agent, and it is related to busulfan. The common toxicities for busulfan in the setting of allo-HSCT are myelosuppression, hepatitis veno-occlusive disease (VOD), nausea, vomiting, stomatitis, diarrhea, fever, abdominal pain, anxiety, anorexia, insomnia, and electrolyte abnormalities. Of note, busulfan has an increased risk for seizures, which has not been identified for treosulfan.
L1842: As treosulfan is closely related to busulfan, and is thought to act as an alkylating agent, the known safety profile for busulfan was vital for this analysis.
L1843: Animal toxicity studies revealed that the target organ of treosulfan is the bone marrow, with leukopenia, neutropenia, reticulocytopenia, thrombocytopenia, and lymphopenia observed in the repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats, dogs, and monkeys. There was no evidence of dose-limiting functional or structural changes to the liver, kidneys, heart, lungs, or central nervous system.
L1844: The main safety signal that arose during the development program occurred during MC-FludT.14/L Trial I (treosulfan dose 14 g/m2). Because of higher than anticipated early morbidity and mortality in the treosulfan arm of the study, the study was paused. An amendment was submitted, and the protocol reopened with a lower dose of treosulfan (MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, treosulfan dose 10 g/m2).

L1866: ## Adequacy of the Safety Database
L1867: The safety database was assessed for adequacy with the following conclusions:
L1868: - Because data on race and ethnicity were not collected, the safety database was not adequate to extrapolate to the demographics of the United States population.
- There were sufficient patient numbers to assess for rare adverse reactions.
- There were not sufficient patients in the “≥75 years of age” age group to assess safety signals in that population.
- There were sufficient patients to evaluate differences by sex.
- There were sufficient patients treated at the 10 and 14 g/m2 dose levels to assess for a dose-toxicity relationship.
L1869: ## 8.3.3 Adequacy of Applicant's Clinical Safety Assessments
L1870: ## Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
L1871: The doses of concomitant medications were not provided in cm.xpt or adcm.xpt. Table 43 shows the duration of follow-up for mortality, SAE reporting, TEAE reporting, and laboratory testing for each of the studies included in the ISS. MC-FludT.6/L, which was the dose finding Phase 2 study, had the longest follow-up for SAE, TEAE, and safety labs. Additionally, Cohort 4 had longer SAE and SAR follow-up than the other studies included in Cohorts 1, 2, and 3.
L1872: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L1873: NDA 214759

L1877: ## Categorization of Adverse Events
L1878: Adverse events were reported down to the verbatim term. The adverse events were coded using MedDRA version 20.0 for all studies and for the pooled safety population. Where indicated in the tables or text, some adverse events are presented as grouped terms (Table 44). Regimen-related toxicity (RRT) is defined as directly caused by the conditioning regimen. Bearman's RRT criteria are used unless otherwise specified. Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) included events that started from the start of study drug administration through HSCT day +30, unless otherwise indicated.

L1884: ## Routine Clinical Tests
L1885: See 8.1.1 and 8.1.2 for descriptions of the frequency of clinical assessments for studies MC-FludT.14/L Trials I and II. The frequency of clinical assessments is adequate to assess the risks of serious safety signals identified by HSCT day +30. There are late RRTs associated with HSCT that occur rarely but can be serious; other than graft versus host disease (GVHD), these were not captured reliably in the pivotal study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (Cohort 1).

L1894: Deaths from Cohort 1 were assessed and causes of death were adjudicated (Table 46). In MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, there were 4 deaths (1.5%) from fatal adverse reactions due to treosulfan by Day +30; there were two additional deaths that were adjudicated as transplant-related mortality. In the busulfan arm, there were two deaths prior to Day +30, both of which were adjudicated as adverse reactions due to busulfan. In the treosulfan arm, there were 17 deaths (6%) by transplant day +100, compared to 19 deaths (7%) by transplant day + 100 in the busulfan arm. However, we note that for six patients, we did not have sufficient information to adjudicate the cause of death.

L1902: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L1903: Deaths in the treosulfan arm from Table 46 that are due to regimen-related toxicity that began prior to Day +30 after HSCT are detailed below.
L1904: Subject (b)(6) was a 63-year-old woman with AML in CR1 (2% bone marrow blasts), with a past medical history significant for acute renal impairment (resolved), hypertension, and diabetes. The subject received treosulfan + fludarabine as conditioning for allo-HSCT and received a PBSC as prescribed by the protocol with a matched related donor. On Day + 11, the subject is reported to have developed a lung infection, based on physical symptoms of pneumonia. On Day +12, the subject developed hemolysis and bronchopulmonary hemorrhage. The subject died on Day +13. The lung infection and bronchopulmonary hemorrhage were grade 5. The Applicant considered lung infection and bronchopulmonary hemorrhage as possibly related to treosulfan.
L1905: *Reviewer's Comment: The FDA adjudicated the cause of death to be AR RRT – pulmonary based on the time to event analysis and the narrative provided by the Applicant, which did not provide a microbial etiology.*
L1906: Subject (b)(6) was a 56-year-old man with MDS who received a conditioning regimen with treosulfan + fludarabine and a PBSC allo-HSCT from a matched unrelated donor. On Day +1, the subject developed grade 3 lung infection, with symptoms of dyspnea, fever, CRP elevation, and respiratory insufficiency. This was further specified as pneumonia and progressed to grade 4. On Day +7, the subject developed grade 2 febrile neutropenia. On Day +9, the patient developed grade 5 sepsis, with symptoms of hypotension, acidosis, kidney failure, and respiratory insufficiency. The subject died on Day +14. The Applicant assessed the cause of death as sepsis with lung infection as life-threatening. Both were considered by the Applicant as possibly related to treosulfan.
L1907: *Reviewer's Comment: The FDA adjudicated the cause of death to be AR RRT – pulmonary based on the narrative provided by the Applicant. No microbial etiology was provided.*
L1908: Subject (b)(6) was a 66-year-old man with AML who received a conditioning regimen with treosulfan + fludarabine and a PBSC allo-HSCT from a matched related donor. On Day +11, the patient experienced grade 4 lung infection, which worsened on Day +19 with progression to life-threatening respiratory fatigue. On Day +22, the patient experienced grade 5 sepsis and grade 5 kidney failure. The Applicant considered lung infection and sepsis as unlikely to be related to treosulfan.
L1909: *Reviewer's Comment: The FDA adjudicated the cause of death to be AR RRT – pulmonary. This is based on the time to event and the narrative provided by the Applicant, which did not provide a microbial etiology.*

L1916: ## Serious Adverse Events
L1917: In MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, 23 patients (8.5%) who received treosulfan had at least one serious adverse event (SAE), compared to 20 patients (7.1%) who received busulfan. The most common SAE was infection, with thirteen patients on the treosulfan arm (4.8%) and eight patients on the busulfan arm (2.8%) experiencing this SAE by D+30 (Table 47).

L1937: When evaluating TEAEs by Preferred Term (PT), the most common TEAEs were those expected after allo-HSCT (Table 50). The most common adverse reactions (\ge 10\%) on the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II included musculoskeletal pain, stomatitis, pyrexia, nausea, edema, infection, vomiting, rash, diarrhea, headache, febrile neutropenia, abdominal pain, hypertension, hemorrhage, fatigue, constipation, tachycardia, and hepatotoxicity. The most common grade 3-5 ARs (\ge 10\%) on the treosulfan arm were febrile neutropenia and infection. Rare but potentially serious events that occurred on the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II included ventricular arrhythmias (including tachycardia, see Grouped Term in Table 44 above, and extrasystoles; N = 5, 1.852%), erythema multiforme (N = 8, 2.963%), palmar-plantar erythrodysesthesia syndrome (N = 5, 1.852%), and cardiac failure (N = 8, 2.963%).

L1951: **Reviewer's Comment:** The FDA notes that the Grouped Term of musculoskeletal pain was not considered to be a related AE by the Applicant. However, the timing of this GT indicates that it is an adverse reaction following treosulfan conditioning. We note higher rates of infectious complications in patients following treosulfan-based preparative regimens compared to busulfan-based preparative regimens. We also note a higher rate of fatal ARs through Day +30 after HSCT in patients who received treosulfan-based preparative regimens.
L1952: ## Laboratory Findings
L1953: As treosulfan is used as part of a conditioning regimen for allogeneic HSCT, cytopenias occur in nearly 100% of patients. Other laboratory abnormalities are listed below (Table 52). There were no significant differences in the maximum grades of laboratory abnormalities between the treosulfan and busulfan arms of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II.

L1959: ## Vital Signs
L1960: Vital signs were provided for the following timepoints: within 3 weeks of HSCT, day 0, day 28, day +100, month 6, month 9, month 12, month 15, month 18, and month 24. There were no vital signs submitted from the time periods around treosulfan or busulfan infusions to assess for evidence of infusion reactions. There were not sufficient vital signs collected to assess for outliers within the 30 days following HSCT.
L1961: ## QT/Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
L1962: Formal QT/QTc testing has not been completed. Under “Investigations,” “electrocardiogram abnormality” was identified in one patient on the busulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II and zero patients on the treosulfan arm. Ventricular arrhythmias were identified as rare but potentially serious ARs and are discussed in the TEAE and AR section above.

L1971: ## 8.3.5 Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
L1972: ## Treosulfan Dose (14 g/m2)
L1973: Trial MC-FludT.14/L Trial I used a treosulfan dose of 14 g/m2 for patients randomized to receive treosulfan. This study was stopped early due to increased transplant-related morbidity and mortality observed in the treosulfan arm. Treatment-emergent adverse events through HSCT day +30 are shown in Table 54. There were increased TEAEs of hepatotoxicity, stomatitis, musculoskeletal pain, infection, and febrile neutropenia in the treosulfan cohort, including grades 3-5 TEAEs.

L1980: ## Graft versus Host Disease
L1981: MC-FludT-14.L Trial II was further analyzed for differences between occurrence of acute (aGVHD) and chronic (cGVHD) graft versus host disease between the treosulfan and busulfan arms (Table 55). For analysis of both aGVHD and cGVHD, there was no censoring of the population for death or other events. The rate of aGVHD is defined as the proportion of the safety population diagnosed with aGVHD by HSCT Day +100. There was no risk difference of &gt;10% for any grade of aGVHD. The rate of cGVHD is defined as the proportion of the safety population diagnosed with cGVHD through month +24. There was no risk difference of ≥ 10 for either limited or extensive cGVHD between the treosulfan and busulfan arms.

L1989: ## Drug-Demographic Interactions
L1990: ## TEAEs by Sex
L1991: TEAEs with an RD of &gt;5% between female and male patients are shown in Table 56. Women were more likely to experience GI-related toxicities, including nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, and constipation than men in Trial MC-FludT.14/L Trial II. Men receiving treosulfan were more likely to have hepatotoxicity and renal impairment. Vomiting was significantly different between women and men (p = 0.00082). Vomiting was not more likely in women receiving treosulfan (33%) compared to women receiving busulfan (28%, RD 4.5).

L2002: ## TEAEs by Race
L2003: All studies were performed in Europe. No data on race or ethnicity were collected as part of any study. Therefore, no comparisons can be made.
L2004: ## Drug-Disease Interactions
L2005: Treosulfan was used as part of the conditioning regimen for patients with the diagnoses of AML and MDS. As these are separate diseases, TEAEs were assessed in the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II in the AML and MDS populations separately (Table 58). No TEAEs were more common in patients with AML. Patients with an underlying diagnosis of MDS were statistically (p&lt;0.05) more likely to have TEAEs of hemorrhage, hyperglycemia, cough, edema, hyponatremia, and encephalopathy.

L2011: ## Drug-Donor Interactions
L2012: Treosulfan was used as part of the conditioning regimen for patients undergoing both matched related donor and matched unrelated donor allogeneic HSCT. TEAEs were assessed in the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II in the related and unrelated donor populations (Table 59). There were more TEAEs observed in patients with matched unrelated donors, though this may be due to the difference in patient numbers between these two groups. When evaluating Grade 3-5 TEAEs, infection was more common in patients with a matched related donor (N = 11, 17%) than in those receiving a matched unrelated donor (N = 24, 12%).

L2032: ## 8.3.8 Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
L2033: ## Dose Dependency for Adverse Events
L2034: As MC-FludT.14/L Trial I, in which patients randomized to treosulfan received a dose of 14 g/m2, was stopped early because of increased transplant-related morbidity and mortality, a comparison was performed between all adult patients who received 10 g/m2 and those who received 14 g/m2 of treosulfan. We observed higher rates of hepatotoxicity, GI toxicity, infection (including febrile neutropenia), and musculoskeletal AEs in patients receiving the 14 g/m2 dose of treosulfan, as seen in Table 61.

L2041: **Reviewer's Comment:** The FDA notes that there is notably more toxicity, including infectious complications, GI toxicity, and hepatotoxicity, in patients who received 14 g/m2/dose of treosulfan compared to 10 g/m2/dose of treosulfan. In addition, the higher rates of early fatal adverse reactions in MC-FludT.14/L Trial I underscore the increased risk of morbidity and mortality with the higher dose of treosulfan.
L2042: ## Time Dependency for Adverse Events
L2043: There was insufficient follow-up with detailed safety results to allow an assessment of late toxicities.
L2044: ## 8.3.9 Additional Safety Explorations
L2045: ## Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L2046: An analysis of the SOC "Neoplasms benign, malignant, and unspecified (including cysts and polyps)" in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II revealed one patient in the treosulfan arm and one patient in the busulfan arm with these events.
L2047: ## Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L2048: Because of the proposed mechanism of action of treosulfan, pregnancy and reproduction are discouraged. There have been no studies or literature reports of subjects who became pregnant while receiving treosulfan, or of children who were born of parents who were receiving treosulfan.
L2049: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L2050: NDA 214759
L2051: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L2052: ## Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L2053: Two pediatric studies were submitted as part of this application. All pediatric patients received doses of treosulfan that were equivalent to 14 g/m2 dosing. Safety analysis was performed comparing the pediatric patients to the recommended dose of treosulfan (10 g/m2). The majority of pediatric patients received thiotepe in addition to fludarabine and treosulfan for their conditioning. Analyses comparing pediatric and adult studies (Cohorts 4 and 1) excluded patients who received thiotepe.
L2054: An analysis between the treosulfan arm and the busulfan arm in the Phase 2 randomized study MC-FludT.16/NM is seen in Table 62. Nearly all of the patients in this analysis received a preparative regimen of either treosulfan or busulfan, fludarabine, and thiotepe. The majority of TEAEs were similar between the two arms. Notable exceptions included higher rates of abdominal pain, rash, pruritis, hemorrhage, and infusion-related reactions in the treosulfan arm of this study.

L2073: ## 8.3.10 Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L2074: ## Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L2075: Empirica Signal was used to evaluate the postmarket experience of treosulfan (Figure 15). The
L2076: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L2077: NDA 214759
L2078: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L2079: fifty highest PTs postmarket reports were further analyzed. Forty-eight were known complications of allo-HSCT and deemed TRM. A case series of retinitis was further investigated; all reported cases of retinitis (optic neuritis) were due to infectious causes and are also deemed TRM. Additionally, case reports of respiratory failure were also further investigated. Of the 28 case reports, three were adjudicated to be within 30 days of HSCT and an AR due to the conditioning regimen. The other cases were due to infection or complications from veno-occlusive disease.
L2080: Drug=TREOSULFAN (Subset: Thru_2021)
L2081: Figure 15. Heat Map of Postmarketing Safety Signals by SOC
L2082: Source: Reviewer's Analysis, Empirica Signal
L2083: ## Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L2084: Safety in the postmarket setting is anticipated to be similar to that observed in the pivotal trial submitted and reviewed in this application. It is recognized that treosulfan may be used in populations other than those included in the indication statement. Additionally, patients with lower performance status, higher HCT-CI score, and inadequate organ function may receive treosulfan outside of a clinical trial. For these reasons, the predictability of treosulfan in the postmarket setting is not completely predictable.
L2085: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L2086: NDA 214759
L2087: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L2088: ## 8.3.11 Integrated Assessment of Safety
L2089: The primary data in support of safety for the proposed indication of treosulfan came from study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, in which 270 patients with either AML or MDS were exposed to treosulfan as part of a conditioning regimen prior to allogeneic HSCT. All patients received two doses of treosulfan on days -4 and -3 prior to HSCT, at a dose of 10 g/m2/dose.
L2090: The study population was monitored for death, adverse events, serious adverse events, and laboratory abnormalities (Table 65). By Day +30 following HSCT, there were 4 deaths (1.5%) on the treosulfan arm, all due to fatal pulmonary adverse reactions, as compared to 2 deaths (0.7%) on the busulfan (control) arm.
L2091: Adverse events with treosulfan were mainly similar to those observed with busulfan-based conditioning. Common TEAEs (\ge 20\%) included musculoskeletal pain, stomatitis, pyrexia, nausea, edema, infection, and vomiting. Of those, musculoskeletal pain (12% higher), edema (11% higher), and infection (5% higher) occurred at a rate at least 5% higher in the treosulfan arm than the busulfan arm. The most common grade 3-5 AEs (\ge 5\%) in the treosulfan arm were febrile neutropenia, infection, hypertension, stomatitis, and musculoskeletal pain. Of those, infection occurred at a rate of &gt;5\% compared to the busulfan arm (6% higher in the treosulfan arm).
L2092: Safety data supporting the dose of treosulfan (10 g/m2/dose) were from studies MC-FludT.6/L, MC-FludT.7/AML, MC-FludT.8/MDS, and MC-FludT.14/L Trials I and II. In this comparison, 290 patients were dosed at 10 g/m2/dose, and 305 patients were dosed at 14 g/m2/dose.
L2093: ## Summary of Major Safety Events
L2094: **Table 65. Summary of Major Safety Events from MC-FludT.14/L Trial II through Day +30**

L2096: ## SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
L2097: ## 8.4 Statistical Issues
L2098: ## Pivotal Trial 14-II
L2099: Table 66 summarizes statistical issues for Study 14-II and our assessment for them. Because the primary endpoint EFS could not be independently verified by FDA, the review and analysis were focused on OS.
L2100: **Table 66. Summary of Statistical Issues of Pivotal Trial 14-II**
L2101: Issues	Sponsor	FDA	Outcome
Primary Endpoint	EFS	Cannot be verified and NI margin not justified	Prespecified primary endpoint and analysis cannot be used
Analysis Set	FAS	ITT	Results are consistent
Analysis Method for OS	Unstratified Log-rank test
Adjusted Cox Model	Stratified Log-rank test
Stratified Cox Model	When not adjusting for multiplicity and early stopping, OS results are supportive based on HR and 95% CI in the ITT population and the AML and MDS subpopulation
No alpha boundary was prespecified for OS	Exploratory analysis under hypothetical prespecifications	Mixed results were obtained in exploratory analysis using different methods for multiplicity
L2102: - **Analysis set:** ITT and FAS population are similar outcome
- **Interpretation of p-values for OS:** Since there was no pre-specified plan to control multiplicity, p-values are nominal and cannot be included in labeling. To facilitate decision making, we calculated OBF boundaries and Pocock boundaries using information fraction of EFS as an exploratory analysis. As stated in section 8.1.1, the OS result (nominal p-value=0.0049) did not cross the OBF boundary (\alpha=0.0025) but crossed the Pocock boundary (\alpha=0.014).
- **Trial conduct:** We investigated the informative censoring for OS, time from randomization to transplant and time from diagnosis to randomization between treatment arms. We did not find any trial conduct issue that may impact the data quality or potentially introduce bias.
L2103: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L2104: NDA 214759
L2105: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L2106: ## Trial 14-I
L2107: This trial discontinued after planned interim analysis. It was determined that dose should be lower in this elderly population because neutropenic phase was much longer than control.
L2108: This trial preceded Trial 14-II and used the same SAP, therefore all analyses reported by sponsor was based on the FAS population. We did subgroup analysis on OS to investigate any significantly larger detrimental effect on OS comparing to the overall effect. FDA used the ITT population in all analyses. No significantly larger detrimental effect was found in subgroups we analyzed.
L2109: ## 8.5 Conclusions and Recommendations
L2110: Study 14-II is the sole pivotal trial submitted to support efficacy. Because the primary endpoint EFS could not be independently verified by FDA for Study 14-II, no conclusions can be made based on the primary endpoint. Therefore, the statistical review and analysis were focused on OS for Study 14-II.
L2111: There was no prespecified multiplicity approach to test OS after EFS, but there would have been different conclusions regarding statistical significance for OS based on Pocock or O'Brien-Fleming boundaries. These mixed results suggest that it is not clear if OS would have been considered to be significant if there was a prespecified approach; however, no definitive conclusions can be made based on the OS results because a multiplicity approach was not prespecified. The hazard ratio was 0.62 with 95% CI (0.41, 0.92) in AML patients and 0.62 with 95% CI (0.34, 1.10) in MDS patients.
L2112: Regarding other statistical issues, the ITT and FAS population were similar and no trial conduct issues were found. From the statistical perspective, the effect of treosulfan as observed in the OS results appears to be supportive; however, interpreting this data is challenging because there was no prespecified plan to control multiplicity.
L2113: In the absence of verification of the primary endpoint, and without prespecification of Type-1 error for multiple comparisons, Study 14-II does not provide considerable evidence of effectiveness. There is insufficient evidence of effectiveness to outweigh any risks.

L2149: ## 16.4 OCP Appendices
L2150: ## 16.4.1 Pharmacometrics Review
L2151: A population pharmacokinetics (PPK) model was developed by the Applicant to characterize the PK of treosulfan in adults and pediatric patients received allogeneic transplant. Patients intrinsic and extrinsic factors which influence the PK and PK variability of treosulfan were identified. The exposure-response (E-R) relationships for efficacy and safety were explored using multivariate analyses. In this review, FDA Pharmacometrics Reviewer verified the Applicant's analyses, as well as conducted independent analyses to evaluate the Applicant's dose selection.
L2152: ## 1. Population PK Analyses
L2153: Originally, the Applicant developed PPK model based on seven historical studies (from mostly adults and only a limited number of children, **Table 67**) and 2 new pediatric studies (**Table 68**). However, due to the invalidated bioanalytical methods for the quantification of treosulfan, all PK data of the non-medac-sponsored studies were removed from the PPK analysis upon the FDA request. The updated dataset for PPK analysis included 1 historical medac sponsored trial (MC-FludT.14/L Trial I with 24 adult subjects) and 2 new pediatric medac trials (MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FLudT.17/M with 83 subjects). The summary of the continuous covariates for the subjects included in the updated final PPK model are shown in
L2154: **Table 69.** In addition, the dataset included 63 male and 44 female subjects.
L2155: **Table 67. Historical trials included in the final PPK analysis**

L2166: Source: Applicant population PK study report 2021
L2167: The final PPK model for treosulfan is a two-compartment model with linear elimination and intercompartmental clearance (Figure 16). To account for the bioassay differences between historical (MC-FludT.14/L Trial I) and new data (MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M), a SHIFT model parameter was included to describe this difference (C_{p,new} = \text{SHIFT} \times C_p and C_{p,hist} = C_p where C_p represents the predicted concentration). BSA effect was included as a significant covariate on CL, V1, V2 and Q in the final model.
L2168: Figure 16. Model structure of the final PPK model of treosulfan
L2169: Figure 1: Two compartmental model describing disposition of Treosulfan. After IV administration into the central compartment V1, Treosulfan is distributed into the peripheral compartment V2 and eliminated. Both processes have linear kinetics determined by inter-compartmental clearance Q and clearance CL.
L2170: Source: Applicant population PK study report 2020

L2193: The Reviewer also evaluated the GOF for adults and pediatrics, respectively. As shown in Figure 19, in both adult and pediatric population, the updated PPK model adequately described the observed treosulfan concentrations.
L2194: **Figure 19. Goodness-of-fit plots stratified by population**
L2195: Source: Reviewer's analysis
L2196: Based on the GOF and VPC plots, the Applicant's updated final PPK analysis is acceptable for describing the PK of treosulfan in adult and pediatric patients. In the Applicant's PPK model development, multiple parameters could not be precisely estimated, and the effect of renal impairment on CL was removed. Thus, the Reviewer conducted independent analysis to evaluate the impact of renal function on treosulfan clearance and exposure in adults. As shown in Figure 20, a trend of lower BSA-adjusted CL and higher AUCinf (but not significant) in moderate renal impairment subjects (n=2) was observed compared to subjects with normal renal function (n=10). Mild renal impairment (n=12) appears to have no impact on CL and AUCinf of treosulfan. However, it is important to note that the number of subjects in the moderate renal impairment category was very limited. In addition, the lowest CrCL included in the analysis was 47 mL/min, which is still much higher than the lower cutoff of the moderate renal impairment (30 mL/min).

L2202: ## 2. Exposure-response (E-R) analysis for efficacy
L2203: ## 2.1 Adult patients
L2204: The Applicant evaluated the potential relationship between exposure and time to engraftment (defined as time from Day 0 to neutrophil engraftment (first of 3 consecutive days with ANC &gt;0.5 x109/L)) in adult patients based on linear regression model, Kaplan-Meier curves and multivariate analyses. The original analyses were based on both historical trials (SOLCA3 and SOLCA5 were excluded due to lack of information on baseline demographic and disease characteristics) and medac-sponsored trials with the dose range from 10 g/m2 single dose to 14 g/m2 multiple dose. No significant E-R relationship between AUC versus time to engraftment was identified. The updated E-R analyses based on updated PPK model only included 24 adult patients following the dosing regimen of 14 g/m2 in the historical medac-sponsored trial MC-FludT.14/L Trial I. No PK data were collected from Trial II at the recommended dosing regimen of 10 g/m2. Thus, with a limited number of subjects and narrow exposure range at a higher than recommended dosing regimen, the updated E-R analyses in adult patients are not considered informative.
L2205: ## 2.2 Pediatric patients
L2206: Upon FDA's request, the Applicant conducted E-R analyses for time to engraftment based on 2 pediatric trials using Cox proportional hazards models with AUC and Cmax estimated by the updated PPK analyses. No significant relationship between treosulfan exposure and time to engraftment was demonstrated. In addition, no evidence was observed with regard to a potential effect of baseline demographic and disease characteristic variables on time to engraftment. However, because some of the pediatric patients in the studies FludT.16/NM and MC-FLudT.17/M received thiopeta, the E-R analyses results should be interpreted with caution.
L2207: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L2208: NDA 214759
L2209: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L2210: ## 3. Exposure-response analyses for safety
L2211: Applicant original E-R analyses for safety were based on pooled data from adult and pediatric patients included in the original PPK analysis. After the update by removing the invalid PK data, only 24 adult patients in trial MC-FludT.14/L Trial I were retained, and all of them were dosed with 14 g/m2 treosulfan. In addition, the safety in adult patients were compared between MC-FludT.14/L Trial I (14 g/m2/day × 3 days of treosulfan on -6 to -4 day) and Trial II (10 g/m2/day × 3 days of treosulfan on -4 to -2 day). Compared to the proposed dosing regimen (10 g/m2/day × 3 days on -4 to -2), the higher dose regimen (14 g/m2/day × 3 days of treosulfan on -6 to -4 day) resulted in longer duration of neutropenia (17.5 vs. 14 days), higher infection rate (11.9% vs 8.1%) and higher infection-related death (16.1% vs 9.3%). Thus, given the available dose-response relationship for safety as well as the narrow exposure range within the 24 adult patients with valid PK data, no additional exposure-safety analyses in adults were conducted based on updated PK parameters. For details on dose-response relationship for safety, refer to sections 6.3.2 and 8.3.5 in the unireview.
L2212: For pediatric patients, the Applicant conducted multivariate E-R analyses for safety endpoints, including duration of neutropenia, infections, infection-related death and non-relapse mortality based on the updated PK parameters in patients (n=82) from trials MC-FludT.17/M and MC-FludT.16/NM, upon FDA's request. Both AUC and Cmax were assessed. The potential effects of age, [REDACTED], disease type, donor type and Karnofsky/Lansky performance score were investigated. Treosulfan dose in pediatric PK patients (10, 12, or 14 g/m2) was BSA-adapted according to trial protocols MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M. Again, because some of the pediatric patients in the studies FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M received thiotepe, the E-R analyses results should be interpreted with caution.
L2213: ## Infection-related mortality
L2214: Out of 82 children from both malignant and non-malignant diseases trials, only 1 pediatric patient (1.2%) with the event of infection-related death was observed. This patient had a significant increased AUC and Cmax. Because of the very limited number of subject (n=1), the statistically significant impact of AUC and Cmax are not considered meaningful.
L2215: ## Infection
L2216: Out of 82 pediatric patients, 33 (40.2%) subjects with infections with at least CTCAE grade III were observed, and they were equally distributed between malignant and non-malignant diseases. No significant effect of treosulfan AUC or Cmax, baseline demographic and disease characteristic variables could be detected by logistic regression models.
L2217: ## Gastrointestinal (GI) disorders
L2218: Out of 82 patients, 41 (50.0%) subjects with GI disorder with at least CTCAE grade III were observed. No significant effect of treosulfan AUC or Cmax was detected by logistic regression models. Only the age of patients had a significant effect on risk for GI disorder.
L2219: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L2220: NDA 214759
L2221: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L2222: ## Duration of neutropenia
L2223: In the 80 pediatric patients, no significant effect of treosulfan AUC or C_{max}, baseline demographic and disease characteristic variables could be detected by linear regression models. In addition, no evidence was observed with regard to a potential effect of baseline demographic and disease characteristic variables.
L2224: ## 4. PK Simulation to Support Pediatric dose selection
L2225: The Applicant proposed the following dose levels in pediatric patients to obtain comparable treosulfan AUC24h to an average adult after administration of treosulfan 10 g/m2 (refer to section 6.3.2):
L2226: - 9.0 g/m2 if BSA \le 0.7 m2 or in the age range 1 to 4 years
- 9.5 g/m2 if 0.7 m2 &lt; BSA \le 1.1 m2 or in the age range 5 to 9 years
- 10.0 g/m2 if BSA &gt; 1.1 m2 or in the age range 10 to 18 years

L2233: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L2234: NDA 214759
L2235: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L2236: To evaluate whether the Applicant proposed pediatric dosing regimen is reasonable, the Reviewer conducted independent simulation and compared the predicted AUC24h among different BSA groups and dosing regimens. Average BSA per age group was calculated by the Applicant from average population body weight and height surveyed in the United States. The simulation was conducted for 1000 replicates to generate a reasonable number of virtual subjects (n=28000) for each dosing regimen. The BSA distribution included for each simulation is shown in **Figure 21**.
L2237: Simulation was performed using the Applicant developed final PPK model. The simulations used 1000 replicates of the Applicant generated virtual population as discussed above with 28 subjects per replicate. The software used to perform the simulation was NONMEM 7.4. The simulation result shows that the Applicant proposed pediatric dosing regimen is expected to produce similar AUC24h compared to the median AUC24h in adult following 10 \text{ g/m}^2 dosing (target exposure). However, a simplified dosing regimen of 10 \text{ g/m}^2 for the entire pediatric population with BSA range of 0.25 to 2.25 \text{ m}^2 is also predicted to provide comparable AUC24h without clinically significant difference compared to the target exposure in adults. For pediatric patients with \text{BSA} \le 0.7 \text{ m}^2, AUC24h is expected to be 11.2% higher than the target exposure; for pediatric subjects with BSA within 0.7 to 1.1 \text{ m}^2, AUC24h is expected to be 4.7% higher than the target exposure; for pediatric subjects with \text{BSA} &gt; 1.1 \text{ m}^2, AUC24h is expected to be 2.8% lower than the target exposure in adults, as shown in **Figure 22**. Thus, for the convenience of dosing and to avoid potential dosing error, we recommend the 10 \text{ g/m}^2 dosing regimen for all pediatric patients with the age \ge 1 year.

L2244: ## 16.4.2 PBPK Review
L2245: ## Executive Summary
L2246: The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant's PBPK report, entitled "Treosulfan DDI" to predict the effects of treosulfan on the PK of 1) midazolam (a CYP3A4 substrate), omeprazole (a CYP2C19 substrate) and digoxin (a P-gp substrate).
L2247: The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report, supporting modeling files, and the Applicant's response to the FDA's information requests (IRs) submitted on December 21, 2020, March 3, 2021 and April 27, 2021 and concluded the followings.
L2248: - The treosulfan PBPK model is adequate to predict the treosulfan PK profiles following 2 hours infusion of 14 g/m2 for three consecutive days.
- There is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited an inhibitory effect on CYP enzymes and P-gp in in vitro incubation systems.
- The PBPK model and mechanistic static model were used to predict the magnitude of enzyme mediated DDI caused by treosulfan and its metabolite, EBDM, respectively. Results
L2249: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L2250: NDA 214759
L2251: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L2252: of analysis showed that the potential DDI of treosulfan as a CYP3A4 inhibitor and a CYP2C19 inhibitor cannot be excluded.
L2253: - The PBPK model was used to predict the magnitude of P-gp mediated DDI caused by treosulfan. Results of analysis showed that the potential DDI of treosulfan as a P-gp inhibitor cannot be excluded. This could be important for P-gp substrates with a narrow therapeutic index.
L2254: ## Applicant's PBPK Modeling Effort
L2255: ## PBPK software
L2256: PK-Sim V8.0 (Open Systems Pharmacology) was used by the Applicant to develop the PBPK models and for DDI predictions. The reviewer used the PK-Sim V 9.0 for analyses.
L2257: ## Model development
L2258: ## Treosulfan
L2259: The treosulfan PBPK model was developed for intravenous dosing in healthy subjects. The plasma protein binding of treosulfan is considered to be negligible. The population pharmacokinetic model estimated volume of distribution is about 40 L, indicating that treosulfan may distribute into both extracellular and intracellular fluid. The standard PK-Sim 3-compartment model (intracellular, interstitial and vascular space) with a single permeation barrier between the interstitial and intracellular space in each organ was used to characterize the distribution of treosulfan.
L2260: Treosulfan is a water-soluble prodrug that is converted non-enzymatically at pH 7.4 and 37 °C to two active metabolites (EBDM and DEB) along with the release of methanesulfonic acid. Treosulfan's nonenzymatic activation is pH- and temperature- dependent and is stopped at pH &lt;5.02. This activation process occurs in both blood and tissue compartments. Treosulfan is excreted in the kidney via glomerular filtration, and the fraction of unchanged treosulfan excreted in urine is about 15-40%. The fraction excreted via glomerular filtration was estimated based on the observed accumulative amount of unchanged treosulfan excreted in the urine. The observed plasma PK of treosulfan following the intravenous infusion was used to estimate the systemic transformation rate of treosulfan.
L2261: In vitro studies indicated that treosulfan was a reversible inhibitor for CYP2C19 with an IC50 value of 972 µM. Treosulfan was a reversible inhibitor for CYP3A4 using midazolam as a substrate with an IC50 value of 1870 mM but did not show inhibitory effect on CYP3A4 when using testosterone as a substrate. In vitro transport studies showed that treosulfan inhibited P-gp-mediated transport of loperamide with an IC50 value of ~3000 mM.

L2277: ## Esomeprazole and Romeprazole
L2278: Omeprazole is a proton pump inhibitor. It is administered as a racemic mixture of its two enantiomers, esomeprazole and R-omeprazole. Omeprazole is metabolized mainly by CYP2C19 and CYP3A4. Both enantiomers are competitive and time-dependent inhibitors of CYP2C19. The omeprazole PBPK models were used to predict the DDI potential of treosulfan as a CYP2C19 inhibitor. The Applicant's esomeprazole and R-omeprazole PBPK models were based on Kanacher et al.7. The DDI between omeprazole and CYP2C19 inhibitors was validated with 2 clinical studies. Representative model validation and DDI evaluation results were shown in Figure 23.
L2279: ## Digoxin
L2280: Digoxin is a P-gp substrate. The digoxin PBPK model was used to predict the DDI potential of treosulfan as a P-gp inhibitor. The Applicant's digoxin PBPK model was based on Hanke et al.8. The DDI between digoxin and P-gp inhibitors was validated with 12 clinical studies. Representative model validation and DDI evaluation results were shown in Figure 23.
L2281: ## FDA's assessment
L2282: The Applicant conducted in vitro studies to evaluate the inhibitory effect of treosulfan toward CYPs and transporters and the in-vitro K_i values for CYP3A4, CYP2C19 and P-gp were incorporated into the PBPK model to evaluate the DDI potential of treosulfan as a perpetrator with CYP3A4, CYP2C19 and P-gp substrates.

L2292: ## Applicant's response to FDA's IR
L2293: (b) (4)
L2294: ## FDA's assessment
L2295: ## 1. CYP inhibition study
L2296: The in vitro treosulfan buffer stability study indicated that both treosulfan and EBDM were present in the in vitro incubation system, even at the beginning of the incubation. The concentration of EBDM at time 0 was 2121 mM in 10,000 mM treosulfan solution in phosphate buffer in the in vitro buffer stability study, where the samples were prepared and analyzed directly. In the in vitro CYP inhibition study, a time interval would be expected
L2297: ## NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
L2298: NDA 214759
L2299: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L2300: between the solution preparation and initiation of pre-incubation, thus the concentration of EBDM at time 0 may be higher than 2121 mM in 10,000 mM treosulfan solution in phosphate buffer. In addition, the EBDM concentrations in the phosphate buffer appeared to reach a plateau at around 2 hours post-incubation. Therefore, the fact that observed inhibitory effect was not altered after pre-incubation for 30 min would not be adequate to justify that the inhibitory effect was not caused by EBDM. There is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on CYP enzymes in vitro. For further discussion regarding the DDI potential of treosulfan as a CYP inhibitor, please refer to the result section.
L2301: ## 2. P-gp inhibition study
L2302: Both treosulfan and its two metabolites, EBDM and DEB were present in the in vitro transport system after 60 min incubation. In addition, P-gp preferentially transports more lipophilic substrates. Given the ranked lipophilicity is DEB&gt;EBDM&gt;treosulfan, there is also an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on P-gp in in vitro system. For further discussion regarding the DDI potential of treosulfan as a P-gp inhibitor, please refer to the result section.
L2303: ## PBPK model application
L2304: The developed PBPK model was used to simulate the DDI for treosulfan in the following scenarios.
L2305: - To predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of midazolam (a CYP3A4 substrate) following multiple intravenous infusion of treosulfan (14 g/m2, QD for 3 days) and a single oral dose administration of midazolam (2 mg) on day 2 in healthy subjects.
- To predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of omeprazole (a CYP2C19 substrate) following multiple intravenous infusion of treosulfan (14 g/m2, QD for 3 days) and multiple oral dose administration of omeprazole (10 mg, QD for 3 days) in healthy subjects.
- To predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of digoxin (a P-gp substrate) following multiple intravenous infusion of treosulfan (14 g/m2, QD for 3 days) and multiple oral dose administration of digoxin (1 mg, QD for 3 days) in healthy subjects.
L2306: ## Results
L2307: ## 1. Can treosulfan PBPK model describe treosulfan PK in healthy subjects?
L2308: **Yes.** The treosulfan model was able to capture the observed treosulfan PK profiles and accumulative amount of unchanged treosulfan in the urine following 2 hr intravenous infusion of 14 g/m2 on three consecutive days (Figure 24 and Table 72).

L2318: ## **2. Can treosulfan PBPK model predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of midazolam (a CYP3A4 substrate)?**
L2319: As aforementioned, there is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on CYP3A4 in in vitro incubation system. Two potential scenarios are discussed in more detail as follows.
L2320: ## **Scenario 1: Treosulfan is a more potent inhibitor of CYP3A4.**
L2321: The Applicant's treosulfan model was used to predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of midazolam after incorporating the in vitro obtained K_i value of 1213 mM (calculated based on in vitro measured IC_{50} value of 1870 mM) against CYP3A4. About 2-fold increase in midazolam exposure was predicted when coadministered with treosulfan. The effect of a time difference in dosing of midazolam at 1 and 2 hours before treosulfan treatment or 0, 1, 2, 3 and 4 hours after treosulfan treatment was also evaluated. Applicant's analysis showed that an about 2-fold increase in midazolam exposure would be expected if midazolam is given within 2 hours after the treosulfan administration, otherwise, the midazolam exposure change would be expected to be less than 1.5-fold. The reviewer further conducted a sensitivity analysis to assess the effect of CYP3A4 K_i values on the predicted midazolam exposure. The model predicted a 6-fold increase in midazolam exposure when a 10-fold lower K_i value was applied.
L2322: ## **Scenario 2: The metabolite, EBDM, is a more potent inhibitor of CYP3A4.**
L2323: The Applicant's treosulfan model did not include the metabolite, EBDM. Thus, the inhibition potential of EBDM on a CYP3A substrate (such as midazolam) cannot be evaluated using a PBPK modeling approach. Using a mechanistic static model described in the FDA guidance on in vitro drug interaction studies9, the reviewer's analysis showed that midazolam AUC ratios were less than 1.2 and about 3 when the K_i value of EBDM on CYP3A pathway was set as the same value as the in vitro K_i value and a tenth of in vitro value, respectively. The assumptions for these calculations are 1) f_{mCYP3A4} for midazolam was assumed to be 1.0; 2) the EBDM K_i value against CYP3A4 was assumed to be the same as the EBDM concentration that was formed from treosulfan at a concentration of 1870 \muM (IC_{50}) in the phosphate buffer at time 0 in the in vitro buffer stability study.
L2324: In conclusion, there is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on CYP3A4 in in vitro system. The potential DDI of treosulfan as a CYP3A4 inhibitor cannot be excluded.
L2325: 9 https://www.fda.gov/media/134582/download

L2329: ## 3. Can treosulfan PBPK model predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of omeprazole (a CYP2C19 substrate)?
L2330: As aforementioned, there is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on CYP2C19 in in vitro incubation system. Two potential scenarios are discussed in more detail as follows.
L2331: ## Scenario 1: Treosulfan is a more potent inhibitor of CYP2C19.
L2332: The Applicant's treosulfan model was used to predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of omeprazole after incorporating the in vitro derived K_i value of 778 mM (calculated based on in vitro measured IC_{50} value of 972 mM) against CYP2C19. About 1.6-fold increase in omeprazole exposure was predicted when coadministered with treosulfan. The effect of a time difference in dosing of omeprazole at 1 and 2 hours before treosulfan treatment or 0, 1, 2, 3 and 4 hours after treosulfan treatment was also evaluated. The simulated results showed that an about 1.6-fold increase in omeprazole exposure would be expected if omeprazole is given within 1 hour before or after the treosulfan administration, otherwise, the omeprazole exposure change would be expected to be less than 1.3-fold. The reviewer further conducted a sensitivity analysis to assess the effect of CYP2C19 K_i values on the predicted omeprazole exposure. The simulated omeprazole AUC ratios were about 3.0 and 4.0 using a 10-fold lower K_i value for both CYP2C19 and CYP3A4 and a 40-fold10 lower CYP2C19 and a 10-fold lower CYP3A4 K_i value than the in vitro determined, respectively, when coadministered with treosulfan.
L2333: ## Scenario 2: The metabolite, EBDM, is a more potent inhibitor of CYP2C19.
L2334: The Applicant's treosulfan model did not include the metabolite, EBDM. Thus, the inhibition potential of EBDM on a CYP2C19 substrate (such as omeprazole) cannot be evaluated using a PBPK modeling approach. Using a mechanistic static model11, the reviewer's analysis showed that omeprazole AUC ratios were about 1.3 and 3 when the K_i value of EBDM on CYP2C19 pathway was set as the same value as the in vitro value and a tenth of in vitro value, respectively. The assumptions for these calculations are 1) fmCYP2C19 for omeprazole was assumed to be 0.8612; 2) the EBDM in vitro K_i value against CYP2C19 was assumed to be the same as the EBDM concentration that was formed from treosulfan at a concentration of 972 mM (IC_{50}) in the phosphate buffer at time 0 in the in vitro buffer stability study.
L2335: 10 Caiping Yao, Kent L Kunze, William F Trager, Evan D Kharasch, René H Levy. Comparison of in vitro and in vivo inhibition potencies of fluvoxamine toward CYP2C19. Drug Metab Dispos. 2003 May;31(5):565-71.
L2336: 11 https://www.fda.gov/media/134582/download
L2337: 12 Koji Chiba, Keiko Shimizu, Motohiro Kato, Takaaki Nishibayashi, Kazuki Terada, Nobuo Izumo, Yuichi Sugiyama. Prediction of inter-individual variability in the pharmacokinetics of CYP2C19 substrates in humans. Drug Metab Pharmacokinet. 2014;29(5):379-86.

L2340: Grafapex (treosulfan)
L2341: In conclusion, there is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on CYP2C19 in in vitro system. The potential DDI of treosulfan as a CYP2C19 inhibitor cannot be excluded.
L2342: ## 4. Can treosulfan PBPK model predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of digoxin (a P-gp substrate)?
L2343: As aforementioned, there is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on P-gp in in vitro incubation system. Two potential scenarios are discussed in more detail as follows.
L2344: ## Scenario 1: Treosulfan is a more potent inhibitor of P-gp.
L2345: The Applicant's treosulfan model was used to predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of digoxin after incorporating the in vitro obtained K_i value of 1774 mM (calculated based on in vitro measured IC_{50} value of 3000 mM) against P-gp. About 1.1-fold increase in digoxin exposure was predicted when coadministered with treosulfan. The reviewer further conducted a sensitivity analysis to assess the effect of P-gp K_i values on the predicted digoxin exposure. The simulated digoxin AUC ratios were about 1.35 and 1.56 using a 20-fold and 94-fold lower P-gp K_i values13 than the in vitro determined, respectively, when coadministered with treosulfan.
L2346: ## Scenario 2: The metabolite, EBDM, is a more potent inhibitor of P-gp.
L2347: The Applicant's treosulfan model did not include the metabolite, EBDM. Thus, the inhibition potential of EBDM on a P-gp substrate cannot be evaluated using a PBPK modeling approach. The P-gp mediated interaction by EBDM would be expected to be lower than that caused by treosulfan, given the ratio of EBDM to treosulfan concentration in the in vitro systems (\sim 0.2) was significantly higher than those observed in vivo (\sim 0.01 in study MC-FludT._{16}/NM and MC-FludT._{17}/M).
L2348: In conclusion, there is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on P-gp in in vitro system. The potential DDI of treosulfan as a P-gp inhibitor cannot be excluded. This could be important for P-gp substrates with a narrow therapeutic index.
L2349: 13 https://www.certara.com/app/uploads/2017/06/Howgate_2017_PAGE_P-glycoprotein.pdf

The left graph, titled "Myeloid Chimerism," plots H2-D^+ CD11b^+ (%) on the Y-axis against Weeks - Post BMT (0 to 16) on the X-axis. The right graph, titled "T-Lymphoid Chimerism," plots H2-D^+ CD3^+ (%) on the Y-axis against Weeks - Post BMT (0 to 16) on the X-axis.
Legend details:
